8.11
Inverse of sample covariance matrix and partial correlation coefficient

This is the sample version of Problem
29.7
.

Based on
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
, we can compute the sample covariance matrix
\hat{\Sigma}
. Denote its inverse by
\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}=(\hat{\sigma}^{jk})_{1\leq j,k\leq p}
. Show that for any pair
j\neq k
, we have

\hat{\sigma}^{jk}=0\Longleftrightarrow\hat{\rho}_{x_{j}x_{k}\mid x_{\backslash(j,k)}}=0

where
\hat{\rho}_{x_{j}x_{k}\mid x_{\backslash(j,k)}}
is the partial correlation coefficient of
X_{j}
and
X_{k}
given all other variables.

Chapter 9
Cochran’s Formula and Omitted-Variable Bias

36
Cochran’s formula

Consider an
n\times 1
vector
Y
, an
n\times k
matrix
X_{1}
, and an
n\times l
matrix
X_{2}
. Similar to the FWL theorem, we do not impose any statistical models.
We can fit the following OLS:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{1}\hat{\beta}_{1}+X_{2}\hat{\beta}_{2}+\hat{\varepsilon},

(56)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{2}\tilde{\beta}_{2}+\tilde{\varepsilon},

(57)

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{2}\hat{\delta}+\hat{U},

(58)

where
\hat{\varepsilon},\tilde{\varepsilon}
are the residual vectors, and
\hat{U}
is the residual matrix.
The last OLS fit means the OLS fit of each column of
X_{1}
on
X_{2}
, and therefore the corresponding residual
\hat{U}
is an
n\times k
matrix.

Theorem 9.1

Under the OLS fits (
56
)–(
58
), we have

\tilde{\beta}_{2}=\hat{\beta}_{2}+\hat{\delta}\hat{\beta}_{1}.

This is a pure linear algebra fact similar to the FWL theorem. It is called
Cochran’s formula
in statistics. Sir David Cox
(
Cox, 2007
)
attributed the formula to
Cochran, 1938
although Cochran himself attributed the formula to
Fisher, 1925a
.

Cochran’s formula may seem familiar to readers knowing the chain rule in calculus. In a deterministic world with scalar
y,x_{1},x_{2}
, if

y(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}\beta_{1}+x_{2}\beta_{2}

and

x_{1}(x_{2})=x_{2}\delta,

then

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}y}{\text{d}x_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial y}{\partial x_{1}}\frac{\partial x_{1}}{\partial x_{2}}+\frac{\partial y}{\partial x_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}.

But the OLS decompositions above do not establish any deterministic relationships among
Y
and
(X_{1},X_{2})
.

In some sense, the formula is obvious. From the first and the third OLS fits, we have

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=X_{1}\hat{\beta}_{1}+X_{2}\hat{\beta}_{2}+\hat{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\left(X_{2}\hat{\delta}+\hat{U}\right)\hat{\beta}_{1}+X_{2}\hat{\beta}_{2}+\hat{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=X_{2}\hat{\delta}\hat{\beta}_{1}+\hat{U}\hat{\beta}_{1}+X_{2}\hat{\beta}_{2}+\hat{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=X_{2}\left(\hat{\delta}\hat{\beta}_{1}+\hat{\beta}_{2}\right)+\left(\hat{U}\hat{\beta}_{1}+\hat{\varepsilon}\right).

(59)

This suggests that
\tilde{\beta}_{2}=\hat{\beta}_{2}+\hat{\delta}\hat{\beta}_{1}
. The above derivation follows from simple algebraic manipulations and does not use any properties of the OLS. To prove Theorem
9.1
, we need to verify that the last line is indeed the OLS fit of
Y
on
X_{2}.
The proof is fact very simple.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
:

Based on the above discussion,
we only need to show that (
59
) is the OLS fit of
Y

on
X_{2}
, which is equivalent to show that
\hat{U}\hat{\beta}_{1}+\hat{\varepsilon}

is orthogonal to all columns of
X_{2}.
This follows from

X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\left(\hat{U}\hat{\beta}_{1}+\hat{\varepsilon}\right)=X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{U}\hat{\beta}_{1}+X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\varepsilon}=0,

because
X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{U}=0
based on the OLS fit in (
58
) and
X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\varepsilon}=0

based on the OLS fit in (
56
).

\square

Figure
7
illustrates Theorem
9.1
. Intuitively,
\tilde{\beta}_{2}
measures the total impact of
X_{2}
on
Y
, which has two channels:
\hat{\beta}_{2}
measures the impact acting directly and
\hat{\delta}\hat{\beta}_{1}
measures the impact acting indirectly through
X_{1}
.

Figure 7:
A diagram for Cochran’s formula

Figure
7
shows the interplay among three variables. Theoretically, we can discuss a system of more than three variables which is called the
path model
. This more advanced topic is beyond the scope of this book.
Wright, 1921
;
Wright, 1934
’s initial discussion of this approach was motivated by genetic studies. See
Freedman, 2009
for a textbook introduction.

37
Omitted-variable bias

The proof of Theorem
9.1
is very simple. However, it is one of the most insightful formulas in statistics. Econometricians often call it the
omitted-variable bias
formula because it quantifies the bias of the OLS coefficient of
X_{2}
in the short regression omitting possibly important variables in
X_{1}
. If the OLS coefficient from the long regression is unbiased then the the OLS coefficient from the short regression has a biased term

\hat{\delta}\hat{\beta}_{1},

which equals the product of the coefficient of
X_{2}
in the OLS fit of
X_{1}
on
X_{2}
and the coefficient of
X_{1}
in the long regression.

Below I will discuss a canonical example of using OLS to estimate the treatment effect in observational studies. For unit
i\ (i=1,\ldots,n)
, let
y_{i}
be the outcome,
z_{i}
be the binary treatment indicator (
1
for the treatment group and
0
for the control group) and
x_{i}
be the observed covariate vector. Practitioners often fit the following OLS:

y_{i}=\tilde{\beta}_{0}+\tilde{\beta}_{1}z_{i}+\tilde{\beta}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}_{2}x_{i}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}

and interpret
\tilde{\beta}_{1}
as the treatment effect estimate. However, observational studies may suffer from unmeasured confounding, that is, the treatment and control units differ in unobserved but important ways. In the simplest case, the above OLS may have omitted a variable
u_{i}
for each unit
i
, which is called a
confounder
. The oracle OLS is

y_{i}=\hat{\beta}_{0}+\hat{\beta}_{1}z_{i}+\hat{\beta}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}_{2}x_{i}+\hat{\beta}_{3}u_{i}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}

and the coefficient
\hat{\beta}_{1}
is an unbiased estimator if the model with
u_{i}
is correct.
With
X_{1}
containing the values of the
u_{i}
’s and
X_{2}
containing the values of the
(1,z_{i},x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}})
’s, Cochran’s formula implies that

\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\beta}_{0}\\
\tilde{\beta}_{1}\\
\tilde{\beta}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{0}\\
\hat{\beta}_{1}\\
\hat{\beta}_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\hat{\beta}_{3}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\delta}_{0}\\
\hat{\delta}_{1}\\
\hat{\delta}_{2}\end{pmatrix}

where
(\hat{\delta}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{1},\hat{\delta}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
is the coefficient vector in the OLS fit of
u_{i}
on
(1,z_{i},x_{i})
. Therefore, we can
quantify the difference between the observed estimate
\tilde{\beta}_{1}
and oracle estimate
\hat{\beta}_{1}
:

\tilde{\beta}_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{1}=\hat{\beta}_{3}\hat{\delta}_{1},

which is sometimes called the
confounding bias
.

Using the basic properties of OLS, we can show that
\hat{\delta}_{1}
equals the difference in means of
e_{i}=u_{i}-\hat{\delta}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{i}
across the treatment and control groups:

\hat{\delta}_{1}=\bar{e}_{1}-\bar{e}_{0},

where the bar and subscript jointly denote the sample mean of a particular variable within a treatment group.
So

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}-\hat{\beta}_{1}=\hat{\beta}_{3}(\bar{e}_{1}-\bar{e}_{0}).

(60)

Moreover, we can obtain a more explicit formula for
\hat{\delta}_{1}
:

\hat{\delta}_{1}=\bar{u}_{1}-\bar{u}_{0}-\hat{\delta}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{0}).

So

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}-\hat{\beta}_{1}=\hat{\beta}_{3}(\bar{u}_{1}-\bar{u}_{0})-\hat{\beta}_{3}\hat{\delta}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{0}).

(61)

Both (
60
) and (
61
) give some insights into the bias due to omitting an important covariate
u
. It is clear that the bias depends on
\hat{\beta}_{3}
, which quantifies the relationship between
u
and
y
. The formula (
60
) shows that the bias also depends on the imbalance in means of
u
across the treatment and control groups, after adjusting for the observed covariates
x
, that is, the imbalance in means of the residual confounding. The formula (
61
) shows a more explicit formula of the bias. The above discussion is often called
bias analysis
in epidemiology or
sensitivity analysis
in statistics and econometrics.

38
Homework problems

9.1
Baron–Kenny method for mediation analysis

The Baron–Kenny method, popularized by
Baron and Kenny, 1986
, is one of the most cited methods in social science.
It concerns the interplay among three variables
z,m,y
controlling for some other variables
x
. Let
Z,M,Y
be
n\times 1
vectors representing the observed values of
z,m,y
, and let
X
be the
n\times p
matrix representing the observations of
x
. The question of interest is to assess the “direct” and “indirect” effects of
z
on
y
, acting independently and through
m
, respectively. We do not need to define these notions precisely since we are only interested in the algebraic property below.

The Baron–Kenny method runs the OLS

Y=\hat{\beta}_{0}1_{n}+\hat{\beta}_{1}Z+\hat{\beta}_{2}M+X\hat{\beta}_{3}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{Y}

and interprets
\hat{\beta}_{1}
as the estimator of the “direct effect” of
z
on
y
.
The “indirect effect” of
z
on
y
through
m
has two estimators. First, based on the OLS

Y=\tilde{\beta}_{0}1_{n}+\tilde{\beta}_{1}Z+X\tilde{\beta}_{3}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{Y},

define the
difference estimator
as

\tilde{\beta}_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{1}.

Second, based on the OLS

M=\hat{\gamma}_{0}1_{n}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}Z+X\hat{\gamma}_{2}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{M},

define the
product estimator
as

\hat{\gamma}_{1}\hat{\beta}_{2}.

Figure
8
illustrates the OLS fits used in defining the estimators.

Figure 8:
The graph for the Baron–Kenny method

Prove that

\tilde{\beta}_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{1}=\hat{\gamma}_{1}\hat{\beta}_{2}

that is, the difference estimator and product estimator are numerically identical.

9.2
A special case of path analysis

Figure
9
represents the order of the variables
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Run the following OLS:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{0}1_{n}+\hat{\beta}_{1}X_{1}+\hat{\beta}_{2}X_{2}+\hat{\beta}_{3}X_{3}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{Y},

\displaystyle X_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}_{0}1_{n}+\hat{\delta}_{1}X_{1}+\hat{\delta}_{2}X_{2}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{3},

\displaystyle X_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{0}1_{n}+\hat{\theta}_{1}X_{1}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{2},

and

Y=\tilde{\beta}_{0}1_{n}+\tilde{\beta}_{1}X_{1}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{Y}.

Show that

\tilde{\beta}_{1}=\hat{\beta}_{1}+\hat{\beta}_{2}\hat{\theta}_{1}+\hat{\beta}_{3}\hat{\delta}_{1}+\hat{\beta}_{3}\hat{\delta}_{2}\hat{\theta}_{1}.

Remark: The OLS coefficient of
X_{1}
in the short regression of
Y
on
(1_{n},X_{1})
equals the summation of all the path coefficients from
X_{1}
to
Y
as illustrated by Figure
9
. This problem is a special case of the path model, but the conclusion holds in general.

Figure 9:
A path model

9.3
EHW in long and short regressions

Theorem
9.1
gives Cochran’s formula related to the coefficients from three OLS fits.
This problem concerns the covariance estimation.
There are at least two ways to estimate the covariance of
\tilde{\beta}_{2}
in the short regression (
57
). First, from the second OLS fit, the EHW covariance estimator is

\tilde{V}_{2}=(X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{2})^{-1}X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\text{diag}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2})X_{2}(X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{2})^{-1}.

Second, Cochran’s formula implies that

\tilde{\beta}_{2}=(\hat{\delta},I_{l})\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1}\\
\hat{\beta}_{2}\end{pmatrix}

is a linear transformation of the coefficient from the long regression, which further justifies the EHW covariance estimator

\tilde{V}_{2}^{\prime}=(\hat{\delta},I_{l})(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\text{diag}(\hat{\varepsilon}^{2})X(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\delta}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
I_{l}\end{pmatrix}.

Show that

\tilde{V}_{2}^{\prime}=(X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{2})^{-1}X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\text{diag}(\hat{\varepsilon}^{2})X_{2}(X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{2})^{-1}.

Hint: Use the result in Problem
7.1
.

Remark: Based on Theorem
7.2
, the EHW covariance estimator for
\hat{\beta}_{2}
is

\hat{V}_{2}=(\tilde{X}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\tilde{X}_{2})^{-1}\tilde{X}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\text{diag}(\hat{\varepsilon}^{2})\tilde{X}_{2}(\tilde{X}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\tilde{X}_{2})^{-1},

where
\tilde{X}_{2}=(I_{n}-H_{1})X_{2}
.

9.4
Statistical properties of under-fitted OLS

Assume that
Y=X\beta+\varepsilon=X_{1}\beta_{1}+X_{2}\beta_{2}+\varepsilon
follows the Gauss–Markov model, where
X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times l}
, and
\textup{cov}(\varepsilon)=\sigma^{2}I_{n}
. However, we only fit the OLS of
Y
on
X_{2}
with coefficient
\tilde{\beta}_{2}
and estimated variance
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}
. Show that

E(\tilde{\beta}_{2})=(X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{2})^{-1}X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{1}\beta_{1}+\beta_{2},\quad\textup{var}(\tilde{\beta}_{2})=\sigma^{2}(X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{2})^{-1}

and

E(\tilde{\sigma}^{2})=\sigma^{2}+\beta_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(I_{n}-H_{2})X_{1}\beta_{1}/(n-l)\geq\sigma^{2}.

Part IV
Model Fitting, Checking, and Misspecification

Chapter 10
Multiple Correlation Coefficient

This chapter will introduce the
R^{2}
, the
multiple correlation coefficient
, also called the
coefficient of determination

(
Wright, 1921
)
. It can achieve two goals:
first, it extends the sample Pearson correlation
coefficient between two scalars to a measure of correlation between a
scalar outcome and a vector covariate;
second, it measures how well multiple covariates
can linearly represent an outcome.

39
Equivalent definitions of
R^{2}

I start with the standard definition of
R^{2}
between
Y
and
X
.
Slightly different from other chapters,
X
excludes the column of
1
’s, so now
X
is an
n\times(p-1)
matrix.
Based on the OLS of
Y
on
(1_{n},X)
, we define

R^{2}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}.

We have discussed before that including
1_{n}
in the OLS ensures

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=0,

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{y}_{i},

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\bar{y}=\bar{\hat{y}},

i.e., the average of the fitted values equals the average of the original observed
outcomes. So I use
\bar{y}
for both the means of outcomes
and the fitted values. With scaling factor
(n-1)^{-1}
, the denominator
of
R^{2}
is the sample variance of the outcomes, and the numerator
of
R^{2}
is the sample variance of the fitted values. We can verify
the following decomposition:

Lemma 10.1

We have the following variance decomposition:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{y}_{i})^{2}.

I leave the proof of Lemma
10.1
as Problem
10.1
.
Lemma
10.1
states that the total sum of squares
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}

equals the regression sum of squares
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}

plus the residual sum of squares
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{y}_{i})^{2}
.
From Lemma

10.1
,
R^{2}
must be lie within the interval

[0,1]
which measures the proportion of the regression sum of squares
in the total sum of squares. An immediate consequence of Lemma
10.1
is that

\textsc{rss}=(1-R^{2})\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}.

We can also verify that
R^{2}
is the squared sample Pearson correlation
coefficient between
Y
and
\hat{Y}
.

Theorem 10.1

We have
R^{2}=\hat{\rho}_{y\hat{y}}^{2}
where

\hat{\rho}_{y\hat{y}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}}.

(62)

I leave the proof of Theorem
10.1
as Problem
10.2
. It states that the multiple correlation coefficient equals the squared Pearson correlation coefficient between
y_{i}
and
\hat{y}_{i}
.
Although the sample Pearson correlation coefficient can be positive or negative,
R^{2}
is always non-negative.
Geometrically,
R^{2}
equals the squared cosine of the angle between the centered vectors
Y-\bar{y}1_{n}
and
\hat{Y}-\bar{y}1_{n}
; see Chapter
28.A
.

In terms of long and short regressions, we can partition the design matrix into
1_{n}
and
X
, then the OLS fit of the long regression is

Y=1_{n}\hat{\beta}_{0}+X\hat{\beta}+\hat{\varepsilon},

(63)

and the OLS fit of the short regression is

Y=1_{n}\tilde{\beta}_{0}+\tilde{\varepsilon},

(64)

with
\tilde{\beta}_{0}=\bar{y}
.
The total sum of squares is the residual sum of squares from the short
regression so by Lemma
10.1
,
R^{2}
also equals

R^{2}=\frac{\textsc{rss}_{\text{short}}-\textsc{rss}_{\text{long}}}{\textsc{rss}_{\text{short}}}.

(65)

40
R^{2}
and the
F
statistic

Under the Normal linear model

Y=1_{n}\beta_{0}+X\beta+\varepsilon,\qquad\varepsilon\sim\textsc{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}),

(66)

we can use the
F
statistic to test whether
\beta=0
. This

F
statistic is a monotone function of
R^{2}
. Most standard software
packages report both
F
and
R^{2}
. I first give a numeric result without assuming that model (
66
) is correct.

Theorem 10.2

We have

F=\frac{n-p}{p-1}\times\frac{R^{2}}{1-R^{2}}.

Proof of Theorem
10.2
:

Based on the long regression (
63
) and the short regression
(
64
), we have (
65
) and

F=\frac{(\textsc{rss}_{\text{short}}-\textsc{rss}_{\text{long}})/(p-1)}{\textsc{rss}_{\text{long}}/(n-p)}.

So the conclusion follows.

\square

I then give the exact distribution of
R^{2}
under the Normal linear model.

Corollary 10.1

Under the Normal linear model (
66
), if
\beta=0
,
then

R^{2}\sim\textup{Beta}\left(\frac{p-1}{2},\frac{n-p}{2}\right).

Proof of Corollary
10.1
:

By definition, the
F
statistic can be represented as

F=\frac{\chi_{p-1}^{2}/(p-1)}{\chi_{n-p}^{2}/(n-p)}

where
\chi_{p-1}^{2}
and
\chi_{n-p}^{2}
denote independent
\chi_{p-1}^{2}
and
\chi_{n-p}^{2}
random variables, respectively, with a little abuse of notation. Using Theorem
10.2
,
we have

\frac{R^{2}}{1-R^{2}}=F\times\frac{p-1}{n-p}=\frac{\chi_{p-1}^{2}}{\chi_{n-p}^{2}}

which implies

R^{2}=\frac{\chi_{p-1}^{2}}{\chi_{p-1}^{2}+\chi_{n-p}^{2}}\text{.}

Because
\chi_{p-1}^{2}\sim\textup{Gamma}\left(\frac{p-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)

and
\chi_{n-p}^{2}\sim\textup{Gamma}\left(\frac{n-p}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)
by Proposition
29.1
,
we have

R^{2}=\frac{\textup{Gamma}\left(\frac{p-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\textup{Gamma}\left(\frac{p-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)+\textup{Gamma}\left(\frac{n-p}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}

where
\textup{Gamma}\left(\frac{p-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\textup{Gamma}\left(\frac{n-p}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)
denote independent Gamma random variables, with a little abuse of notation.
The
R^{2}
follows the Beta distribution by the Beta–Gamma duality in Theorem
29.1
.

\square

+

check
.
names

)

The
R
code is in

Homework problems

.

10.3
Exact distribution of
\hat{\rho}

Assume the Normal linear model
y_{i}=\alpha+\beta x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}
with
a univariate
x_{i}
with
\beta=0
and
\varepsilon_{i}
’s IID
\textsc{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
.
Find the exact distribution of
\hat{\rho}_{xy}
.

10.4
Partial
R^{2}

The form (
65
) of
R^{2}
is well defined in more general long and short regressions:

\hat{Y}=1_{n}\hat{\beta}_{0}+X\hat{\beta}+W\hat{\gamma}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{Y}

and

\hat{Y}=1_{n}\tilde{\beta}_{0}+W\tilde{\gamma}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{Y}

where
X
is an
n\times k
matrix and
W
is an
n\times l
matrix. Define the partial
R^{2}
between
Y
and
X
given
W
as

R^{2}_{Y.X|W}=\frac{\textsc{rss}(Y\sim 1_{n}+W)-\textsc{rss}(Y\sim 1_{n}+X+W)}{\textsc{rss}(Y\sim 1_{n}+W)}

which spells out the formulas of the long and short regressions. This is an intuitive measure of the multiple correlation between
Y
and
X
after controlling for
W
. The following properties make this intuition more explicit.

1.

The partial
R^{2}
equals

R^{2}_{Y.X|W}=\frac{R^{2}_{Y.XW}-R^{2}_{Y.W}}{1-R^{2}_{Y.W}}

where
R^{2}_{Y.XW}
is the multiple correlation between
Y
and
(X,W)
, and
R^{2}_{Y.W}
is the multiple correlation between
Y
and
W
.

2.

The partial
R^{2}
equals the
R^{2}
between
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{Y}
and
\tilde{\epsilon}_{X}
:

R^{2}_{Y.X|W}=R^{2}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{Y}.\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}}

where
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}
is the residual matrix from the OLS fit of
X
on
(1_{n},W)
.

Prove the above two results.

Do the following two results hold?

\displaystyle R^{2}_{Y.XW}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R^{2}_{Y.W}+R^{2}_{Y.X|W},

\displaystyle R^{2}_{Y.XW}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R^{2}_{Y.W|X}+R^{2}_{Y.X|W}.

For each result, give a proof if it is correct, or give a counterexample if it is incorrect in general.

10.5
Omitted-variable bias in terms of the partial
R^{2}

Revisit Section
37
on the following three OLS fits. The first one involves only the observed variables:

y_{i}=\tilde{\beta}_{0}+\tilde{\beta}_{1}z_{i}+\tilde{\beta}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}_{2}x_{i}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i},

and the second and third ones involve the unobserved
u
:

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{0}+\hat{\beta}_{1}z_{i}+\hat{\beta}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}_{2}x_{i}+\hat{\beta}_{3}u_{i}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{i},

\displaystyle u_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}_{0}+\hat{\delta}_{1}z_{i}+\hat{\delta}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{i}+\hat{v}_{i}.

The omitted-variable bias formula states that
\tilde{\beta}-\hat{\beta}_{1}=\hat{\beta}_{3}\hat{\delta}_{1}
. This formula is simple but may be difficult to interpret since
u
is unobserved and its scale is unclear to researchers.

Prove that the formula has an alternative form:

|\tilde{\beta}_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{1}|^{2}=R^{2}_{Y.U\mid ZX}\times\frac{R^{2}_{Z.U\mid X}}{1-R^{2}_{Z.U\mid X}}\times\frac{\textsc{rss}(Y\sim 1_{n}+Z+X)}{\textsc{rss}(Z\sim 1_{n}+X)}.

Remark:
Cinelli and Hazlett, 2020
suggested the partial
R^{2}
parametrization for the omitted-variable bias formula.
The formula has three factors: the first two factors depend on the unknown
sensitivity parameters

R^{2}_{Y.U\mid ZX}
and
R^{2}_{Z.U\mid X}
, and the third factor equals the ratio of the two residual sums of squares based on the observed data.

Chapter 11
Leverage Scores and Leave-One-Out Formulas

43
Leverage scores

We have seen the use of the hat matrix
H=X(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
in previous chapters. Because

H=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{pmatrix}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&\cdots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix},

its
(i,j)
th element equals
h_{ij}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{j}.

In this chapter, we will pay special attention
to its diagonal elements

h_{ii}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\quad(i=1,\ldots,n)

often called the
leverage scores
, which play important roles in many
discussions later.

First, because
H
is a projection matrix of rank
p
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{ii}=\text{trace}(H)=\text{rank}(H)=p

which implies that

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{ii}=p/n,

i.e., the average of the leverage scores equals
p/n
and the maximum of the leverage scores must be larger than or equal to
p/n
, which is close
to zero when
p
is small relative to
n
.

Second, because
H=H^{2}
and
H=H^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
, we have

h_{ii}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}h_{ji}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}^{2}=h_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{j\neq i}h_{ij}^{2}\geq h_{ii}^{2}

which implies

h_{ii}\in[0,1],

i.e., each leverage score is bounded between zero and one
13
13

13

This also follows from Theorem
28.4
since the eigenvalues of
H
are
0
and
1
.
.

Third, because
\hat{Y}=HY
, we have

\hat{y}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}y_{j}=h_{ii}y_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}h_{ij}y_{j}

which implies that

\frac{\text{$\partial\hat{y}_{i}$}}{\partial y_{i}}=h_{ii}.

So
h_{ii}
measures the contribution of
y_{i}
in the predicted
value
\hat{y}_{i}.
In general, we do not want the contribution
of
y_{i}
in predicting itself to be too large, because this means
we do not borrow enough information from other observations, making
the prediction very noisy. This is also clear from the variance of the predicted value

\hat{y}_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}
under the Gauss--Markov model:
14
14

14

We have already proved a more general result on the covariance matrix of
\hat{Y}
in Theorem
4.2
.

\displaystyle\text{{var}}(\hat{y}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\textup{cov}(\hat{\beta})x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}h_{ii}.

So the variance of
\hat{y}_{i}
increases with
h_{ii}
.

The final property of
h_{ii}
is less obvious: it measures whether
observation
i
is an outlier based on its covariate value, that
is, whether
x_{i}
is far from the center of the data. Partition the
design matrix as
X=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1_{n}&X_{2}\end{array}\right)
with
H_{1}=n^{-1}1_{n}1_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.
The covariates
X_{2}
has sample mean
\bar{x}_{2}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}
and sample covariance

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i2}-\bar{x}_{2})(x_{i2}-\bar{x}_{2})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)^{-1}X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(I_{n}-H_{1})X_{2}.

The sample Mahalanobis distance between
x_{i2}
and the center
\bar{x}_{2}

is

D_{i}^{2}=(x_{i2}-\bar{x}_{2})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}S^{-1}(x_{i2}-\bar{x}_{2}).

The following theorem shows that
h_{ii}
is a monotone function
of
D_{i}^{2}
:

Theorem 11.1

We have

h_{ii}=\frac{1}{n}+\frac{D_{i}^{2}}{n-1},

(67)

so
h_{ii}\geq 1/n
.

Proof of Theorem
11.1
:

The definition of
D_{i}^{2}
implies that it is the
(i,i)
th element
of the following matrix:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{12}-\bar{x}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n2}-\bar{x}_{2}\end{array}\right)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}S^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}x_{12}-\bar{x}_{2}&\cdots&x_{n2}-\bar{x}_{2}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I_{n}-H_{1})X_{2}\left\{(n-1)^{-1}X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(I_{n}-H_{1})X_{2}\right\}^{-1}X_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(I_{n}-H_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)\tilde{X}_{2}(\tilde{X}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\tilde{X}_{2})^{-1}\tilde{X}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)\tilde{H}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)(H-H_{1}),

recalling that
\tilde{X}_{2}=(I_{n}-H_{1})X_{2}
,
\tilde{H}_{2}=\tilde{X}_{2}(\tilde{X}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\tilde{X}_{2})^{-1}\tilde{X}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
, and
H=H_{1}+\tilde{H}_{2}
by Lemma
7.2
.
Therefore,

D_{i}^{2}=(n-1)(h_{ii}-1/n)

which implies (
67
).

\square

These are the basic properties of the leverage scores.

Chatterjee and Hadi, 1988
provided an in-depth discussion of the properties of the leverage scores.
We will see their roles frequently in later parts of the chapter.

Another advanced result on the leverage scores is due to

Huber, 1973
. He proved that in the linear model with non-Normal IID
\varepsilon_{i}\sim[0,\sigma^{2}]
and
\sigma^{2}<\infty
, all linear combinations of the OLS coefficient are asymptotically Normal if and only if the maximum leverage score converges to
0
. This is a very elegant asymptotic result on the OLS coefficient. I give more details in Chapter
30
as an application of the Lindeberg–Feller CLT.

44
Leave-one-out formulas

To measure the impact of the
i
th observation on the final OLS estimator,
a natural approach is to delete the
i
th row from the full data

X=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{array}\right),\quad Y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right),

and check how much the OLS estimator changes. Let

X_{[-i]}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{i-1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
x_{i+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{array}\right),\quad Y_{[-i]}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{i-1}\\
y_{i+1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right)

denote the leave-
i
-out data, and define

\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}=(X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]})^{-1}X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y_{[-i]}

(68)

as the corresponding OLS estimator. We can fit
n
OLS by deleting
the
i
th row
(i=1,\ldots,n)
. However, this is computationally
intensive especially when
n
is large. The following theorem shows
that we need only to fit OLS once.

Theorem 11.2

Recalling that
\hat{\beta}
is the full data OLS,
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}

is the residual and
h_{ii}
is the leverage score for the
i
th
observation, we have

\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}=\hat{\beta}-(1-h_{ii})^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}

if
h_{ii}\neq 1
.

Proof of Theorem
11.2
:

From (
68
), we need to invert

X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]}=\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}x_{i^{\prime}}x_{i^{\prime}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X-x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

and calculate

X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y_{[-i]}=\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}x_{i}y_{i}=X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y-x_{i}y_{i},

which are the original
X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X
and
X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y
without the contribution of the
i
th observation.
Using the following Sherman–Morrison formula in Problem
28.3
:

(A+uv^{\tiny\textsc{t}})^{-1}=A^{-1}-(1+v^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A^{-1}u)^{-1}A^{-1}uv^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A^{-1}

with
A=X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X,u=x_{i}
, and
v=-x_{i}
we can invert
X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]}

as

\displaystyle(X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}+\left\{1-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\right\}^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}+\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}

\displaystyle=(X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]})^{-1}X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y_{[-i]}

\displaystyle=\left\{(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}+\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}\right\}\left(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y-x_{i}y_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y

\displaystyle\ -(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}y_{i}

\displaystyle\ +\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y

\displaystyle\ -\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}y_{i}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}-(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}y_{i}+\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}-h_{ii}\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}y_{i}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}-\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}y_{i}+\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\hat{y}_{i}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}-\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}.

\square

With the leave-
i
-out OLS estimator
\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}
, we can
define the predicted residual

\hat{\varepsilon}_{[-i]}=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}_{[-i]},

which is different from the original residual
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}.
The
predicted residual based on leave-one-out can better measure the performance
of the prediction because it mimics the real problem of predicting a
future observation. In contrast, the original residual based on the full
data
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}
gives an overly optimistic measure of the performance of the prediction. This is related
to the overfitting issue discussed later. Under the Gauss–Markov
model, Theorem
4.2
implies that the original residual
has mean zero and variance

\textup{var}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{i})=\sigma^{2}(1-h_{ii}),

(69)

and we can show that the predicted residual has mean zero and variance

\textup{var}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{[-i]})=\textup{var}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}_{[-i]})=\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{2}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]})^{-1}x_{i}.

(70)

The following theorem further simplifies the predicted residual and
its variance.

Theorem 11.3

We have

\hat{\varepsilon}_{[-i]}=\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}/(1-h_{ii}),

and under Assumption
4.1
, we have

\textup{var}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{[-i]})=\sigma^{2}/(1-h_{ii}).

(71)

Proof of Theorem
11.3
:

By definition and Theorem
11.2
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\varepsilon}_{[-i]}

\displaystyle=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}

\displaystyle=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\left\{\hat{\beta}-\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}+\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}

\displaystyle=\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}+h_{ii}\left(1-h_{ii}\right)^{-1}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}

\displaystyle=\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}/(1-h_{ii})\text{.}

(72)

Combining (
69
) and (
72
), we can derive its variance formula.

\square

Comparing formulas (
70
) and (
71
),
we obtain that

1+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]})^{-1}x_{i}=(1-h_{ii})^{-1}=\left\{1-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\right\}^{-1}.

This is not an obvious linear algebra identity, but it follows immediately from
the two ways of calculating the variance of the predicted residual.

45
Applications of the leave-one-out formulas

45.1
Gauss updating formula

Consider an online setting in which the data points come sequentially
as illustrated by the figure below:

In this setting, we can update
the OLS estimator step by step: based on the first
n
data points

(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, we calculate the OLS estimator
\hat{\beta}_{(n)}
,
and with an additional data point
(x_{n+1},y_{n+1})
, we update
the OLS estimator as
\hat{\beta}_{(n+1)}
. These two OLS estimators
are closely related as shown in the following theorem.

Theorem 11.4

Let
X_{(n)}
be the design matrix and
Y_{(n)}

be the outcome vector for the first
n
observations. We have

\hat{\beta}_{(n+1)}=\hat{\beta}_{(n)}+\gamma_{(n+1)}\hat{\varepsilon}_{[n+1]},

where
\gamma_{(n+1)}=(X_{(n+1)}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{(n+1)})^{-1}x_{n+1}
and

\hat{\varepsilon}_{[n+1]}=y_{n+1}-x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}_{(n)}

is the predicted residual of the
(n+1)
the outcome based on the
OLS of the first
n
observations.

Proof of Theorem
11.4
:

This is the reverse form of the leave-one-out formula. We can view
the first
n+1
data points as the full data, and
\hat{\beta}_{(n)}

as the OLS estimator leaving the
(n+1)
the observation out.
Applying Theorem
11.2
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{(n)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}_{(n+1)}-(X_{(n+1)}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{(n+1)})^{-1}x_{n+1}\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{n+1}}{1-h_{n+1,n+1}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}_{(n+1)}-\gamma_{(n+1)}\hat{\varepsilon}_{[n+1]},

where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{n+1}
is the
(n+1)
th residual based
on the full data OLS, and the
(n+1)
th predicted residual equals

\hat{\varepsilon}_{[n+1]}=\hat{\varepsilon}_{n+1}/(1-h_{n+1,n+1})

based on Theorem
11.3
.

\square

Theorem
11.4
shows that to obtain
\hat{\beta}_{(n+1)}

from
\hat{\beta}_{(n)}
, the adjustment depends on the predicted
residual
\hat{\varepsilon}_{[n+1]}
. If we have a perfect prediction
of the
(n+1)
th observation based on
\hat{\beta}_{(n)}
, then
we do not need to make any adjustment to obtain
\hat{\beta}_{(n+1)}
;
if the predicted residual is large, then we need to make a large
adjustment.

Theorem
11.4
suggests an algorithm for sequentially
computing the OLS estimators. But it gives a formula that involves inverting
X_{(n+1)}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{(n+1)}
at each step.
Using the Sherman–Morrison formula in Problem
28.3

for updating the inverse of
X_{(n+1)}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{(n+1)}
based on the
inverse of
X_{(n)}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{(n)}
, we have an even simpler algorithm
below:

(G1)

Start with
V_{(n)}=(X_{(n)}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{(n)})^{-1}
and
\hat{\beta}_{(n)}
.

(G2)

Update

V_{(n+1)}=V_{(n)}-\left\{1+x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}V_{(n)}x_{n+1}\right\}^{-1}V_{(n)}x_{n+1}x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}V_{(n)}.

(G3)

Calculate
\gamma_{(n+1)}=V_{(n+1)}x_{n+1}
and
\hat{\varepsilon}_{[n+1]}=y_{n+1}-x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}_{(n)}
.

(G4)

Update
\hat{\beta}_{(n+1)}=\hat{\beta}_{(n)}+\gamma_{(n+1)}\hat{\varepsilon}_{[n+1]}
.

45.2
Outlier detection based on residuals

Under the Normal linear model
Y=X\beta+\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon\sim\textsc{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n})
,
we know some basic probabilistic properties of the residual vector:

E(\hat{\varepsilon})=0,\qquad\textup{var}(\hat{\varepsilon})=\sigma^{2}(I_{n}-H).

At the same time, the residual vector is computable based on the data.
So it is sensible to check whether these properties of the residual
vector are plausible based on data, which in turn serves as modeling
checking for the Normal linear model.

The first quantity is the standardized residual

\text{standr}_{i}=\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}^{2}(1-h_{ii})}}.

We may hope that it has mean 0 and variance 1. However, because
of the dependence between
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}
and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
, it is not easy to quantify the exact distribution
of
\text{standr}_{i}
.

The second quantity is the studentized residual
based on the predicted residual:

\text{studr}_{i}=\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{[-i]}}{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{[-i]}^{2}/(1-h_{ii})}}=\frac{y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}}{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{[-i]}^{2}/(1-h_{ii})}},

where
\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{[-i]}^{2}
are the estimates of the coefficient and variance based
on the leave-
i
-out OLS. Because
(y_{i},\hat{\beta}_{[-i]},\hat{\sigma}_{[-i]}^{2})

are mutually independent under the Normal linear model, we can show
that

\text{studr}_{i}\sim t_{n-p-1}.

(73)

Because we know the distribution of
\text{studr}_{i}
, we can compare it to the quantiles
of the
t
distribution.

The third quantity is Cook’s distance
(
Cook, 1977
)
:

\displaystyle\text{cook}_{i}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}-\hat{\beta})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X(\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}-\hat{\beta})/(p\hat{\sigma}^{2})

\displaystyle=(X\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}-X\hat{\beta})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}-X\hat{\beta})/(p\hat{\sigma}^{2}),

where the first form measures the change of the OLS estimator and
the second form measures the change in the predicted values based
on leaving-
i
-out. It has a slightly different motivation, but
eventually, it is related to the previous two residuals due to the leave-one-out formulas.

Theorem 11.5

Cook’s distance is related to the standardized residual via:

\textup{cook}_{i}=\textup{standr}_{i}^{2}\times\frac{h_{ii}}{p(1-h_{ii})}.

I leave the proof of Theorem
11.5
as Problem
11.4
.

I will end this subsubsection with two examples. The
R
code is in
code11
.3.2.
R
. The first one is simulated.
I generate data from a univariate Normal linear model without outliers. I then use
hatvalues
,
r
.
standard
,
r
.
student
and
cooks
.
distance
to an
lm
.
object
to calculate the leverage scores, standardized residuals, studentized residuals, and Cook’s distances. Their plots are in the first column of Figure
10
.

(

)

If I add
8
to the outcome of the last observation, the plots change to the second column of Figure

. If I add
8
to the
50
th observation, the plots change to the last column of Figure

. Both visually show the outliers.
In this example, the three residual plots give qualitatively the same pattern, so the choice among them does not matter much. In general cases, we may prefer
\text{studr}_{i}
because it has a known distribution under the Normal linear model.

Figure 10:
Outlier detections

The second one is a further analysis of the Lalonde data. Based on the plots in Figure
11
, there are indeed some outliers in the data. It is worth investigating them more carefully.

Figure 11:
Outlier detections in the LaLonde data

Although the outliers detection methods above are very classic, they are rarely implemented in modern data analyses. They are simple and useful diagnostics. I recommend using them at least as a part of the exploratory data analysis.

45.3
Jackknife

Jackknife is a general strategy for bias reduction and variance estimation proposed by
Quenouille, 1949
;
Quenouille, 1956
and popularized by
Tukey, 1958
. Based on independent data
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
, how to estimate the variance of a general estimator
\hat{\theta}(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
of the parameter
\theta
? Define
\hat{\theta}_{[-i]}
as the estimator without observation
i
, and the pseudo-value as

\tilde{\theta}_{i}=n\hat{\theta}-(n-1)\hat{\theta}_{[-i]}.

The jackknife point estimator is
\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{j}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\theta}_{i}
, and the jackknife variance estimator is

\hat{V}_{\textsc{j}}=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{\theta}_{i}-\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{j}})(\tilde{\theta}_{i}-\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{j}})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

We have already shown that the OLS coefficient is unbiased and derived several variance estimators for it.
Here we focus on the jackknife in OLS using the leave-one-out formula for the coefficient. The pseudo-value is

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\hat{\beta}-(n-1)\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\hat{\beta}-(n-1)\left\{\hat{\beta}-(1-h_{ii})^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}+(n-1)(1-h_{ii})^{-1}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}x_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}.

The jackknife point estimator is

\hat{\beta}_{\textsc{j}}=\hat{\beta}+\frac{n-1}{n}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}}{1-h_{ii}}\right).

It is a little unfortunate that the jackknife point estimator is not identical to the OLS estimator, which is BLUE under the Gauss–Markov model.
We can show that
E(\hat{\beta}_{\textsc{j}})=\beta
and it is a linear estimator. So the Gauss–Markov theorem ensures that
\textup{cov}(\hat{\beta}_{\textsc{j}})\succeq\textup{cov}(\hat{\beta})
.

Nevertheless, the difference between
\hat{\beta}_{\textsc{j}}
and
\hat{\beta}
is quite small. I omit their difference in the following derivation. Assuming that
\hat{\beta}_{\textsc{j}}\cong\hat{\beta}
, we can continue to calculate the approximate jackknife variance estimator:

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\textsc{j}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{\beta}_{i}-\hat{\beta})(\tilde{\beta}_{i}-\hat{\beta})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n-1}{n}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}}{1-h_{ii}}\right)^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1},

which is almost identical to the HC3 form of the EHW covariance matrix introduced in Chapter
23.2
.
Miller, 1974
first analyzed the jackknife in OLS but dismissed it immediately.
Hinkley, 1977
modified the original jackknife and proposed a version that is identical to HC1, and
Wu, 1986
proposed some further modifications and proposed a version that is identical to HC2.

Weber, 1986
made connections between EHW and jackknife standard errors.
However,
Long and Ervin, 2000
’s finite-sample simulation seems to favor the original jackknife or HC3.

Homework problems

11.1
Implementing the Gauss updating formula

Implement the algorithm in (G
1
)–(G
4
), and try it on simulated data.

11.2
The distribution of the studentized residual

Prove (
73
).

11.3
Leave-one-out coefficient

Prove

\hat{\beta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\hat{\beta}_{[-i]},

and find the weights
w_{i}
’s. Show they are positive and sum to
one. Does
\hat{\beta}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\beta}_{[-i]}
hold in general?

11.4
Cook’s distance and the standardized residual

Prove Theorem
11.5
.

11.5
The relationship between the standardized and studentized residual

Prove the following results.

1.

(n-p-1)\hat{\sigma}_{[-i]}^{2}=(n-p)\hat{\sigma}^{2}-\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}/(1-h_{ii})
.

2.

There is a monotone relationship between the standardized and studentized
residual:

\text{studr}_{i}=\text{standr}_{i}\sqrt{\frac{n-p-1}{n-p-\text{standr}_{i}^{2}}}.

Remark: The right-hand side of the first result must be nonnegative so
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\geq\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}/(1-h_{ii})
, which
implies the following inequality:

h_{ii}+\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}}\leq 1.

From this inequality, if
h_{ii}=1
then
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=0
which further implies that
h_{ij}=0
for all
j\neq i.

11.6
Subset and full-data OLS coefficients

Leaving one observation out, we have the OLS coefficient formula in Theorem
11.2
. Leave a subset of observations out, we have the OLS coefficient formula below. Partition the covariate matrix and outcome vector based on a subset
S
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
:

X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{S}\\
X_{\backslash S}\end{pmatrix},\qquad Y=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{S}\\
Y_{\backslash S}\end{pmatrix}.

Without using the observations in set
S
, we have the OLS coefficient

\hat{\beta}_{\backslash S}=(X_{\backslash S}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{\backslash S})^{-1}X_{\backslash S}Y_{\backslash S}.

The following formula facilitates the computation of many
\hat{\beta}_{\backslash S}
’s simultaneously, which relies crucially on the subvector of the residuals

\hat{\varepsilon}_{S}=Y_{S}-X_{S}\hat{\beta}_{\backslash S}

and the submatrix of
H

H_{SS}=X_{\backslash S}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X_{\backslash S}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

corresponding to the set
S
.

Theorem 11.6

Recalling that
\hat{\beta}
is the full data OLS, we have

\hat{\beta}_{\backslash S}=\hat{\beta}-(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X_{\backslash S}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(I-H_{SS})^{-1}\hat{\varepsilon}_{S}

where
I
is the identity matrix with the same dimension of
H_{SS}
.

Prove Theorem
11.6
.

11.7
Bounding the leverage score

Theorem
11.1
shows
n^{-1}\leq h_{ii}\leq 1
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Prove the following stronger result:

n^{-1}\leq h_{ii}\leq s_{i}^{-1}

where
s_{i}
is the number of rows that are identical to
x_{i}
or
-x_{i}
.

11.8
More on the leverage score

Show that

\text{det}(X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]})=(1-h_{ii})\text{det}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X).

Remark: If
h_{ii}=1
, then
X_{[-i]}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{[-i]}
is degenerate with determinant
0
. Therefore, if we delete an observation
i
with leverage score
1
, the columns of the covariate matrix become linearly dependent.

Chapter 12
Population Ordinary Least Squares and Inference with a Misspecified Linear Model

Previous chapters assume fixed
X
and random
Y
. We can also view each observation
(x_{i},y_{i})
as IID draws from a population and discuss population OLS. We will view the OLS from the level of random variables instead of data points. Besides its theoretical interest, the population OLS facilitates the discussion of the properties of misspecified linear models and motivates a prediction procedure without imposing any distributional assumptions.

47
Population OLS

Assume that
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are IID with the same distribution
as
(x,y)
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Below I will use
(x,y)
to denote a general observation, dropping the subscript
i
for simplicity.
Let
the joint distribution be
F(x,y)
, and
E(\cdot)
,
\textup{var}(\cdot)
,
and
\textup{cov}(\cdot)
be the expectation, variance, and covariance under
this joint distribution. We want to use
x
to predict
y
.
The following theorem states that the conditional expectation
E(y\mid x)

is the best predictor in terms of the mean squared error.

Theorem 12.1

For any function
m(x)
, we have the decomposition

E\left[\left\{y-m(x)\right\}^{2}\right]=E\left[\left\{E(y\mid x)-m(x)\right\}^{2}\right]+E\{\textup{var}(y\mid x)\},

(74)

provided the existence of the moments in (
74
). This implies

E(y\mid x)=\arg\min_{m(\cdot)}E\left[\left\{y-m(x)\right\}^{2}\right]

with the minimum value equaling
E\{\textup{var}(y\mid x)\},
the expectation of the conditional
variance of
y
given
x
.

Theorem
12.1
is well known in probability theory. I relegate its proof as Problem
12.1
.
We have finite data points, but the function
E(y\mid x)
lies in
an infinite dimensional space. Nonparametric estimation of
E(y\mid x)

is generally a hard problem, especially with a multidimensional
x
.
As a starting point, we often use a linear function of
x
to approximate

E(y\mid x)
and define the population OLS coefficient as

\beta=\arg\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathcal{L}(b),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(b)

\displaystyle=E\left\{(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=E\left\{y^{2}+b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b-2yx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b\right\}

\displaystyle=E(y^{2})+b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}E\left(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)b-2E\left(yx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)b

is a quadratic function of
b
. From the first-order condition,
we have

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}(b)}{\partial b}\mid_{b=\beta}=2E\left(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\beta-2E\left(xy\right)=0

based on Proposition
28.7
,
so

\beta=\left\{E\left(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\right\}^{-1}E\left(xy\right);

(75)

from the second order condition, we have

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{L}(b)}{\partial b\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}=2E\left(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\geq 0.

So
\beta
is the unique minimizer of
\mathcal{L}(b)
.

The above derivation shows that
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
is the best linear predictor,
and the following theorem states precisely that
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
is
the best linear approximation to the possibly nonlinear conditional mean
E(y\mid x).

Theorem 12.2

We have

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\arg\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}E\left[\left\{E(y\mid x)-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b\right\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{E\left(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\right\}^{-1}E\left\{xE(y\mid x)\right\}.

As a special case, when the covariate “
x
” in the above formulas contains
1
and a scalar
x
,
the OLS coefficient has the following form.

Corollary 12.1

For scalar
x
and
y
, we have

(\alpha,\beta)=\arg\min_{(a,b)}E(y-a-bx)^{2},

where
\alpha=E(y)-E(x)\beta
and

\beta=\frac{\textup{cov}(x,y)}{\textup{var}(x)}=\rho_{xy}\sqrt{\frac{\textup{var}(y)}{\textup{var}(x)}}

Recall that

\rho_{xy}=\frac{\textup{cov}(x,y)}{\sqrt{\textup{var}(x)\textup{var}(y)}}

is the population Pearson correlation coefficient. So Corollary
12.1
gives the population version of the Galtonian formula.
I leave the proofs of Theorem
12.2
and Corollary
12.1
as Problems
12.2
and
12.3
.

With
\beta
, we can define

\varepsilon=y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta

(76)

as the population residual. Because we usually do not use the upper-case letter
E
for
\varepsilon
, this notation may cause confusions with previous discussion on OLS where
\varepsilon
denotes the vector of the error terms. Here
\varepsilon
is a scalar in (
76
).
By the definition of
\beta
, we can verify

E(x\varepsilon)=E\left\{x(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\right\}=E(xy)-E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\beta=0.

(77)

If we include
1
as a component of
x
, then
E(\varepsilon)=0
,
which, coupled with (
77
), implies
\textup{cov}(x,\varepsilon)=0
.
So with an intercept in
\beta
, the mean of the population residual
must be zero, and it is uncorrelated with other covariates by construction.

We can also rewrite (
76
) as

y=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon,

(78)

which holds by the definition of the population OLS coefficient and residual without any modeling
assumption. We call (
78
) the
population
OLS decomposition
.

48
Population FWL theorem and Cochran’s formula

To aid interpretation of the population OLS coefficient, we have the
population FWL theorem.

Theorem 12.3

Consider the population OLS decomposition

y=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{p-1}x_{p-1}+\varepsilon

(79)

and the partial population OLS decompositions:

\displaystyle x_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+\text{ no }x_{k}+\cdots+\gamma_{p-1}x_{p-1}+\tilde{x}_{k},

(80)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{0}+\delta_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+\text{ no }x_{k}+\cdots+\delta_{p-1}x_{p-1}+\tilde{y},

(81)

\displaystyle\tilde{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{k}\tilde{x}_{k}+\tilde{\varepsilon},

(82)

where
\tilde{x}_{k}
is the residual from (
80
) and
\tilde{y}
is the residual from (
81
).
The coefficient
\beta_{k}
from (
79
) equals
\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{y})/\textup{var}(\tilde{x}_{k})
, the coefficient of
\tilde{x}_{k}
from the population OLS of
\tilde{y}
on
\tilde{x}_{k}
in (
82
), which also equals

\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},y)/\textup{var}(\tilde{x}_{k}),

the coefficient of
\tilde{x}_{k}
from the population OLS of
y
on
\tilde{x}_{k}
. Moreover,
\varepsilon
from (
79
) equals
\tilde{\varepsilon}
from (
82
).

Similar to the proof of Theorem
7.1
, we can invert the matrix
E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})
in (
75
) to prove Theorem
12.3
directly. Below I adopt an alternative proof which is a modification of the one given by
Angrist and Pischke, 2008
.

Proof of Theorem
12.3
:

Some basic identities of population OLS help to simplify the proof below. First, the OLS decomposition (
80
) ensures

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},x_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+\text{ no }x_{k}+\gamma_{p-1}x_{p-1}+\tilde{x}_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{k})

that is,

\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},x_{k})=\textup{var}(\tilde{x}_{k}).

(83)

It also ensures

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},x_{l})=0,\qquad(l\neq k).

(84)

Second, the OLS decompositions (
79
) and (
81
) ensure

\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\varepsilon)=0,

(85)

because
\tilde{x}_{k}
is a linear combination of
x
by (
80
). It also ensures

\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\delta_{0}+\delta_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+\text{ no }x_{k}+\cdots+\delta_{p-1}x_{p-1})=0,

which further implies

\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},y)=\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{y}).

(86)

Now I prove the equivalent forms of the coefficient
\beta_{k}
.
From (
79
), we have

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},y)

\displaystyle=\textup{cov}\left(\tilde{x}_{k},\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{p-1}x_{p-1}+\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\beta_{k}\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},x_{k})+\sum_{l\neq k}\beta_{l}\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},x_{l})+\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\beta_{k}\textup{var}(\tilde{x}_{k}),

by (
83
)–(
85
).
Therefore,

\beta_{k}=\frac{\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},y)}{\textup{var}(\tilde{x}_{k})},

which also equals
\tilde{\beta}_{k}
by (
86
).

Finally, I prove
\varepsilon=\tilde{\varepsilon}
. It suffices to show that
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\tilde{y}-\tilde{\beta}_{k}\tilde{x}_{k}
satisfies

E(\tilde{\varepsilon})=0,\quad\textup{cov}(x_{k},\tilde{\varepsilon})=0,\quad\textup{cov}(x_{l},\tilde{\varepsilon})=0\ (l\neq k).

The first identity holds by (
82
). The second identity holds because

\textup{cov}(x_{k},\tilde{\varepsilon})=\textup{cov}(x_{k},\tilde{y})-\tilde{\beta}_{k}\textup{cov}(x_{k},\tilde{x}_{k})=\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{y})-\tilde{\beta}_{k}\textup{cov}(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{k})=0

by (
86
), (
83
), and the population OLS of (
82
). The third identity holds because

\textup{cov}(x_{l},\tilde{\varepsilon})=\textup{cov}(x_{l},\tilde{y})-\tilde{\beta}_{k}\textup{cov}(x_{l},\tilde{x}_{k})=0

by (
81
) and (
84
).

\square

We also have a population version of Cochran’s formula.
Assume
(y_{i},x_{i1},x_{i2})_{i=1}^{n}
are IID, where
y_{i}
is a scalar,
x_{i1}
has dimension
k
, and
x_{i2}
has dimension
l
.
We have the following OLS decompositions of random variables

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{i1}+\beta_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{i2}+\varepsilon_{i},

(87)

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{i2}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i},

(88)

\displaystyle x_{i1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{i2}+u_{i}.

(89)

Equation (
87
) defines the population long regression, and Equation (
88
) defines the population short regression. In Equation (
89
),
\delta
is a
l\times k
matrix because it is the OLS decomposition of a vector on a vector. We can view (
89
) as OLS decomposition of each component of
x_{i1}
on
x_{i2}
.
The following theorem states the population version of Cochran’s formula.

Theorem 12.4

Based on (
87
)–(
89
), we have
\tilde{\beta}_{2}=\beta_{2}+\delta\beta_{1}.

I relegate the proof of Theorem
12.4
as Problem
12.5
.

49
Population
R^{2}
and partial correlation coefficient

Exclude
1
from
x
and assume
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p-1}
in this subsection.
Assume that covariates and outcome are centered with mean zero and covariance matrix

\textup{cov}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Sigma_{xx}&\Sigma_{xy}\\
\Sigma_{yx}&\sigma_{y}^{2}\end{array}\right).

There are multiple equivalent definitions of
R^{2}
. I start with the following definition

R^{2}=\frac{\Sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1}\Sigma_{xy}}{\sigma_{y}^{2}},

and will give several equivalent definitions below.
Let
\beta
be the
population OLS coefficient of
y
on
x
, and
\hat{y}=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
be the best
linear predictor.

Theorem 12.5

R^{2}
equals the ratio of the variance of the best linear predictor
of
y
and the variance of
y
itself:

R^{2}=\frac{\textup{var}(\hat{y})}{\textup{var}(y)}.

Proof of Theorem
12.5
:

Because of the centering of
x
, we can verify that

\displaystyle\textup{var}(\hat{y})

\displaystyle=\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\textup{cov}(x)\beta

\displaystyle=\textup{cov}(y,x)\textup{cov}(x)^{-1}\textup{cov}(x)\textup{cov}(x)^{-1}\textup{cov}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\textup{cov}(y,x)\textup{cov}(x)^{-1}\textup{cov}(x,y).

\square

Theorem 12.6

R^{2}
equals the maximum value of the squared Pearson correlation
coefficient between
y
and a linear combination of
x
:

R^{2}=\max_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p-1}}\rho^{2}(y,x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)=\rho^{2}(y,\hat{y}).

Proof of Theorem
12.6
:

We have

\rho^{2}(y,x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)=\frac{\text{{cov}}^{2}(y,x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)}{\textup{var}(y)\textup{var}(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)}=\frac{b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma_{xy}\Sigma_{yx}b}{\sigma_{y}^{2}\times b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma_{xx}b}.

Define
\gamma=\Sigma_{xx}^{1/2}b
and
b=\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2}\gamma

such that
b
and
\gamma
have one-to-one mapping. So the maximum value of

\sigma_{y}^{2}\times\rho^{2}(y,x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)=\frac{\gamma^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{xy}\Sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2}\gamma}{\gamma^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma},

equals the maximum eigenvalue of
\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{xy}\Sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2}
, attained when
\gamma
equals the corresponding eigenvector; see Theorem
28.4
.
This matrix is positive semi-definite and has rank one due to Proposition
28.1
, so it has exactly one non-zero eigenvalue which must equal its trace. So

\displaystyle\max_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p-1}}\rho^{2}(y,x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{y}^{-2}\text{trace}(\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{xy}\Sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{y}^{-2}\text{trace}(\Sigma_{xy}\Sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{y}^{-2}\text{trace}(\Sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1}\Sigma_{xy})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{y}^{-2}\Sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1}\Sigma_{xy}

\displaystyle=R^{2}.

We can easily verify that
R^{2}=\rho^{2}(y,\hat{y}).

\square

We can also define population partial correlation and
R^{2}.
For
scalar
y
and
x
with another scalar or vector
w
, we can define
the population OLS decomposition based on
(1,w)
as

y=\hat{y}+\tilde{y},\qquad x=\hat{x}+\tilde{x},

(90)

where

\tilde{y}=\{y-E(y)\}-\{w-E(w)\}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{y},\qquad\tilde{x}=\{x-E(x)\}-\{w-E(w)\}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{x},

with
\beta_{y}
and
\beta_{x}
being the coefficients of
w
in these population OLS.
We then define the population partial correlation coefficient as

\rho_{yx\mid w}=\rho_{\tilde{y}\tilde{x}}.

If the marginal correlation and partial correlation have different signs, then we have Simpson’s paradox at the population level.

With a scalar
w
, we have a more explicit formula below.

Theorem 12.7

For scalar
(y,x,w)
, we have

\rho_{yx\mid w}=\frac{\rho_{yx}-\rho_{xw}\rho_{yw}}{\sqrt{1-\rho_{xw}^{2}}\sqrt{1-\rho_{yw}^{2}}}.

I relegate the proof of Theorem
12.7
as Problem
12.7
.

We can also extend the definition of
\rho_{yx\mid w}
to the partial
R^{2}
with a scalar
y
and possibly vector
x
and
w
. The population OLS decompositions (
90
) still hold in the general case. Then we can define the partial
R^{2}
between
y
and
x
given
w
as the
R^{2}
between
\tilde{y}
and
\tilde{x}
:

R^{2}_{y.x|w}=R^{2}_{\tilde{y}.\tilde{x}}.

50
Inference for the population OLS

50.1
Inference with the EHW standard errors

Based on the IID data
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, we can easily
obtain the moment estimator for the population OLS coefficient

\hat{\beta}=\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\right),

and the residuals
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}.
Assume finite fourth moments of
(x,y)
. We can use the law of large numbers to show that

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\rightarrow E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}),\quad n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\rightarrow E(xy),

so
\hat{\beta}\rightarrow\beta
in probability. We can use the CLT to show that
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,M)
in distribution, where
M=E(\varepsilon^{2}xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})
,
so

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,V)

(91)

in distribution, where
V=B^{-1}MB^{-1}
with
B=E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})
.
The moment estimator for the asymptotic variance of
\hat{\beta}
is again
the Eicker–Huber–White robust covariance
estimator:

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}=n^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}.

(92)

Following the almost the same proof of Theorem
6.3
, we can show that
\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}
is consistent for the asymptotic covariance
V
. I summarize the formal results below, with the proof relegated as Problem
12.4
.

Theorem 12.8

Assume that
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}(x,y)
with
E(\|x\|^{4})<\infty
and
E(y^{4})<\infty
. We have (
91
) and
n\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}\rightarrow V
in probability.

So the EHW standard error is not only robust to the heteroskedasticity of the errors but also robust to the misspecification of the linear model
(
Huber, 1967
;
White, 1980b
;
Angrist and Pischke, 2008
;
Buja et al., 2019a
)
. Of course, when the linear model is wrong, we need to modify the interpretation of
\beta
: it is the coefficient of
x
in the best linear prediction of
y
or the best linear approximation of the conditional mean function
E(y\mid x)
.

51
To model or not to model?

51.1
Population OLS and the restricted mean model

Figure 12:
Best linear approximations correspond to three different distributions of
x
.

I start with a simple example. In the following calculation, I will use the fact that the
k
th moment of a Uniform
(0,1)
random variable equals
1/(k+1)
.

Example 12.1

Assume that
x\sim F(x)
,
\varepsilon\sim\textsc{N}(0,1),x\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}\varepsilon
,
and
y=x^{2}+\varepsilon
.

1.

If
x\sim F_{1}(x)
is Uniform
(-1,1)
, then the best linear
approximation is
1/3+0\cdot x
. We can see this result from

\beta(F_{1})=\frac{\text{{cov}}(x,y)}{\textup{var}(x)}=\frac{\textup{cov}(x,x^{2})}{\textup{var}(x)}=\frac{E(x^{3})}{\textup{var}(x)}=0,

and
\alpha(F_{1})=E(y)=E(x^{2})=1/3
.

2.

If
x\sim F_{2}(x)
is Uniform
(0,1)
, then the best linear approximation is
-1/6+x
. We can see this result from

\beta(F_{2})=\frac{\textup{cov}(x,y)}{\textup{var}(x)}=\frac{\textup{cov}(x,x^{2})}{\textup{var}(x)}=\frac{E(x^{3})-E(x)E(x^{2})}{E(x^{2})-\left\{E(x)\right\}^{2}}=\frac{1/4-1/2\times 1/3}{1/3-(1/2)^{2}}=1,

and
\alpha(F_{2})=E(y)-\beta E(x)=E(x^{2})-E(x)=1/3-1/2=-1/6

3.

If
x\sim F_{3}(x)
is Uniform
(-1,0)
, then the best linear approximation is
-1/6-x.
This result holds by symmetry.

Figure
12
shows the true conditional mean function
x^{2}
and the best linear approximations. As highlighted in the notation above, the best linear approximation
depends on the distribution of
X
.

From the above, we can see that the best linear approximation
depends on the distribution of
X
. This complicates the interpretation of
\beta
from the population OLS decomposition
(
Buja et al., 2019a
)
. More importantly, this can cause problems if we care about the external validity of statistical inference
(
Sims, 2010
, page 66)
.

However, if we believe the following restricted mean model

\displaystyle E(y\mid x)=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta

(93)

or, equivalently,

y=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon,\qquad E(\varepsilon\mid x)=0,

then the population OLS coefficient is the true parameter of interest:

\displaystyle\left\{E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\right\}^{-1}E(xy)

\displaystyle=\left\{E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\right\}^{-1}E\left\{xE(y\mid x)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\right\}^{-1}E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)

\displaystyle=\beta.

Moreover, the population OLS coefficient does not depend on the distribution
of
x
. The above asymptotic inference applies to this model too.

Freedman, 1981
distinguished two types of OLS: the
regression model
and the
correlation model
, as shown in Figure
13
. The left-hand side represents the regression model, or the restricted mean model (
93
). In the regression model, we first generate
x
and
\varepsilon
under some restrictions, for example,
E(\varepsilon\mid x)=0
, and then generate the outcome based on
y=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon
, a linear function of
x
with error
\varepsilon
. In the correlation model, we start with a pair
(x,y)
, then decompose
y
into the best linear predictor
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
and the leftover residual
\varepsilon
. The latter ensures
E(\varepsilon x)=0
, but the former requires
E(\varepsilon\mid x)=0
. So the former imposes a stronger assumption since
E(\varepsilon\mid x)=0
implies

E(\varepsilon x)=E\{E(\varepsilon x\mid x)\}=E\{E(\varepsilon\mid x)x\}=0.

Figure 13:
Freedman’s classification of OLS

51.3
More on residual plots

Most standard statistical theory for inference assumes a correctly specified linear model (e.g., Gauss–Markov model, Normal linear model, or restricted mean model). However, the corresponding inferential procedures are often criticized since it is challenging to ensure that the model is correctly specified. Alternatively, we can argue that without assuming the linear model, the OLS estimator is consistent for the coefficient in the best linear approximation of the conditional mean function
E(y\mid x)
, which is often a meaningful quantify even the linear model is misspecified. This can be misleading. Example
12.1
shows that the best linear approximation can be a bad approximation to a nonlinear conditional mean function, and it depends on the distribution of the covariates.

A classic statistical approach is to check whether the residual
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}
has any nonlinear trend with respect to the covariates. With only a few covariates, we can plot the residual against each covariate; with many covariates, we can plot the residual against the fitted value
\hat{y}_{i}
. Figure
15
gives four examples with the
R
code in
code12
.5.2.
R
. In these examples, the covariates are the same:

⬇

n

=

200

x1

=

rexp
(
n
)

x2

=

runif
(
n
)

The outcome models differ:

1.

Example a:

y

=

x1

+

x2

+

rnorm
(
n
)

2.

Example b:

y

=

x1

+

x2

+

rnorm
(
n
,

0,

x1
+
x2
)

3.

Example c:

y

=

x1
^2

+

x2
^2

+

rnorm
(

)

4.

Example d:

y

x1
^2

^2

+

rnorm
(

,

0,

x1
+
x2
)

Figure 15:
Residual plots

In the last two examples, the residuals indeed show some nonlinear relationship with the covariates and the fitted value. This suggests that the linear function can be a poor approximation to the true conditional mean function.

52
Conformal prediction based on exchangeability

Chapter
17
discusses the prediction of a future outcome
y_{n+1}
based on
x_{n+1}
and
(X,Y)
. It requires the Normal linear model assumption. Chapter
6
relaxes the Normality assumption on the error term in statistical inference but does not discuss prediction. Under the heteroskedastic linear model assumption, we can predict the mean
E(y_{n+1})=x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
by
x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}
with asymptotic standard error

(x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}x_{n+1})^{1/2},

where
\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}
is the EHW covariance matrix for the OLS coefficient. However, it is fundamentally challenging to predict
y_{n+1}
itself since the heteroskedastic linear model allows it to have a completely unknown variance
\sigma^{2}_{n+1}
.

Under the population OLS formulation, it seems even more challenging to predict the future outcome since we do not even assume that the linear model is correctly specified. In particular,
x_{n+1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}
does not have the same mean as
y_{n+1}
in general. Perhaps surprisingly, we can construct a prediction interval for
y_{n+1}
based on
x_{n+1}
and
(X,Y)
using an idea called
conformal prediction

(
Vovk et al., 2005
;
Lei et al., 2018
)
. It leverages the
exchangeability
15
15

15

Exchangeability is a technical term in probability and statistics. Random elements
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
are exchangeable if
(z_{\pi(1)},\ldots,z_{\pi(n)})
have the same distribution as
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
, where
\pi(1),\ldots,\pi(n)
is a permutation of the integers
1,\ldots,n
. In other words, a set of random elements are exchangeable if their joint distribution does not change under re-ordering. IID random elements are exchangeable.
of the data points

(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n}),(x_{n+1},y_{n+1}).

Pretending that we know the value
y_{n+1}=y^{*}
, we can fit OLS using
n+1
data points and obtain residuals

\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}(y^{*})=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}(y^{*}),\quad(i=1,\ldots,n+1)

where we emphasize the dependence of the OLS coefficient and residuals on the unknown
y^{*}
.
The absolute values of the residuals
|\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}(y^{*})|
’s are also exchangeable, so the rank of
|\hat{\varepsilon}_{n+1}(y^{*})|
, denoted by

\hat{R}_{n+1}(y^{*})=1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}1\{|\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}(y^{*})|\leq|\hat{\varepsilon}_{n+1}(y^{*})|\},

must have a uniform distribution over
\{1,2,\ldots,n,n+1\}
, a known distribution not depending on anything else. It is a pivotal quantity satisfying

\displaystyle\textup{pr}\left\{\hat{R}_{n+1}(y^{*})\leq\lceil(1-\alpha)(n+1)\rceil\right\}\geq 1-\alpha.

(94)

Equivalently, this is a statement linking the unknown quantity
y^{*}
and observed data, so it gives a confidence set for
y^{*}
at level
1-\alpha
. In practice, we can use a grid search to solve for the inequality (
94
) involving
y^{*}
.

12.6
Canonical correlation analysis (CCA)

Assume that
(x,y)
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, has the joint non-degenerate covariance matrix:

\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{xx}&\Sigma_{xy}\\
\Sigma_{yx}&\Sigma_{yy}\end{pmatrix}.

1.

Find the best linear combinations
(\alpha,\beta)
that
give the maximum Pearson correlation coefficient:

(\alpha,\beta)=\text{$\arg\max_{a\in\mathbb{R}^{k},b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}$}\ \rho(y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}a,x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b).

Note that you need to detail the steps in calculating
(\alpha,\beta)
based on the covariance matrix above.

2.

Define the maximum value as
\textsc{cc}(x,y)
. Show that
\textsc{cc}(x,y)\geq 0
and
\textsc{cc}(x,y)=0
if
x\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}y
.

Remark: The maximum value
\textsc{cc}(x,y)
is called the canonical correlation between
x
and
y
.
We can also define partial canonical correlation between
x
and
y
given
w
.

, then
\rho_{yx}=0
.
With another random variable
w
, if
x\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}y\mid w
, does
\rho_{yx\mid w}=0

hold? If so, give a proof; otherwise, give a counterexample.

,

y=x^{3}+\varepsilon
. Find the best linear approximation of

E(y\mid x)
based on

.
