94
More on interpretations of the coefficients

Many practitioners find the coefficients in the logit model difficult to interpret. Another measure of the impact of the covariate on the outcome is the average marginal effect or average partial effect. For a continuous covariate
x_{ij}
, the average marginal effect is defined as

\displaystyle\textsc{ame}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})}{\partial x_{ij}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\beta_{j},

which reduces to the following form for the logit model

\textsc{ame}_{j}=\beta_{j}\times n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(x_{i},\beta)\left\{1-\pi(x_{i},\beta)\right\}.

For a binary covariate
x_{ij}
, the average marginal effect is defined as

\textsc{ame}_{j}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid\ldots,x_{ij}=1,\ldots)-\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid\ldots,x_{ij}=0,\ldots)\}

The
margins
function in the
margins
package can compute the average marginal effects and the corresponding standard errors. In particular, the average marginal effect of
assign
is not significant as shown below. The
R
code in this section is in
code19
.3.
R
.

⬇

(

)

.

)

The interaction term is much more complicated. Contradictory suggestions are given across fields.
Consider
the following model

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1},x_{i2})=g(\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i1}+\beta_{2}x_{i2}+\beta_{12}x_{i1}x_{i2}).

If the link is logit, then epidemiologists interpret
e^{\beta_{12}}
as the interaction between
x_{i1}
and
x_{i2}
on the odds ratio scale. Consider a simple case with binary
x_{i1}
and
x_{i2}
. Given
x_{i2}=1
, the odds ratio of
x_{i1}
on
y_{i}
equals
e^{\beta_{1}+\beta_{12}}
; given
x_{i2}=0
, the odds ratio of
x_{i1}
on
y_{i}
equals
e^{\beta_{1}}
. Therefore, the ratio of the two odds ratio equals
e^{\beta_{12}}
. When we measure effects on the odds ratio scale, the logistic model is a natural choice. The interaction term in the logistic model indeed measures the interaction of
x_{i1}
and
x_{i2}
.

Ai and Norton, 2003
gave a different suggestion.
Define
z_{i}=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i1}+\beta_{2}x_{i2}+\beta_{12}x_{i1}x_{i2}.

We have two ways to define the interaction effect: first,

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1},x_{i2})}{\partial(x_{i1}x_{i2})}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g^{\prime}(z_{i})\beta_{12}.

second,

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1},x_{i2})}{\partial x_{i1}\partial x_{i2}}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i2}}\left\{\frac{\partial\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1},x_{i2})}{\partial x_{i1}}\right\}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i2}}\left\{g^{\prime}(z_{i})(\beta_{1}+\beta_{12}x_{i2})\right\}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{g^{\prime\prime}(z_{i})(\beta_{2}+\beta_{12}x_{i1})(\beta_{1}+\beta_{12}x_{i2})+g^{\prime}(z_{i})\beta_{12}\right\};

Although the first one is more straightforward based on the definition of the average partial effect, the second one is more reasonable based on the natural definition of interaction based on the mixed derivative.
Note that even if
\beta_{12}=0
, the second definition of interaction
does not necessarily equal 0 since

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1},x_{i2})}{\partial x_{i1}\partial x_{i2}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g^{\prime\prime}(z_{i})\beta_{1}\beta_{2}.

This is due to the nonlinearity of the link function.
The second definition quantifies interaction based on the probability itself while the parameters in the logistic model measure the odds ratio. This combination of model and parameter does not seem a natural choice.

96
Extensions of the logistic regression

96.1
Penalized logistic regression

Similar to the high dimensional linear model, we can also extend the logit model to a penalized version. Since the objective function for the original logit model is the log-likelihood, we can minimize the following penalized log-likelihood function:

\arg\min_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{p}}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(\beta)+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{p}\{\alpha\beta_{j}^{2}+(1-\alpha)|\beta_{j}|\},

where

\ell_{i}(\beta)=y_{i}(\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i1}+\cdots+\beta_{p}x_{ip})-\log(1+e^{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i1}+\cdots+\beta_{p}x_{ip}})

is the log-likelihood function. When
\alpha=1
, it gives the ridge analog of the logistic regression; when
\alpha=0
, it gives the lasso analog; when
\alpha\in(0,1)
, it gives the elastic net analog.
The
R
package
glmpath
uses the coordinate descent algorithm based on a quadratic
approximation of the log-likelihood function. We can select the tuning parameter
\lambda
based on cross-validation.

96.2
Case-control study

A nice property of the logit model is that it works not only for the cohort study with data from conditional distribution
y_{i}\mid x_{i}
but also for the case-control study with data from the conditional distribution
x_{i}\mid y_{i}
. The former is a prospective study while the latter is a retrospective study. Below, I will explain the basic idea in
Prentice and Pyke, 1979
.

Assume that
(x_{i},y_{i},s_{i})
IID with

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})=\frac{e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}

(125)

and

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid x_{i},y_{i})=\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid y_{i})=\begin{cases}p_{1},&\text{if }y_{i}=1,\\
p_{0},&\text{if }y_{i}=0.\end{cases}

(126)

But we only have data with
s_{i}=1
with
p_{1}
and
p_{0}
often
unknown. Fortunately, conditioning on
s_{i}=1
, we have the following result.

Theorem 20.1

Under (
125
) and (
126
), we have

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i},s_{i}=1)=\frac{e^{\delta+\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\delta+\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}},

where
\delta=\log(p_{1}/p_{0})
.

Proof of Theorem
20.1
:

We have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i},s_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid x_{i},y_{i}=1)}{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid x_{i},y_{i}=1)+\textup{pr}(y_{i}=0\mid x_{i})\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid x_{i},y_{i}=0)}

by Bayes’ formula. Under the logit model, we have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i},s_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}p_{1}}{\frac{e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}p_{1}+\frac{1}{1+e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}p_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}p_{1}}{e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}p_{1}+p_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}p_{1}/p_{0}}{e^{\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}p_{1}/p_{0}+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\delta+\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\delta+\beta_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}.

\square

Theorem
20.1
ensures that conditioning on
s_{i}=1
,
the model of
y_{i}
given
x_{i}
is still logit with the intercept
changing from
\beta_{0}
to
\beta_{0}+\log(p_{1}/p_{0})
. Although
we cannot consistently estimate the intercept without knowing
(p_{1},p_{0})
,
we can still estimate all the slopes.
Kagan, 2001
showed that the logistic link is the only one that enjoys this property.

Samarani et al., 2019
hypothesized that variation in the inherited activating Killer-cell Immunoglobulin-like Receptor genes in humans is associated with their innate susceptibility/resistance to developing Crohn’s disease. They used a case-control study from three cities (Manitoba, Montreal, and Ottawa) in Canada to investigate the potential association.

97
Other model formulations

97.1
Latent linear model

Let
y_{i}=1(y_{i}^{*}\geq 0)
where

y_{i}^{*}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon_{i}

and
-\varepsilon_{i}
has distribution function
g(\cdot)
and is
independent of
x_{i}
. From this latent linear model, we can verify
that

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=\textup{pr}(y_{i}^{*}\geq 0\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=\textup{pr}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon_{i}\geq 0\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=\textup{pr}(-\varepsilon_{i}\leq x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=g(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta).

So the
g(\cdot)
function can be interpreted as the distribution
function of the error term in the latent linear model.

This latent variable formulation provides another way to interpret the coefficients in the models for binary data. It is a powerful way to generate models for more complex data. We will see another example in the next chapter.

97.2
Inverse model

Assume that

\displaystyle y_{i}\sim\text{Bernoulli}(q),

(127)

and

\displaystyle x_{i}\mid y_{i}=1\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma),\quad x_{i}\mid y_{i}=0\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{0},\Sigma),

(128)

where
x_{i}
does not contain
1
.
This is called the linear discriminant model.
We can verify that
y_{i}\mid x_{i}
follows a logit model as shown in the theorem below.

Theorem 20.2

Under (
139
) and (
140
), we have

\textup{logit}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})\}=\alpha+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta,

where

\displaystyle\alpha=\log\frac{q}{1-q}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mu_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{1}-\mu_{0}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{0}\right),\quad\beta=\Sigma^{-1}\left(\mu_{1}-\mu_{0}\right).

(129)

Proof of Theorem
20.2
:

Using Bayes’ formula, we have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1,x_{i})}{\textup{pr}(x_{i})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1)\textup{pr}(x_{i}\mid y_{i}=1)}{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1)\textup{pr}(x_{i}\mid y_{i}=1)+\textup{pr}(y_{i}=0)\textup{pr}(x_{i}\mid y_{i}=0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\Delta}}{1+e^{\Delta}},

where

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1)\textup{pr}(x_{i}\mid y_{i}=1)}{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=0)\textup{pr}(x_{i}\mid y_{i}=0)}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{q\left\{(2\pi)^{p}\text{det}(\Sigma)\right\}^{-1/2}\exp\left\{-(x_{i}-\mu_{1})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}(x_{i}-\mu_{1})/2\right\}}{(1-q)\left\{(2\pi)^{p}\text{det}(\Sigma)\right\}^{-1/2}\exp\left\{-(x_{i}-\mu_{0})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}(x_{i}-\mu_{0})/2\right\}}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{q\exp\left\{-(x_{i}-\mu_{1})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}(x_{i}-\mu_{1})/2\right\}}{(1-q)\exp\left\{-(x_{i}-\mu_{0})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}(x_{i}-\mu_{0})/2\right\}}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{q\exp\left\{-\left(-2x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{1}+\mu_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{1}\right)/2\right\}}{(1-q)\exp\left\{-\left(-2x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{0}+\mu_{0}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{0}\right)/2\right\}}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{q}{1-q}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mu_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{1}-\mu_{0}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{0}\right)+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\left(\mu_{1}-\mu_{0}\right).

So
y_{i}\mid x_{i}
follows a logistic model with

\alpha
and
\beta
given in (
21.1
).

\square

We can easily obtain the moment estimators for the unknown parameters under (
139
) and (
140
). Let
n_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}
and
n_{0}=n-n_{1}
. The moment estimator for
q
is

\hat{q}=n_{1}/n,

the sample mean of the
y_{i}
’s. The moment estimators for
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{0}
are

\hat{\mu}_{1}=n_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}x_{i},\quad\hat{\mu}_{0}=n_{0}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-y_{i})x_{i},

the sample means of the
x_{i}
’s for units with
y_{i}=1
and
y_{i}=0
, respectively. The moment estimator for
\Sigma
is

\hat{\Sigma}=\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(x_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{1})(x_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{1})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-y_{i})(x_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{0})(x_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{0})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right]/(n-2),

the pooled covariance matrix, after centering the
x_{i}
’s by the
y
-specific means.
Based on Theorem
20.2
, we can obtain estimates
\hat{\alpha}
and
\hat{\beta}
by replacing the true parameters with their moment estimators. This gives us another way to fit the logistic model.

Efron, 1975
compared the above moment estimator and the MLE under the logistic model. Since the linear discriminant model imposes stronger assumptions, the estimator based on Theorem
20.2
is more efficient. In contrast, the MLE of the logistic model is more robust because it does not impose the Normality assumption on
x_{i}
.

98
Homework problems

20.1
Invariance of logistic regression

This problem extends Problem
3.4
.

Assume
\tilde{x}_{i}=x_{i}\Gamma
with an invertible
\Gamma
. Run logistic regression of
y_{i}
’s on
x_{i}
’s to obtain the coefficient
\hat{\beta}
and fitted probabilities
\hat{\pi}_{i}
’s. Run another logistic regression of
y_{i}
’s on
\tilde{x}_{i}
’s to obtain the coefficient
\tilde{\beta}
and fitted probabilities
\tilde{\pi}_{i}
’s.

Show that
\hat{\beta}=\Gamma\tilde{\beta}
and
\hat{\pi}_{i}=\tilde{\pi}_{i}
for all
i
’s.

20.2
Two logistic regressions

This is an extension of Problem
17.2
.

Given data
(x_{i},z_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
where
x_{i}
denotes the covariates,
z_{i}\in\{1,0\}
denotes the binary group indicator, and
y_{i}
denotes the binary outcome. We can fit two separate logistic regressions:

\text{logit}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid z_{i}=1,x_{i})\}=\gamma_{1}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{1}

and

\text{logit}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid z_{i}=0,x_{i})\}=\gamma_{0}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{0}

with the treated and control data, respectively. We can also fit a joint logistic regression using the pooled data:

\text{logit}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid z_{i},x_{i})\}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{z}z_{i}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\alpha_{x}+z_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\alpha_{zx}.

Let the parameters with hats denote the MLEs, for example,
\hat{\gamma}_{1}
is the MLE for
\gamma_{1}
. Find
(\hat{\alpha}_{0},\hat{\alpha}_{z},\hat{\alpha}_{x},\hat{\alpha}_{zx})
in terms of
(\hat{\gamma}_{1},\hat{\beta}_{1},\hat{\gamma}_{0},\hat{\beta}_{0}).

20.3
Likelihood for Probit model

Write down the likelihood function for the Probit model, and derive the
steps for Newton’s method and Fisher scoring for computing the MLE. How do we estimate the asymptotic covariance matrix of the MLE?

20.4
Logit and general exponential family

Efron, 1975
pointed out an extension of Theorem
20.2
.
Show that under (
139
) and

f(x_{i}\mid y_{i}=y)=g(\theta_{y},\eta)h(x_{i},\eta)\exp(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{y}),\quad(y=0,1)

with parameters
(\theta_{1},\theta_{0},\eta)
,
we have

\text{logit}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})\}=\alpha+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta.

Find the formulas of
\alpha
and
\beta
in terms of
(\theta_{1},\theta_{0},\eta)
.

Hint: As a sanity check, you can compare this problem with Theorem
20.2
.

20.5
Empirical comparison of logistic regression and linear discriminant analysis

Compare the performance of logistic regression and linear discriminant analysis in terms of prediction accuracy. You should simulate at least three cases: (1) the model for linear discriminant analysis is correct; (2) the model for linear discriminant analysis is incorrect but the model for logistic regression is correct; (3) the model for logistic regression is incorrect.

20.6
Quadratic discriminant analysis

Assume that

y_{i}\sim\text{Bernoulli}(q),

and

x_{i}\mid y_{i}=1\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),\quad x_{i}\mid y_{i}=0\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{0},\Sigma_{0}),

where
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
does not contain
1
.
Prove that

\textup{logit}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})\}=\alpha+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Lambda x_{i},

where

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\frac{q}{1-q}-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\text{det}(\Sigma_{1})}{\text{det}(\Sigma_{0})}-\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\mu_{1}-\mu_{0}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}\mu_{0}),

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}_{1}\mu_{1}-\Sigma^{-1}_{0}\mu_{0},

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\Sigma^{-1}_{1}-\Sigma^{-1}_{0}).

Remark: This problem extends the linear discriminant model in Section
97.2
to the quadratic discriminant model by allowing for heteroskedasticity in the conditional Normality of
x
given
y
. It implies the logistic model with the linear, quadratic, and interaction terms of the basic covariates.

20.7
Logit and other links

Compute the minimizer and minimum value of
\max_{y}|g_{\text{logit}}(by)-g_{*}(y)|
for
*=
cauchit and cloglog.

20.8
Data analysis

Reanalyze the data in Section
96.2
, stratifying the analysis based on
center
. Do the results vary significantly across centers?

20.9
R^{2}
in logistic regression

Recall that
R^{2}
in the linear model measures the linear dependence of the outcome on the covariates.
However, the definition of
R^{2}
is not obvious in the logistic model. The
glm
function in
R
does not return any
R^{2}
for the logistic regression.

Recall the following equivalent definitions of
R^{2}
in the linear model

\displaystyle R^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{y}_{i})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}^{2}_{y\hat{y}}=\frac{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})\right)^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}.

The fitted values are
\hat{\pi}_{i}=\pi(x_{i},\hat{\beta})
in the logistic model, which have mean
\bar{y}
with the intercept included in the model. Analogously, we can define
R^{2}
in the logistic model as

\displaystyle R^{2}_{\text{model}}=\frac{\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{m}}}{\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{t}}},\quad R^{2}_{\text{residual}}=1-\frac{\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{r}}}{\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{t}}},\quad R^{2}_{\text{correlation}}=\hat{\rho}^{2}_{y\hat{\pi}}=\frac{C_{y\hat{\pi}}^{2}}{\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{t}}\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{m}}},

where

\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{t}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2},\quad\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{m}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{\pi}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2},\quad\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{r}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{\pi}_{i})^{2},\quad C_{y\hat{\pi}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})(\hat{\pi}_{i}-\bar{y}).

These three definitions are not equivalent in general. In particular,
R^{2}_{\text{model}}
differs from
R^{2}_{\text{residual}}
since

\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{t}}=\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{m}}+\textsc{ss}_{\textsc{r}}+2C_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\pi}}

where

C_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\pi}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{\pi}_{i})(\hat{\pi}_{i}-\bar{y}).

1.

Prove that
R^{2}_{\text{model}}\geq 0,R^{2}_{\text{correlation}}\geq 0
with equality holding if
\hat{\pi}_{i}=\bar{y}
for all
i
. Prove that
R^{2}_{\text{model}}\leq 1,R^{2}_{\text{residual}}\leq 1,R^{2}_{\text{correlation}}\leq 1
with equality holding if
y_{i}=\hat{\pi}_{i}
for all
i
.

Note that
R^{2}_{\text{residual}}
may be negative. Give an example.

2.

Define

\bar{\hat{\pi}}_{1}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\hat{\pi}_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}},\quad\bar{\hat{\pi}}_{0}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-y_{i})\hat{\pi}_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-y_{i})}

as the average of the fitted values for units with
y_{i}=1
and
y_{i}=0
, respectively. Define

D=\bar{\hat{\pi}}_{1}-\bar{\hat{\pi}}_{0}.

Prove that

D=(R^{2}_{\text{model}}+R^{2}_{\text{residual}})/2=\sqrt{R^{2}_{\text{model}}R^{2}_{\text{correlation}}}.

Further prove that
D\geq 0
with equality holding if
\hat{\pi}_{i}=\bar{y}
for all
i
, and
D\leq 1
with equality holding if
y_{i}=\hat{\pi}_{i}
for all
i
.

3.

McFadden, 1974
defined the following
R^{2}
:

R^{2}_{\text{mcfadden}}=1-\frac{\log L(\hat{\beta})}{\log L(\tilde{\beta})}

recalling that

\tilde{\beta}
is the MLE assuming that all coefficients except the intercept are zero, and
\hat{\beta}
is the MLE without any restrictions.
This
R^{2}
must lie within
[0,1]
. Verify that under the Normal linear model, the above formula does not reduce to the usual
R^{2}
.

4.

Cox and Snell, 1989
defined the following
R^{2}
:

R^{2}_{\text{CS}}=1-\left(\frac{L(\tilde{\beta})}{L(\hat{\beta})}\right)^{2/n}.

Verify that under the Normal linear model, the above formula reduces to the usual
R^{2}
.

Remark:
Tjur, 2009
gave an excellent discussion of
R^{2}_{\text{model}},R^{2}_{\text{residual}},R^{2}_{\text{correlation}}
and
D
.
Nagelkerke, 1991
pointed out that the upper bound of this
R^{2}_{\text{CS}}
is
1-(L(\tilde{\beta}))^{2/n}<1
and proposed to modify it as

R^{2}_{\text{nagelkerke}}=\frac{1-\left(\frac{L(\tilde{\beta})}{L(\hat{\beta})}\right)^{2/n}}{1-\left(L(\tilde{\beta})\right)^{2/n}}

to ensure that its upper bound is
1
.
However, this modification seems purely ad hoc.
Although
D
is an appealing definition of
R^{2}
for the logistic model, it does not generalize to other models.
Overall, I feel that
R^{2}_{\text{correlation}}
is a better definition that easily generalizes to other models.

Zhang, 2017
defined an
R^{2}
based on the variance function of the outcome for generalized linear models including the binary logistic model.

Hu et al., 2006
studied the asymptotic properties of some of the
R^{2}
s above.

Chapter 21
Logistic Regressions for Categorical Outcomes

\chaptermark

Multinomial and Proportional Odds Model

Categorical outcomes are common in empirical research. The first type of categorical outcome is nominal. For example, the outcome denotes the preference for fruits (apple, orange, and pear) or transportation services (Uber, Lyft, or BART). The second type of categorical outcome is ordinal. For example, the outcome denotes the course evaluation at Berkeley (1, 2, …, 7) or Amazon review (1 to 5 stars).
This chapter discusses statistical modeling strategies for categorical outcomes, including two classes of models corresponding to the nominal and ordinal outcomes, respectively.

99
Multinomial distribution

A categorical random variable
y
taking values in
\left\{1,\ldots,K\right\}

with probabilities
\textup{pr}(y=k)=\pi_{k}
(
k=1,\ldots,K
) is
often called a multinomial distribution, denoted by

y\sim\text{Multinomial}\left\{1;(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{K})\right\},

(130)

where
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}=1.

We can calculate the mean and covariance matrix of
y
:

Proposition 21.1

If
y
is the Multinomial
random variable in (
130
), then
(1(y=1),\ldots,1(y=K-1))

has mean
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{K-1})
and covariance matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\pi_{1}(1-\pi_{1})&-\pi_{1}\pi_{2}&\cdots&-\pi_{1}\pi_{K-1}\\
-\pi_{1}\pi_{2}&\pi_{2}(1-\pi_{2})&\cdots&-\pi_{2}\pi_{K-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-\pi_{1}\pi_{K-1}&-\pi_{2}\pi_{K-1}&\cdots&\pi_{K-1}(1-\pi_{K-1})\end{array}\right).

(131)

As a byproduct, we know that the matrix in (
131
)
is positive semi-definite.

Proof of Proposition
21.1
:

Without loss of generality, I will calculate the
(1,1)
th and the
(1,2)
th element of the matrix. First,
1(y=1)
is Bernoulli with probability
\pi_{1}
, so the
(1,1)
th element equals var
(1(y=1))=\pi_{1}(1-\pi_{1})
. Similarly, the
(2,2)
th element equals var
(1(y=2))=\pi_{2}(1-\pi_{2})
.

Second,
1(y=1)+1(y=2)
is Bernoulli with probability
\pi_{1}+\pi_{2}
, so var
(1(y=1)+1(y=2))=(\pi_{1}+\pi_{2})(1-\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})
. Therefore, the
(1,2)
-th element equals

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(1(y=1),1(y=2))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\textup{var}(1(y=1)+1(y=2))-\textup{var}(1(y=1))-\textup{var}(1(y=2))\right\}/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi_{1}\pi_{2}.

\square

With independent samples of
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, we want to
model
y_{i}
based on covariates
x_{i}
27
27

27

An alternative strategy is to model
1(y_{i}=k)\mid x_{i}
for each
k
. The advantage of this strategy is that it reduces to binary logistic models. The disadvantage of this strategy is that it does not model the whole distribution of
y_{i}
and can lose efficiency in estimation.
:

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\text{Multinomial}\left[1;\left\{\pi_{1}(x_{i}),\ldots,\pi_{K}(x_{i})\right\}\right],

where
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}(x_{i})=1
for all
x_{i}.

We can write the probability mass function of
\textup{pr}(y_{i}\mid x_{i})
as

\displaystyle\pi_{y_{i}}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\pi_{k}(x_{i})\text{ if }y_{i}=k\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\pi_{k}(x_{i})\right\}^{1(y_{i}=k)}.

Here
\pi_{k}(x_{i})
is a general function of
x_{i}
. The remaining parts of this chapter will discuss the canonical choices of
\pi_{k}(x_{i})
for nominal and ordinal outcomes.

100
Multinomial logistic model for nominal outcomes

100.1
Modeling

Viewing category
K
as the reference level, we can model the ratio of
the probabilities of categories
k
and
K
as

\log\frac{\pi_{k}(x_{i})}{\pi_{K}(x_{i})}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}\qquad(k=1,\ldots,K-1)

which implies that

\pi_{k}(x_{i})=\pi_{K}(x_{i})e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}\qquad(k=1,\ldots,K-1).

Due to the normalization, we have

\begin{array}[]{cclr}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}(x_{i})=1&\Longrightarrow&\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{K}(x_{i})e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}=1\\
&\Longrightarrow&\pi_{K}(x_{i})\sum_{k=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}=1\\
&\Longrightarrow&\pi_{K}(x_{i})=1\Big/\sum_{k=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}\\
&\Longrightarrow&\pi_{k}(x_{i})=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}&(k=1,\ldots,K-1).\end{array}

A more compact form is

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(x_{i})=\pi_{k}(x_{i},\beta)=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}},\qquad(k=1,\ldots,K)

(132)

where
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{K-1})
denotes the parameter
with
\beta_{K}=0
for the reference category. From the ratio form of (
132
), we can only identify
\beta_{k}-\beta_{K}
for all
k=1,\ldots,K
. So for convenience, we impose the restriction
\beta_{K}=0
.
Model (
132
) is called the multinomial logistic regression model.

Similar to the binary logistic regression model, we can interpret the coefficients as the conditional log odds ratio compared to the reference level:

\displaystyle\beta_{k,j}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\pi_{k}(\ldots,x_{ij}+1,\ldots)}{\pi_{K}(\ldots,x_{ij}+1,\ldots)}-\log\frac{\pi_{k}(\ldots,x_{ij},\ldots)}{\pi_{K}(\ldots,x_{ij},\ldots)}

\displaystyle=\log\left\{\frac{\pi_{k}(\ldots,x_{ij}+1,\ldots)}{\pi_{K}(\ldots,x_{ij}+1,\ldots)}\Big/\frac{\pi_{k}(\ldots,x_{ij},\ldots)}{\pi_{K}(\ldots,x_{ij},\ldots)}\right\}.

100.2
MLE

The likelihood function for the multinomial logistic model is

\displaystyle L(\beta)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\pi_{k}(x_{i})\right\}^{1(y_{i}=k)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}\right\}^{1(y_{i}=k)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[\left\{\prod_{k=1}^{K}e^{1(y_{i}=k)x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}\right\}\Big/\sum_{k=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}\right],

and
the log-likelihood function is

\log L(\beta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}1(y_{i}=k)x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}-\log\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}\right\}\right].

The score function is

\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{K-1}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{p(K-1)}

with

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{x_{i}1(y_{i}=k)-\frac{x_{i}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left\{1(y_{i}=k)-\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left\{1(y_{i}=k)-\pi_{k}(x_{i},\beta)\right\}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\quad(k=1,\ldots,K-1).

The Hessian matrix is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{1}\partial\beta_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}&\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{1}\partial\beta_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{1}\partial\beta_{K-1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{2}\partial\beta_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}&\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{2}\partial\beta_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{2}\partial\beta_{K-1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{K-1}\partial\beta_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}&\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{K-1}\partial\beta_{2}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{K-1}\partial\beta_{K-1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{p(K-1)\times p(K-1)}

(133)

with the
(k,k)
th block

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{k}\partial\beta_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\left(\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{(\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}})^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{k}(x_{i},\beta)\left\{1-\pi_{k}(x_{i},\beta)\right\}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}\quad(k=1,\ldots,K-1)

and the
(k,l)
th block

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta_{k}\partial\beta_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\left(\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\frac{-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}{(\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{k}(x_{i},\beta)\pi_{l}(x_{i},\beta)x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}\quad(k\neq l:k,l=1,\ldots,K-1).

We can verify that the Hessian matrix is negative semi-definite based
on Proposition
21.1
, which
is left as Problem
21.2
.

In
R
, the function
multinom
in the
nnet
package uses Newton’s method to fit the multinomial logistic model. We can make inference about the parameters based on the asymptotic Normality of the MLE. Based on a new observation with covariate
x_{n+1}
, we can make prediction based on the fitted probabilities
\pi_{k}(x_{n+1},\hat{\beta})
, and furthermore classify it into
K
categories based on

\hat{y}_{n+1}=\arg\max_{1\leq k\leq K}\pi_{k}(x_{n+1},\hat{\beta}).

101
A latent variable representation for the multinomial logistic regression

We can view the multinomial logistic regression (
132
) as an extension of the binary logistic regression model. The binary logistic regression has a latent variable representation as shown in Section
97.1
. The multinomial logistic regression also has a latent variable representation below.

Assume

\begin{cases}U_{i1}&=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{1}+\varepsilon_{i1},\\
\vdots\\
U_{iK}&=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{K}+\varepsilon_{iK},\end{cases}

where
\varepsilon_{i1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{iK}
are IID standard Gumbel random variables
28
28

28

See Section
29.A.3
for a review.
.
Using the language of economics,
(U_{i1},\ldots,U_{iK})
are the utilities associated with the choices
(1,\ldots,K)
. So unit
i
chooses
k
if
k
has the highest utility:

y_{i}=k\quad\text{ if }U_{ik}>U_{il}\text{ for all }l\neq k.

We can show that this latent variable model implies (
132
). This follows from the lemma below, which is due to
McFadden, 1974
29
29

29

Daniel McFadden shared the 2000 Nobel Memorial Prize in Economic Sciences with James Heckman.

. When
K=2
, it also gives another latent variable representation for the binary logistic regression, which is different from the one in Section
97.1
.

Lemma 21.1

Assume

\begin{cases}U_{1}&=V_{1}+\varepsilon_{1},\\
\vdots\\
U_{K}&=V_{K}+\varepsilon_{K},\end{cases}

where
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{K}
are IID standard Gumbel.
Define

y=\arg\max_{1\leq l\leq K}U_{l}

as the index corresponding to the maximum of the
U_{k}
’s.
We have

\textup{pr}(y=k)=\frac{e^{V_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{V_{l}}}.

Proof of Lemma
21.1
:

Recall that the standard Gumbel random variable has
CDF

F(z)=\exp(-e^{-z})

and density

f(z)=\exp(-e^{-z})e^{-z}.

The event “
y=k
” is equivalent to the event “
U_{k}>U_{l}

for all
l\neq k
”, so

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y=k)=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(U_{k}>U_{l},l=1,\ldots,k-1,k+1,\ldots,K)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(V_{k}+\varepsilon_{k}>V_{l}+\varepsilon_{l},l=1,\ldots,k-1,k+1,\ldots,K)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textup{pr}(V_{k}+z>V_{l}+\varepsilon_{l},l=1,\ldots,k-1,k+1,\ldots,K)f(z)\textup{d}z

where the last line follows from conditioning on
\varepsilon_{k}
. By independence of the
\varepsilon
’s, we have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y=k)=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\prod_{l\neq k}\textup{pr}(\varepsilon_{l}<V_{k}-V_{l}+z)f(z)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\prod_{l\neq k}\exp(-e^{-V_{k}+V_{l}-z})\exp(-e^{-z})e^{-z}\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sum_{l\neq k}e^{-V_{k}+V_{l}}e^{-z}\right)\exp(-e^{-z})e^{-z}\textup{d}z.

Changing of variables
t=e^{-z}
with
\textup{d}z=-1/t\textup{d}t
, we obtain

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y=k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\sum_{l\neq k}e^{-V_{k}+V_{l}}\right)\exp(-t)\textup{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(-tC_{k})\textup{d}t

where

C_{k}=1+\sum_{l\neq k}e^{-V_{k}+V_{l}}.

The integral simplifies to
1/C_{k}
due to the density of the exponential
distribution. Therefore,

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y=k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\sum_{l\neq k}e^{-V_{k}+V_{l}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{V_{k}}}{e^{V_{k}}+\sum_{l\neq k}e^{V_{l}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{V_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{V_{l}}}.

\square

This lemma is remarkable. It extends to more complex utility functions. I will use it again in Section
104
.

102
Proportional odds model for ordinal outcomes

For ordinal outcomes, we can still use the multinomial logistic model,
but by doing this, we discard the ordering information in the outcome.
Consider a simple case with a scalar
x_{i}
, the multinomial logistic model does not rule out the possibility that
\beta_{k}<0
and
\beta_{k+1}>0
, which implies that
x_{i}
increases the probability of category
k+1
but decreases the probability of category
k
. However, the outcome must first reach level
k
and then increase to level
k+1
. If this happens, it may be hard to interpret the model.

Motivated
by the latent linear representation of the binary logistic model,
we imagine that the ordinal outcome arises from discretizing a continuous
latent variable:

\displaystyle y_{i}^{*}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon_{i},\quad\textup{pr}(\varepsilon_{i}\leq z\mid x_{i})=g(z)

(134)

and

\displaystyle y_{i}=k,\quad\text{ if }\alpha_{k-1}<y_{i}^{*}\leq\alpha_{k},\quad(k=1,\ldots,K)

(135)

where

-\infty=\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{K-1}<\alpha_{K}=\infty.

Figure
41
illustrates the data generating process with
K=4.

Figure 41:
Latent variable representation of the ordinal outcome

The unknown parameters are
(\beta,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K-1})
.
The distribution of the latent error term
g(\cdot)
is known, for
example, it can be logistic or Normal. The former results in the proportional
odds logistic model, and the latter results in the ordered Probit
model. Based on the latent linear model, we can compute

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{i}\leq k\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=\textup{pr}(y_{i}^{*}\leq\alpha_{k}\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=\textup{pr}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon_{i}\leq\alpha_{k}\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=\textup{pr}(\varepsilon_{i}\leq\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta\mid x_{i})

\displaystyle=g(\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta).

I will focus on the proportional odds logistic model in the main text and defer the details for the ordered Probit model to Problem
21.5
.
With this model, we have

\textup{pr}(y_{i}\leq k\mid x_{i})=\frac{e^{\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}

or

\text{logit}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}\leq k\mid x_{i})\}=\log\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}\leq k\mid x_{i})}{\textup{pr}(y_{i}>k\mid x_{i})}=\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta.

(136)

The model has the “proportional odds” property because

\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}\leq k\mid\ldots,x_{ij}+1,\ldots)}{\textup{pr}(y_{i}>k\mid\ldots,x_{ij}+1,\ldots)}\Big/\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}\leq k\mid\ldots,x_{ij},\ldots)}{\textup{pr}(y_{i}>k\mid\ldots,x_{ij},\ldots)}=e^{-\beta_{j}}

which is a positive constant not depending on
k
.

The sign of
x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
is negative due to the latent variable
representation. Some textbooks and software packages use a positive
sign, but the function
polr
in package
MASS
of
R
uses (
136
).

The proportional odds model implies a quite complicated form
of the probability for each category:

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=k\mid x_{i})=\frac{e^{\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}-\frac{e^{\alpha_{k-1}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\alpha_{k-1}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}},\quad(k=1,\ldots,K).

So the likelihood function is

\displaystyle L(\beta,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=k\mid x_{i})\}^{1(y_{i}=k)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left(\frac{e^{\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\alpha_{k}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}-\frac{e^{\alpha_{k-1}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{\alpha_{k-1}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}\right)^{1(y_{i}=k)}.

The log-likelihood function is concave
(
Pratt, 1981
;
Burridge, 1981
)
, and it is strictly concave in most cases. The function
polr
in the
R
package
MASS
computes the MLE of the proportional odds model using the BFGS algorithm. It uses the explicit formulas of the gradient of the log-likelihood function, and computes the Hessian matrix numerically. I relegate the formulas of the gradient as a homework problem. For more details of the Hessian matrix, see
Agresti, 2010
, which is a textbook discussion on modeling ordinal data.

103
A case study

I use a small observational dataset from the Karolinska Institute in Stockholm, Sweden to illustrate the application of logistic regressions.
Rubin, 2008
used this dataset to investigate whether it is better for cardia cancer patients to be treated in a large or small-volume hospital, where volume is defined by the number of patients with cardia cancer treated in recent years. I use the following variables:
highdiag
indicating whether a patient was diagnosed at a high volume hospital,
hightreat
indicating whether a patient was treated at a high volume hospital,
age
representing the age,
rural
indicating whether the patient was from a rural area, and
survival
representing the years of survival after diagnosis with three categories (“1”, “2-4”, “5+”). The
R
code is in
code20
.5.
R
.

103.4
Proportional odds logistic for the outcome

The multinomial logisitic model does not reflect the ordering information of the outcome. For instance, in the regression with
highdiag
, the coefficient for level “2-4” is
-0.06973187<0
, but the coefficient for level “5+” is
0.64036289>0
, which means that
highdiag
decreases the probability of “2-4” but increases the probability of “5”. However, this is illogical since a patient must first live longer than two years and then live longer than five years. Nevertheless, it is not a severe problem in this case study because those coefficients are not significant.

104
Discrete choice models

104.1
Model

The covariates in model (
132
) depend only on individuals.

McFadden, 1974
extends it to allow for choice-specific covariates
z_{ik}
. His formulation is based on the latent utility representation:

\begin{cases}U_{i1}&=z_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta+\varepsilon_{i1},\\
\vdots\\
U_{iK}&=z_{iK}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta+\varepsilon_{iK},\end{cases}

where
\varepsilon_{i1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{iK}
are IID standard Gumbel. Unit
i
chooses
k
if
k
has the highest utility. Lemma
21.1
implies that

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(z_{i})=\pi_{k}(z_{i},\theta)=\frac{e^{z_{ik}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}},\qquad(k=1,\ldots,K).

(137)

Model (
137
) seems rather similar to model (
132
). However, there are many subtle differences. First, a component of
z_{ik}
may vary only with choice
k
, for example, it can represent the price of choice
k
. Partition
z_{ik}
into three types of covariates:
x_{i}
that only vary cross individuals,
c_{k}
that only vary across choices, and
w_{ik}
that vary across both individuals and choices. Model (
137
) reduces to

\pi_{k}(z_{i})=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{x}+c_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{c}+w_{ik}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{w}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{x}+c_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{c}+w_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{w}}}=\frac{e^{c_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{c}+w_{ik}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{w}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{c_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{c}+w_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta_{w}}},

that is, the individual-specific covariates drop out. Therefore,
z_{ik}
in model (
137
) does not contain covariates that vary only with individuals. In particular,
z_{ik}
in model (
137
) does not contain the constant, but in contrast, the
x_{i}
in model (
132
) ususally contains the intercept by default.

Second, if we want to use individual-specific covariates in the model, they must have choice-specific coefficients. So a more general model unifying (
132
) and (
137
) is

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(x_{i},w_{ik},\theta,\beta)=\frac{e^{w_{ik}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{w_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}},\qquad(k=1,\ldots,K).

(138)

Equivalently, we can create pseudo covariates
z_{ik}
as the original
w_{ik}
together with interaction of
x_{i}
and the dummy for choice
k
. For example, if
K=3
and
x_{i}
contain the intercept and a scalar individual-specific covariate, then
(z_{i1},z_{i2},z_{i3})
are

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}z_{i1}\\
z_{i2}\\
z_{i3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}w_{i1}&1&0&x_{i}&0\\
w_{i2}&0&1&0&x_{i}\\
w_{i3}&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

where
K=3
is the reference level.
So with augmented covariates, the discrete choice model (
137
) is strictly more general than the multinomial logistic model (
132
). In the special case with
K=2
, model (
137
) reduces to

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}

where
x_{i}=z_{i1}-z_{i2}
.

Based on the model specification (
137
), the log likelihood function is

\log L(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}1(y_{i}=k)\left(z_{ik}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta-\log\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}\right).

So the score function is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\log L(\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}1(y_{i}=k)\left(z_{ik}-\frac{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}z_{il}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}1(y_{i}=k)\{z_{ik}-E(z_{ik};\theta)\},

where
E(\cdot;\theta)
is the average value of
\{z_{i1},\ldots,z_{iK}\}
over the probability mass function

p_{k}(\theta)=e^{z_{ik}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}/\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}.

The Hessian matrix is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta\partial\theta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\log L(\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}1(y_{i}=k)\frac{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}z_{il}z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}-\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}z_{il}\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta}z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}{(\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{z_{il}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}1(y_{i}=k)\textup{cov}(z_{ik};\theta),

where
\textup{cov}(\cdot;\theta)
is the covariance matrix of
\{z_{i1},\ldots,z_{iK}\}
over the probability mass function defined above.
From these formulas, we can easily compute the MLE using Newton’s method and obtain its asymptotic distribution based on the inverse of the Fisher information matrix.

104.3
More comments

The assumption of Gumbel error terms is very strong. However, relaxing this assumption leads to much more complicated forms of the conditional probabilities of the outcome. The model (
137
) implies that

\frac{\pi_{k}(z_{i})}{\pi_{l}(z_{i})}=\exp\{(z_{ik}-z_{il})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\theta\},

so the choice between
k
and
l
does not depend on the existence of other choices. This is called the
independence of irrelevant alternatives (IIA) assumption. This is often a plausible assumption. However, it may be violated. For example, with the apple and orange, someone chooses the apple; but with the apple, orange, and banana, s/he may choose the orange.

The model (
137
) is the basic form of the discrete choice model.
Train, 2009
is a monograph on this topic which provides many extensions.

105
Homework problems

21.1
Inverse model for the multinomial logit model

The following result extends Theorem
20.2
.

Assume that

\displaystyle y_{i}\sim\text{Multinomial}(1;q_{1},\ldots,q_{K}),

(139)

and

\displaystyle x_{i}\mid y_{i}=k\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{k},\Sigma),

(140)

where
x_{i}
does not contain
1
.
We can verify that
y_{i}\mid x_{i}
follows a multinomial logit model as shown in the theorem below.

Theorem 21.1

Under (
139
) and (
140
), we have

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=k\mid x_{i})=\frac{e^{\alpha_{k}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{\alpha_{l}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}},

where

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}=\log q_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{k},\quad\beta_{k}=\Sigma^{-1}\mu_{k}.

Prove Theorem
21.1
.

21.2
Hessian matrix in the multinomial logit model

Prove that the Hessian matrix (
133
) of the log-likelihood function of the multinomial logit model is negative semi-definite.

Hint: Use Proposition
21.1
.

21.3
Iteratively reweighted least squares algorithm for the multinomial logit model

Similar to the binary logistic model, Newton’s method for computing the MLE for the multinomial logit model can be written as iteratively reweighted least squares. Give the details.

21.4
Score function of the proportional odds model

Derive the explicit formulas of the score function of the proportional odds model.

21.5
Ordered Probit regression

If we choose
\varepsilon_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\textsc{N}(0,1)
in (
134
), then the corresponding model is called the ordered Probit regression. Write down the likelihood function and derive the score function for this model.

Remark: You can use the function
polr
in
R
to fit this model with the specification
method

=

"probit"
.

21.6
Case-control study and multinomial logistic model

This problem extends Theorem
20.1
.

Assume that

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=k\mid x_{i})=\frac{e^{\alpha_{k}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{\alpha_{l}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}

and

\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid y_{i}=k,x_{i})=\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid y_{i}=k)=p_{k}

for
k=1,\ldots,K
.
Show that

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=k\mid x_{i},s_{i}=1)=\frac{e^{\tilde{\alpha}_{k}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{k}}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}e^{\tilde{\alpha}_{l}+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta_{l}}}

with
\tilde{\alpha}_{k}=\alpha_{k}+\log p_{k}
for
k=1,\ldots,K
.

Chapter 22
Regression Models for Count Outcomes

A random variable for counts can take values in
\left\{0,1,2,\ldots\right\}
. This type of variable is common in applied statistics. For example, it can represent how many times you visit the gym every week, how many lectures you have missed in the linear model course, how many traffic accidents happened in certain areas during certain periods, etc. This chapter focuses on statistical modeling of those outcomes given covariates.
Hilbe, 2014
is a textbook focusing on count outcome regressions.

106
Some random variables for counts

I first review four canonical choices of random variables for modeling count data.

106.1
Poisson

A random variable
y
is Poisson
(\lambda)
if its probability mass
function is

\textup{pr}(y=k)=e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!},\quad(k=0,1,2,\ldots)

which sums to
1
by the Taylor expansion formula
e^{\lambda}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}
.
The Poisson
(\lambda)
random variable has the following properties:

Proposition 22.1

If
y\sim\textup{Poisson}(\lambda)
, then

E(y)=\textup{var}(y)=\lambda.

Proposition 22.2

If
y_{1},\ldots,y_{K}
are mutually independent with
y_{k}\sim\textup{Poisson}(\lambda_{k})
for
k=1,\ldots,K
,
then

y_{1}+\cdots+y_{K}\sim\textup{Poisson}(\lambda),

and

(y_{1},\ldots,y_{K})\mid y_{1}+\cdots+y_{K}=n\sim\textup{Multinomial}\left(n,(\lambda_{1}/\lambda,\ldots,\lambda_{K}/\lambda)\right),

where
\lambda=\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{K}
.

Conversely, if
S\sim\textup{Poisson}(\lambda)
with
\lambda=\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{K}
, and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{K})\mid S=n\sim\textup{Multinomial}\left(n,(\lambda_{1}/\lambda,\ldots,\lambda_{K}/\lambda)\right)
, then
y_{1},\ldots,y_{K}
are mutually independent with
y_{k}\sim\textup{Poisson}(\lambda_{k})
for
k=1,\ldots,K
.

Where does the Poisson random variable come from? One way to generate Poisson is through independent Bernoulli random variables. I will review
Le Cam, 1960
’s theorem below without giving a proof.

Theorem 22.1

Suppose
X_{i}
’s are independent Bernoulli random variables with probabilities
p_{i}
’s
(i=1,\ldots,n)
. Define
\lambda_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}
and
S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
. Then

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big|\textup{pr}(S_{n}=k)-e^{-\lambda_{n}}\frac{\lambda_{n}^{k}}{k!}\Big|\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{2}.

As a special case, if
p_{i}=\lambda/n
, then Theorem
22.1
implies

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big|\textup{pr}(S_{n}=k)-e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\Big|\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\lambda/n)^{2}=\lambda^{2}/n\rightarrow 0.

So the sum of IID Bernoulli random variables is approximately Poisson if the probability has order
1/n.
This is called the law of rare events, or Poisson limit theorem, or Le Cam’s theorem. By Theorem
22.1
, we can use Poisson as a model for the sum of many rare events.

106.2
Negative-Binomial

The Poisson distribution restricts that the mean must be the same
as the variance. It cannot capture the feature of overdispersed data
with variance larger than the mean. The Negative-Binomial is an extension
of the Poisson that allows for overdispersion. Here the definition of the Negative-Binomial below is different from its standard definition, but it is more natural as an extension of the Poisson
30
30

30

With IID Bernoulli
(p)
trials, the Negative-Binomial distribution,
denoted by
y\sim\text{NB}^{\prime}(r,p)
, is the number of success before
the
r
th failure.
Its probability mass function is

\textup{pr}(y=k)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}k+r-1\\
k\end{array}\right)(1-p)^{r}p^{k},\quad(k=0,1,2,\ldots)

If
p=\mu/(\mu+\theta)
and
r=\theta
then these two definitions
coincide.
This definition is more restrictive because
r
must
be an integer.
. Define
y
as the Negative-Binomial random
variable, denoted by
\text{NB}(\mu,\theta)
with
\mu>0
and
\theta>0
,
if

\begin{cases}y\mid\lambda&\sim\text{Poisson}(\lambda),\\
\lambda&\sim\text{Gamma}(\theta,\theta/\mu).\end{cases}

(141)

So the Negative-Binomial is the Poisson with a random Gamma intensity, that is, the Negative-Binomial is a scale mixture of the Poisson. If
\theta\rightarrow\infty
, then
\lambda
is a point mass at
\mu
and the Negative-Binomial reduces to Poisson
(\mu)
. We can verify that it has the following probability mass function.

Proposition 22.3

The Negative-Binomial random
variable defined in (
141
) has the probability
mass function

\textup{pr}(y=k)=\frac{\Gamma(k+\theta)}{\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(\theta)}\left(\frac{\theta}{\mu+\theta}\right)^{\theta}\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu+\theta}\right)^{k},\quad(k=0,1,2,\ldots).

Proof of Proposition
22.3
:

We have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y=k)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{pr}(y=k\mid\lambda)f(\lambda)\text{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\frac{(\theta/\mu)^{\theta}}{\Gamma(\theta)}\lambda^{\theta-1}e^{-(\theta/\mu)\lambda}\text{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{(\theta/\mu)^{\theta}}{k!\Gamma(\theta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{k+\theta-1}e^{-(1+\theta/\mu)\lambda}\text{d}\lambda.

The function in the integral is the density function of Gamma
(k+\theta,1+\theta/\mu)

without the normalizing constant

\frac{(1+\theta/\mu)^{k+\theta}}{\Gamma(k+\theta)}.

So

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y=k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\theta/\mu)^{\theta}}{k!\Gamma(\theta)}\Big/\frac{(1+\theta/\mu)^{k+\theta}}{\Gamma(k+\theta)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(k+\theta)}{k!\Gamma(\theta)}\left(\frac{\theta}{\mu+\theta}\right)^{\theta}\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu+\theta}\right)^{k}.

\square

We can derive the mean and variance of the Negative-Binomial.

Proposition 22.4

The Negative-Binomial random
variable defined in (
141
) has moments

\displaystyle E(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu,

\displaystyle\textup{var}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\theta}>E(y).

Proof of Proposition
22.4
:

Recall Proposition
29.2
that a Gamma
(\alpha,\beta)
random variable has mean
\alpha/\beta
and variance
\alpha/\beta^{2}
.
We have

E(y)=E\left\{E(y\mid\lambda)\right\}=E(\lambda)=\frac{\theta}{\theta/\mu}=\mu,

and

\textup{var}(y)=E\left\{\textup{var}(y\mid\lambda)\right\}+\textup{var}\left\{E(y\mid\lambda)\right\}=E(\lambda)+\textup{var}(\lambda)=\frac{\theta}{\theta/\mu}+\frac{\theta}{(\theta/\mu)^{2}}=\mu+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\theta}.

\square

So the dispersion parameter
\theta
controls the variance of the Negative-Binomial. With the same mean, the Negative-Binomial has a larger variance than Poisson. Figure
42
further compares the log probability mass functions of the Negative Binomial and Poisson. It shows that the Negative Binomial has a slightly higher probability at zero but much heavier tails than the Poisson.

Figure 42:
Comparing the log probabilities of Poisson and Negative-Binomial with the same mean

106.3
Zero-inflated count distributions

Many count distributions have larger masses at zero compared to Poisson and Negative-Binomial. Therefore, it is also important to have more general distributions capturing this feature of empirical data. We can simply add an additional zero component to the Poisson or the Negative-Binomial.

A zero-inflated Poisson random variable
y
is a mixture of two components:
a point mass at zero and a Poisson
(\lambda)
random variable, with
probabilities
p
and
1-p
, respectively. So
y
has the probability
mass function

\textup{pr}(y=k)=\begin{cases}p+(1-p)e^{-\lambda},&\text{if }k=0,\\
(1-p)e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!},&\text{if }k=1,2,\ldots.\end{cases}

and moments below:

Proposition 22.5

E(y)=(1-p)\lambda
and
\textup{var}(y)=(1-p)\lambda(1+p\lambda).

A zero-inflated Negative-Binomial random variable
y
is a mixture
of two components: a point mass at zero and a NB
(\mu,\theta)
random
variable, with probabilities
p
and
1-p
, respectively. So
y

has probability mass function

\textup{pr}(y=k)=\begin{cases}p+(1-p)\left(\frac{\theta}{\mu+\theta}\right)^{\theta},&\text{if }k=0,\\
(1-p)\frac{\Gamma(k+\theta)}{\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(\theta)}\left(\frac{\theta}{\mu+\theta}\right)^{\theta}\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu+\theta}\right)^{k},&\text{if }k=1,2,\ldots.\end{cases}

and moments below:

Proposition 22.6

E(y)=(1-p)\mu
and
\textup{var}(y)=(1-p)\mu(1+\mu/\theta+p\mu).

I leave the proofs of Propositions
22.5
and
22.6
as Problem
22.2
.

107
Regression models for counts

To model a count outcome
y_{i}
given
x_{i}
, we can still use
OLS. However, a problem with OLS is that the predicted value can be negative.
This can be easily fixed by running OLS of
\log(y_{i}+1)
given

x_{i}
. However, this still does not reflect the fact that
y_{i}

is a count outcome. For example, these two OLS fits cannot easily make
a prediction for the probabilities
\textup{pr}(y_{i}\geq 1\mid x_{i})
or

\textup{pr}(y_{i}>3\mid x_{i})
. A more direct approach is to model the
conditional distribution of
y_{i}
given
x_{i}
using the distributions
reviewed in Section
106
.

107.1
Poisson regression

Poisson regression assumes

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{i}\mid x_{i}&\sim\text{Poisson}(\lambda_{i}),\\
\lambda_{i}&=\lambda(x_{i},\beta)=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}.\end{cases}

So the mean and variance of
y_{i}\mid x_{i}
are

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=\textup{var}(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}.

Because

\log E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta,

this model is sometimes called the log-linear model, with the coefficient

\beta_{j}
interpreted as the conditional log mean ratio:

\log\frac{E(y_{i}\mid\ldots,x_{ij}+1,\ldots)}{E(y_{i}\mid\ldots,x_{ij},\ldots)}=\beta_{j}.

The likelihood function for independent Poisson random variables is

L(\beta)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\lambda_{i}}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{y_{i}}}{y_{i}!}\propto\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\lambda_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{y_{i}},

and omitting the constants, we can write the log-likelihood function
as

\displaystyle\log L(\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\lambda_{i}+y_{i}\log\lambda_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}+y_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta\right).

The score function is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-x_{i}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}+x_{i}y_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left(y_{i}-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left\{y_{i}-\lambda(x_{i},\beta)\right\},

and the Hessian matrix is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\left(e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

which is negative semi-definite. When the Hessian is negative definite,
the MLE is unique. It must satisfy that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left(y_{i}-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left\{y_{i}-\lambda(x_{i},\hat{\beta})\right\}=0.

We can solve this nonlinear equation using Newton’s method:

\displaystyle\beta^{\text{new}}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\text{old}}-\left\{\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta^{\text{old}})}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right\}^{-1}\frac{\partial\log L(\beta^{\text{old}})}{\partial\beta}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\text{old}}-(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}W^{\text{old}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(Y-\Lambda^{\text{old}}),

where

X=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{array}\right),\quad Y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right)

and

\Lambda^{\text{old}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp(x_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta^{\text{old}})\\
\vdots\\
\exp(x_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta^{\text{old}})\end{array}\right),\quad W^{\text{old}}=\text{diag}\left\{\exp(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta^{\text{old}})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}.

Similar to the derivation for the logit model, we can simplify Newton’s
method to

\beta^{\text{new}}=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}W^{\text{old}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}W^{\text{old}}Z^{\text{old}},

where

Z^{\text{old}}=X\beta^{\text{old}}+(W^{\text{old}})^{-1}(Y-\Lambda^{\text{old}}).

So we have an iterative reweighted least squares algorithm. In
R
, we can use the
glm
function with “
family

=

poisson
(
link

=

"log"
)
” to fit the Poisson regression, which uses Newton’s method.

Statistical inference based on

\hat{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{a}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}\left\{\beta,\left(-\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\hat{\beta})}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right)^{-1}\right\}=\textsc{N}\left\{\beta,(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{W}X)^{-1}\right\},

where
\hat{W}=\text{diag}\left\{\exp(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

After obtaining the MLE, we can predict the mean
E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})
by
\hat{\lambda}_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}
. Because Poisson regression is a fully parametrized model, we can also predict any other probability quantities involving
y_{i}\mid x_{i}
. For example, we can predict
\textup{pr}(y_{i}=0\mid x_{i})
by
e^{-\hat{\lambda}_{i}}
, and
\textup{pr}(y_{i}\geq 3\mid x_{i})
by
1-e^{-\hat{\lambda}_{i}}(1+\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{2}/2)
.

107.2
Negative-Binomial regression

Negative-Binomial regression assumes

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{i}\mid x_{i}&\sim\text{NB}(\mu_{i},\theta),\\
\mu_{i}&=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta},\end{cases}

so it has conditional mean and variance:

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta},\quad\textup{var}(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}(1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}/\theta).

It is also a log-linear model.

The log-likelihood function for Negative-Binomial regression is
\log L(\beta,\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}l_{i}(\beta,\theta)
with

\displaystyle l_{i}(\beta,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\Gamma(y_{i}+\theta)-\log\Gamma(y_{i}+1)-\log\Gamma(\theta)

\displaystyle+\theta\log\left(\frac{\theta}{\mu_{i}+\theta}\right)+y_{i}\log\left(\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\theta}\right),

where
\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}
has partial derivative
\partial\mu_{i}/\partial\beta=\mu_{i}x_{i}
.
We can use Newton’s algorithm or Fisher scoring algorithm to compute the MLE
(\hat{\beta},\hat{\theta})
which requires deriving the first and second derivatives of
\log L(\beta,\theta)
with respect to
(\beta,\theta)
. I will derive some important components and relegate other details to Problem
22.1
. First,

\frac{\partial\log L(\beta,\theta)}{\partial\beta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1+\mu_{i}/\theta)^{-1}(y_{i}-\mu_{i})x_{i}.

The corresponding first-order condition can be viewed as the estimating equation of Poisson regression with weights
(1+\mu_{i}/\theta)^{-1}
. Second,

\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta,\theta)}{\partial\beta\partial\theta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\theta)^{2}}(y_{i}-\mu_{i})x_{i}.

We can verify

E\left\{\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta,\theta)}{\partial\beta\partial\theta}\mid X\right\}=0

since each term inside the summation has conditional expectation zero. This implies that the Fisher information matrix is diagonal, so
\hat{\beta}
and
\hat{\theta}
are asymptotically independent.

The
glm
.
nb
in the
MASS
package iterate between
\beta
and
\theta
: given
\theta
, update
\beta
based on Fisher scoring; given
\beta
, update
\theta
based on Newton’s algorithm. It reports standard errors based on the inverse of the Fisher information matrix.
31
31

31

The command
rnbreg
in
Stata
uses the Berndt–Hall–Hall–Hausman (BHHH) algorithm by default, which may give slightly different numbers compared with
R
. The BHHH algorithm is similar to Newton’s algorithm but avoids calculating the Hessian matrix.

107.3
Zero-inflated regressions

The zero-inflated Poisson regression assumes that

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\begin{cases}0,&\text{with probability }p_{i},\\
\text{Poisson}(\lambda_{i}),&\text{with probability }1-p_{i},\end{cases}

where

p_{i}=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma}}{1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma}},\quad\lambda_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}.

The zero-inflated Negative-Binomial regression assumes that

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\begin{cases}0,&\text{with probability }p_{i},\\
\text{NB}(\mu_{i},\theta),&\text{with probability }1-p_{i},\end{cases}

where

p_{i}=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma}}{1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma}},\quad\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}.

To avoid over-parametrization, we can also restrict some coefficients
to be zero.
The
zeroinfl
function in the
R
package
pscl
can fit the zero-inflated Poisson and Negative-Binomial regressions.
