Homework problems

22.1
Newton’s method for Negative-Binomial regression

Calculate the score function and Hessian matrix based on the log-likelihood function of the Negative-Binomial regression.
What is the joint asymptotic distribution of the MLE
(\hat{\beta},\hat{\theta})
?

22.2
Moments of Zero-inflated Poisson and Negative-Binomial

Prove Propositions
22.5
and
22.6
.

22.3
Overdispersion and zero-inflation

Show that for a zero-inflated Poisson, if
p\leq 1/2
then
E(y)<\textup{var}(y)
always holds. What is the condition for
E(y)<\textup{var}(y)
when
p>1/2
?

22.4
Poisson latent variable and the binary regression model with the cloglog link

Assume that
y_{i}^{*}\mid x_{i}\sim\text{Poisson}(e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta})
,
and define
y_{i}=1(y_{i}^{*}>0)
as the indicator that
y_{i}^{*}
is
not zero. Show that
y_{i}\mid x_{i}
follows a cloglog model, that
is,

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})=g(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),

where
g(z)=1-\exp(-e^{z})
.

Remark: The cloglog model for binary outcome arises naturally from a latent Poisson model. It was only briefly mentioned in Chapter
20
.

22.5
Likelihood for the zero-inflated Poisson regression

Write down the likelihood function for the Zero-inflated Poisson model,
and derive the steps for Newton’s method.

22.6
Likelihood for the Zero-inflated Negative-Binomial regression

Write down the likelihood function for the Zero-inflated Negative-Binomial model, and
derive the steps for Newton’s method.

22.7
Prediction in the Zero-inflated Negative-Binomial regression

After obtaining the MLE
(\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma})
and its asymptotic covariance matrix
\hat{V}
, predict the conditional mean
E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})
, the conditional probability
\textup{pr}(y_{i}=0\mid x_{i})
, and the conditional probability
\textup{pr}(y_{i}\geq 5\mid x_{i})
. What are the associated asymptotic standard errors?

22.8
Data analysis

Zeileis et al., 2008
gives a tutorial on count outcome regressions using the dataset from
Deb and Trivedi, 1997
. Replicate and extend their analysis based on the discussion in this chapter.

22.9
Data analysis

Fisman and Miguel, 2007
was an application of Negative-Binomial regression, and
Albergaria and Fávero, 2017
replicated their study and argued that the zero-inflated Negative-Binomial regression was more appropriate. Replicate and extend their analysis based on the discussion in this chapter.

Chapter 23
Generalized Linear Models: A Unification

\chaptermark

GLM: a Unification

This chapter unifies Chapters
20
–
22
under the formulation of the generalized linear model (GLM) by
Nelder and Wedderburn, 1972
.

110
Generalized Linear Models

So far we have discussed the following models for independent observations

(y_{i},x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
.

Example 23.1

The Normal linear model for continuous outcomes assumes

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{i},\sigma^{2}),\quad\text{with }\mu_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta.

(142)

Example 23.2

The logistic model for binary outcomes assumes

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\textup{Bernoulli}(\mu_{i}),\quad\text{with }\mu_{i}=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}{1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}.

(143)

Example 23.3

The Poisson model for count outcomes assumes

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\textup{Poisson}(\mu_{i}),\quad\text{with }\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}.

(144)

Example 23.4

The Negative-Binomial model for overdispersed count outcomes assumes

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\textsc{NB}(\mu_{i},\delta),\quad\text{with }\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}.

(145)

We use
\delta
for the dispersion parameter to avoid
confusion because
\theta
means something else below (Chapter
22
uses
\theta
for the dispersion parameter).

In the above models,
\mu_{i}
denotes the conditional mean. This chapter
will unify Examples
23.1
–
23.4
as GLMs.

110.1
Exponential family

Consider a general conditional probability density or mass function:

f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\theta_{i},\phi)=\exp\left\{\frac{y_{i}\theta_{i}-b(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)}+c(y_{i},\phi)\right\},

(146)

where
(\theta_{i},\phi)
are unknown parameters, and
\left\{a(\cdot),b(\cdot),c(\cdot,\cdot)\right\}

are known functions. The above conditional density (
146
)
is called the
natural exponential family with dispersion
, where
\theta_{i}

is the natural parameter depending on
x_{i}
, and
\phi
is the
dispersion parameter. Sometimes,
a(\phi)=1
and
c(y_{i},\phi)=c(y_{i})
,
simplifying the conditional density to a
natural exponential family
. Examples
23.1
–
23.4

have a unified structure as (
146
), as detailed below.

Example 23.1
(continued)

Model (
142
) has conditional probability
density function

\displaystyle f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\mu_{i},\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{-1/2}\exp\left\{-\frac{(y_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{\frac{y_{i}\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{i}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^{2})-\frac{y_{i}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right\},

with

\theta_{i}=\mu_{i},\quad b(\theta_{i})=\frac{1}{2}\theta_{i}^{2},

and

\phi=\sigma^{2},\quad a(\phi)=\sigma^{2}=\phi.

Example 23.2
(continued)

Model (
143
) has conditional probability
mass function

\displaystyle f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\mu_{i}^{y_{i}}(1-\mu_{i})^{1-y_{i}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mu_{i}}{1-\mu_{i}}\right)^{y_{i}}(1-\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{y_{i}\log\frac{\mu_{i}}{1-\mu_{i}}-\log\frac{1}{1-\mu_{i}}\right\},

with

\theta_{i}=\log\frac{\mu_{i}}{1-\mu_{i}}\Longleftrightarrow\mu_{i}=\frac{e^{\theta_{i}}}{1+e^{\theta_{i}}},\quad b(\theta_{i})=\log\frac{1}{1-\mu_{i}}=\log(1+e^{\theta_{i}}),

and

a(\phi)=1.

Example 23.3
(continued)

Model (
144
) has conditional probability mass
function

\displaystyle f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=e^{-\mu_{i}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{y_{i}}}{y_{i}!}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{y_{i}\log\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}-\log y_{i}!\right\},

with

\theta_{i}=\log\mu_{i},\quad b(\theta_{i})=\mu_{i}=e^{\theta_{i}},

and

a(\phi)=1.

Example 23.4
(continued)

Model (
145
), for a fixed
\delta
, has conditional
probability mass function

\displaystyle f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(y_{i}+\delta)}{\Gamma(\delta)\Gamma(y_{i}+1)}\left(\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\delta}\right)^{y_{i}}\left(\frac{\delta}{\mu_{i}+\delta}\right)^{\delta}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{y_{i}\log\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\delta}-\delta\log\frac{\mu_{i}+\delta}{\delta}+\log\Gamma(y_{i}+\delta)-\log\Gamma(\delta)-\log\Gamma(y_{i}+1)\right\},

with

\theta_{i}=\log\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\delta}\Longleftrightarrow\frac{\delta}{\mu_{i}+\delta}=1-e^{\theta_{i}},\quad b(\theta_{i})=\delta\log\frac{\mu_{i}+\delta}{\delta}=-\delta\log(1-e^{\theta_{i}}),

and

a(\phi)=1.

The logistic and Poisson models are simpler without the dispersion parameter.
The Normal linear model has a dispersion parameter for the variance.
The Negative-Binomial model is more complex: without fixing
\delta

it does not belong to the exponential family with dispersion.

The exponential family (
146
)
has nice properties derived from the classic Bartlett’s identities
(
Bartlett, 1953
)
.
I first review Bartlett’s identities:

Lemma 23.1

Given a probability density or mass
function
f(y\mid\theta)
indexed by a scalar parameter
\theta
, if we can
change the order of expectation and differentiation, then

E\left(\frac{\partial\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta}\right)=0

and

E\left\{\left(\frac{\partial\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta}\right)^{2}\right\}=E\left(-\frac{\partial^{2}\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta^{2}}\right).

This lemma is well-known in classic statistical theory for likelihood,
and I give a simple proof below.

Proof of Lemma
23.1
:

Define
\ell(y\mid\theta)=\log f(y\mid\theta)
as the log likelihood function, so
e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}
is the density satisfying

\int e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}\textnormal{d}y=\int f(y\mid\theta)\textnormal{d}y=1

by the definition of a probability density function (we can replace the integral by summation for a probability mass function). Differentiate the above identity to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\int e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}\textnormal{d}y=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\int\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}\textnormal{d}y=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\int e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\ell(y\mid\theta)\textnormal{d}y=0,

which implies Bartlett’s first identity.
Differentiate it twice to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\int e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\ell(y\mid\theta)\textnormal{d}y=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\int\left[e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\ell(y\mid\theta)\right\}^{2}+e^{\ell(y\mid\theta)}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\ell(y\mid\theta)\right]\textnormal{d}y=0,

which implies Bartlett’s second identity.

\square

Lemma
23.1

implies the moments of the exponential family (
146
).

Theorem 23.1

The first two moments of (
146
)
are

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\theta_{i},\phi)\equiv\mu_{i}=b^{\prime}(\theta_{i})

and

\textup{var}(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\theta_{i},\phi)\equiv\sigma_{i}^{2}=b^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{i})a(\phi).

Proof of Theorem
23.1
:

The first two derivatives of the log conditional density are

\frac{\partial\log f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\theta_{i},\phi)}{\partial\theta_{i}}=\frac{y_{i}-b^{\prime}(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)},\quad\frac{\partial^{2}\log f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\theta_{i},\phi)}{\partial\theta_{i}^{2}}=-\frac{b^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)}.

Lemma
23.1
implies that

E\left\{\frac{y_{i}-b^{\prime}(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)}\right\}=0,\quad E\left[\left\{\frac{y_{i}-b^{\prime}(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)}\right\}^{2}\right]=\frac{b^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)},

which further imply the first two moments of
y_{i}
given
x_{i}
.

\square

110.2
Generalized linear model

Section
110.1
is general, allowing the
mean parameter
\mu_{i}
to depend on
x_{i}
in an arbitrary way.
This flexibility does not immediately generate a useful statistical
procedure. To borrow information across observations, we assume that
the relationship between
y_{i}
and
x_{i}
remain “stable” and
can be captured by a fixed parameter
\beta
. A simple starting point
is to use
x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
to approximate
\mu_{i}
, which, however,
works naturally only for outcomes taking values in a wide range of
(-\infty,\infty)
. For general outcome variables,
we can link its mean and the linear combination of covariates by

\mu_{i}=\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),

where
\mu(\cdot)
is a known function and
\beta
is an unknown
parameter. The inverse of
\mu(\cdot)
is called the link function. This is called a GLM, which
has the following components:

(C1)

the conditional distribution (
146
)
as an exponential family with dispersion;

(C2)

the conditional mean
\mu_{i}=b^{\prime}(\theta_{i})
and variance
\sigma_{i}^{2}=b^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{i})a(\phi)

in Theorem
23.1
;

(C3)

the function linking the conditional mean and covariates
\mu_{i}=\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)
.

Models (
142
)–(
145
)
are the classical examples.
Figure
48
illustrates the relationship among key quantities in a GLM. In particular,

\theta_{i}=(b^{\prime})^{-1}(\mu_{i})=(b^{\prime})^{-1}\left\{\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\right\}

(147)

depends on
x_{i}
and
\beta
, with
(b^{\prime})^{-1}
indicating the inverse function of
b^{\prime}(\cdot)
.

Figure 48:
Quantities in a GLM

111
MLE for GLM

The contribution of unit
i
to the log-likelihood function is

\ell_{i}=\log f(y_{i}\mid x_{i};\beta,\phi)=\frac{y_{i}\theta_{i}-b(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)}+c(y_{i},\phi).

The contribution of unit
i
to the score function is

\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\beta}=\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\theta_{i}}\frac{\partial\theta_{i}}{\partial\mu_{i}}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta},

where

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\theta_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{i}-b^{\prime}(\theta_{i})}{a(\phi)},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\theta_{i}}{\partial\mu_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{b^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{i})}=\frac{a(\phi)}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}

follow from Theorem
23.1
.
So

\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\beta}=\frac{y_{i}-b^{\prime}(\theta_{i})}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta}=\frac{y_{i}-\mu_{i}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta},

leading to the following score equation for the MLE:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{y_{i}-\mu_{i}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta}=0,

(148)

or, more explicitly,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{y_{i}-\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\mu^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)x_{i}=0

The general relationship (
147
) between
\theta_{i}

and
\beta
is quite complicated. A natural choice of
\mu(\cdot)

is to cancel
(b^{\prime})^{-1}
in (
147
) so that

\mu(\cdot)=b^{\prime}(\cdot)\Longrightarrow\theta_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta.

This link function
\mu(\cdot)
is called the canonical link or the natural link, which leads
to further simplifications:

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=b^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\Longrightarrow\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta}=b^{\prime\prime}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)x_{i}=b^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{i})x_{i}=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{a(\phi)}x_{i},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\beta}=\frac{y_{i}-\mu_{i}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{a(\phi)}x_{i}=a(\phi)^{-1}x_{i}(y_{i}-\mu_{i})

(149)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle a(\phi)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}(y_{i}-\mu_{i})=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left(y_{i}-\mu_{i}\right)=0.

We have shown that the MLEs of models (
142
)–(
144
)
all satisfy (
149
). However, the MLE of (
145
)
does not because it does not use the natural link function resulting in
\mu(\cdot)\neq b^{\prime}(\cdot)
:

\mu(*)=e^{*},\quad b^{\prime}(*)=\delta\frac{e^{*}}{1-e^{*}}.

Using Bartlett’s second identity in Lemma
23.1
,
we can write the expected Fisher information conditional on covariates
as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\left(\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\mid x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\left\{\left(\frac{y_{i}-\mu_{i}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\right)^{2}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\mid x_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu_{i}}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\left\{\mu^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\right\}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

\displaystyle=X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}WX,

where

W=\text{diag}\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\left\{\mu^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\right\}^{2}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}.

With the canonical link, it further simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\left(\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\ell_{i}}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\mid x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\left\{\left(\frac{y_{i}-\mu_{i}}{a(\phi)}\right)^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\mid x_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{a(\phi)\right\}^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

We can obtain the estimated covariance matrix by replacing the unknown parameters with their estimates.
Now we review the estimated covariance matrices of the classical GLMs with canonical links.

Example 23.1
(continued)

In the Normal linear model,

\hat{V}=\hat{\sigma}^{2}(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}

with
\sigma^{2}
estimated further by the residual sum of squares.

Example 23.2
(continued)

In the binary logistic model,

\hat{V}=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{W}X)^{-1}

with
\hat{W}=\text{diag}\{\hat{\pi}_{i}(1-\hat{\pi}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, where
\hat{\pi}_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}/(1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}).

Example 23.3
(continued)

In the Poisson model,

\hat{V}=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{W}X)^{-1}

with
\hat{W}=\text{diag}\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, where
\hat{\lambda}_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}.

I relegate the derivation of the formula for the Negative-Binomial regression as Problem
23.2
. It is a purely theoretical exercise since
\delta
is usually unknown in practice.

112
Other GLMs

The
glm
function in
R
allows for the specification of the
family
parameters, with the corresponding canonical link functions shown below:

⬇

binomial
(
link

=

"logit"
)

gaussian
(
link

=

"identity"
)

Gamma
(
link

=

"inverse"
)

inverse
.
gaussian
(
link

=

"1/mu^2"
)

poisson
(
link

=

"log"
)

quasi
(
link

=

"identity"
,

variance

=

"constant"
)

quasibinomial
(
link

=

"logit"
)

quasipoisson
(
link

=

"log"
)

Examples
23.1
–
23.3
correspond to the second, the first, and the fifth choices above. Below I will briefly discuss the third choice for the Gamma regression and omit the discussion of other choices. See the help file of the
glm
function and
McCullagh and Nelder, 1989
for more details.

The Gamma
(\alpha,\beta)
random variable is positive with mean
\alpha/\beta
and variance
\alpha/\beta^{2}
. For convenience in modeling, we use a reparametrization Gamma
{}^{\prime}(\mu,\nu)
where

\begin{pmatrix}\mu\\
\nu\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha/\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\nu\\
\nu/\mu\end{pmatrix}.

So its mean equals
\mu
and its variance equals
\mu^{2}/\nu
which is quadratic in
\mu
. A feature of the Gamma random variable is that its coefficient of variation equals
1/\sqrt{\nu}
which does not depend on the mean. So Gamma
{}^{\prime}(\mu,\nu)
is a parametrization based on the mean and the coefficient of variation
(
McCullagh and Nelder, 1989
)
.
32
32

32

The coefficient of variation of a random variable
A
equals
\sqrt{\textup{var}(A)}/E(A)
.

Gamma
{}^{\prime}(\mu,\nu)
has density

f(y)=\frac{\beta^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}y^{\alpha-1}e^{-\beta y}=\frac{(\nu/\mu)^{\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu)}y^{\nu-1}e^{-(\nu/\mu)y},

and we can verify that it belongs to the exponential family with dispersion.
Gamma regression assumes

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\text{Gamma}^{\prime}(\mu_{i},\nu)

where
\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}
. So it is also a log-linear model. This does not correspond to the canonical link. Instead, we should specify
Gamma
(
link

=

"log"
)
to fit the log-linear Gamma regression model.

The log-likelihood function is

\log L(\beta,\nu)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{-\frac{\nu y_{i}}{e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}}+(\nu-1)\log y_{i}+\nu\log\nu-\nu x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta-\log\Gamma(\nu)\right\}.

Then

\frac{\partial\log L(\beta,\nu)}{\partial\beta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\nu y_{i}e^{-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}x_{i}-\nu x_{i})=\nu\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}(y_{i}-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta})x_{i}

and

\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta,\nu)}{\partial\beta\partial\nu}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}(y_{i}-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta})x_{i}.

So the MLE for
\beta
solves the following estimating equation

\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}(y_{i}-e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta})x_{i}=0.

Moreover,
\partial^{2}\log L(\beta,\nu)/\partial\beta\partial\nu
has expectation zero
so the Fisher information matrix is diagonal. In fact, it is identically zero when evaluated at
\hat{\beta}
since it is identical to the estimating equation.

I end this subsection with a comment on the estimating equation of
\beta
. It is similar to the Poisson score equation except for the additional weight
e^{-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}
. For positive outcomes, it is also conventional to fit OLS of
\log y_{i}
on
x_{i}
, resulting in the following estimating equation

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\log y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)x_{i}=0.

Firth, 1988
compared Gamma and log-Normal regressions based on efficiency. However, these two models are not entirely comparable: Gamma regression assumes that the log of the conditional mean of
y_{i}
given
x_{i}
is linear in
x_{i}
, whereas log-Normal regression assumes that the conditional mean of
\log y_{i}
given
x_{i}
is linear in
x_{i}
. By Jensen’s inequality,
\log E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})\geq E(\log y_{i}\mid x_{i}).

See Problem
23.3
for more discussions of Gamma regression.

113
Homework problems

23.1
MLE in GLMs with binary regressors

The MLEs for
\beta
in Models (
142
)–(
144
)
do not have explicit formulas in general. But in the special case
with the covariate
x_{i}
containing
1
and a binary covariate
z_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, their
MLEs do have simple formulas. Find them in terms of sample means of the outcomes.

Then find the variance estimators of
\hat{\beta}
.

23.2
Negative-Binomial covariance matrices

Assume that
\delta
is known.
Derive the estimated asymptotic covariance matrices of the MLE in the Negative-Binomial regression with
\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}
.

23.3
Gamma regression

Verify that Gamma
{}^{\prime}(\mu,\nu)
belongs to the natural exponential family with dispersion. Derive the first- and second-order derivatives of the log-likelihood function and Newton’s algorithm for computing the MLE.
Derive the estimated asymptotic covariance matrices of the MLE.

Show that if
y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim
Gamma
{}^{\prime}(\mu_{i},\nu)
with
\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}
, then

E(\log y_{i}\mid x_{i})=\psi(\nu)-\log(\nu)+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta

and

\textup{var}(\log y_{i}\mid x_{i})=\psi^{\prime}(\nu)

where
\psi(\nu)=\textup{d}\log\Gamma(\nu)/\textup{d}\nu
is the digamma function and
\psi^{\prime}(\nu)
is the trigamma function.

Remark: Use Proposition
29.3
to calculate the moments. The above conditional mean function ensures that the OLS estimator of
\log y_{i}
on
x_{i}
is consistent for all components of
\beta
except for the intercept.

Chapter 24
From Generalized Linear Models to Restricted Mean Models: the Sandwich Covariance Matrix

\chaptermark

GLM and Sandwich Covariance Matrix

This chapter discusses the consequence of misspecified GLMs, extending the EHW covariance estimator to its analogs under the GLMs. It serves as a stepping stone to the next chapter on the generalized estimating equation.

114
Restricted mean model

The logistic, Poisson, and Negative-Binomial models are extensions of
the Normal linear model. All of them are fully parametric models.
However, we have also discussed OLS as a restricted mean model

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta

without imposing any additional assumptions (e.g., the variance) on
the conditional distribution. The restricted
mean model is a semiparametric model. Then a natural question is: what
are the analogs of the restricted mean model for the binary and count models?

Binary outcome is too special because the conditional mean determines
the distribution. So if we assume that the conditional mean is
\mu_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}/(1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta})
,
then conditional distribution must be Bernoulli
(\mu_{i})
. Consequently, misspecification of the conditional mean function implies misspecification of the whole conditional distribution.

For other outcomes, the conditional mean cannot determine the conditional
distribution. If we assume

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),

we can verify that

E\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{y_{i}-\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\right\}=E\left[E\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{y_{i}-\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\mid x_{i}\right\}\right]=0

for any
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}
that can be a function of
x_{i}
, the true parameter
\beta
, and maybe
\phi
. So we can estimate
\beta
by solving the estimating equation:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{y_{i}-\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}=0.

(150)

If
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)=\sigma^{2}(x_{i})=\textup{var}(y_{i}\mid x_{i})
,
then the above estimating equation is the score equation derived from
the GLM of an exponential family. If not, (
150
) is not a score function but it is still a valid estimating equation.
In the latter case,
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)
is a “working”
variance. This has important implications for the practical data analysis.
First, we can interpret the MLE from a GLM more broadly: it is also
valid under a restricted mean model even if the conditional distribution
is misspecified. Second, we can construct more general estimators
beyond the MLEs from GLMs. However, we must address the issue of variance
estimation since the inference based on the Fisher information matrix no longer
works in general.

115
Sandwich covariance matrix

To simplify the notation, we assume
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are IID draws although we usually view the covariates as fixed. This additional assumption is innocuous as the final inferential procedures are identical.

Theorem 24.1

Assume
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are IID with
E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)
. We have

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\beta}-\beta\right)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,B^{-1}MB^{-1})

with

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=E\left\{\frac{1}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right\},

(151)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=E\left[\frac{\sigma^{2}(x)}{\left\{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)\right\}^{2}}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right].

(152)

Proof of Theorem
24.1
:

Applying Theorem
31.1
to

w=(x,y),\quad m(w,\beta)=\frac{y-\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta},

we can derive the asymptotic distribution of the
\hat{\beta}
.

The bread matrix equals

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=-E\left\{\frac{\partial m(w,\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=-E\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\left\{\frac{y-\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=-E\left[\frac{y-\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)}\frac{\partial^{2}\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}+\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\left\{\frac{y-\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)}\right\}\right]

(153)

\displaystyle=-E\left[\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\left\{\frac{y-\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}/\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)+\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\frac{y-\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\left\{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)\right\}^{2}}\right]

(154)

\displaystyle=E\left\{\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}/\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)\right\},

where the first term of (
153
) and the second
term of (
154
) are both zero under the restricted
mean model. The meat matrix equals

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=E\left\{m(w,\beta)m(w,\beta)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle=E\left[\left\{\frac{y-\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x)}\right\}^{2}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\frac{\sigma^{2}(x)}{\left\{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x)\right\}^{2}}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right].

\square

We can estimate the asymptotic variance by replacing
B
and
M

by their sample analogs.
With
\hat{\beta}
and the residual
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta})
, we can conduct statistical inference based on the following Normal approximation:

\hat{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{a}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}(\beta,\hat{V}),

with
\hat{V}\equiv n^{-1}\hat{B}^{-1}\hat{M}\hat{B}^{-1}
,
where

\displaystyle\hat{B}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\hat{\beta})}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta})}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta})}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}},

\displaystyle\hat{M}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{\tilde{\sigma}^{4}(x_{i},\hat{\beta})}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta})}{\partial\beta}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta})}{\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}.

As a special case, when the GLM is correctly specified with
\sigma^{2}(x)=\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x,\beta)
, then
B=M
and the asymptotic variance reduces to the inverse of the Fisher information matrix discussed in Section
111
.

Example 24.1
(continued)

In a working Normal linear model,
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)=\tilde{\sigma}^{2}
is constant and

\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)/\partial\beta=x_{i}
. So

\hat{B}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}},\quad\hat{M}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{(\tilde{\sigma}^{2})^{2}}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

with residual
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}
, recovering the EHW variance estimator

\hat{V}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}.

Example 24.2
(continued)

In a working binary logistic model,
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)=\pi(x_{i},\beta)\{1-\pi(x_{i},\beta)\}
and
\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)/\partial\beta=\pi(x_{i},\beta)\{1-\pi(x_{i},\beta)\}x_{i}
, where
\pi(x_{i},\beta)=\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}/(1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta})
. So

\hat{B}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\pi}_{i}(1-\hat{\pi}_{i})x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}},\quad\hat{M}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

with fitted mean
\hat{\pi}_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}/(1+e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}})
and residual
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-\hat{\pi}_{i}
, yielding a new covariance estimator

\hat{V}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\pi}_{i}(1-\hat{\pi}_{i})x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\pi}_{i}(1-\hat{\pi}_{i})x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}.

Example 24.3
(continued)

In a working Poisson model,
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)=\lambda(x_{i},\beta)
and
\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)/\partial\beta=\lambda(x_{i},\beta)x_{i}
, where
\lambda(x_{i},\beta)=\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}
. So

\hat{B}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{i}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}},\quad\hat{M}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

with fitted mean
\hat{\lambda}_{i}=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}
and residual
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}
, yielding a new covariance estimator

\hat{V}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{i}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{i}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}.

Again, I relegate the derivation of the formulas for the Negative-Binomial regression as a homework problem.
The
R
package
sandwich
implements the above covariance matrices
(
Zeileis, 2006
)
.

Overall, for count outcome regression, it seems that Poisson regression suffices as long as we use the robust standard error. The Negative-Binomial is unlikely to offer more if only the conditional mean is of interest.

116.4
Poisson regression for binary outcomes

Zou, 2004
proposed to use Poisson regression to analyze binary outcomes. This can be reasonable if the parameter of interest is the risk ratio instead of the odds ratio. Importantly, since the Poisson model is a wrong model, we must use the sandwich covariance estimator.

116.5
How robust are the robust standard errors?

Section
114
discusses the restricted mean model as an extension of the GLM, allowing for misspecification of the GLM while still preserving the conditional mean. We can extend the discussion to other parametric models.

Huber, 1967
started the literature on the statistical properties of the MLE in a misspecified model, and

White, 1982
addressed detailed inferential problems.

Buja et al., 2019b
reviewed this topic recently.

The discussion in Section
114
is useful when the conditional mean is correctly specified. However, if we think the GLM is severely misspecified with a wrong conditional mean, then the robust sandwich standard errors are unlikely to be helpful because the MLE converges to a wrong parameter in the first place
(
Freedman, 2006
)
.

\hat{\beta}
without assuming the models are correct.

in Chapter

. For some models, the function
coeftest
(*,

vcov

=

sandwich
)
does work. Alternatively, you can use the nonparametric bootstrap to obtain the robust standard errors.

in Chapter

.

Chapter 25
Generalized Estimating Equation for Correlated Multivariate Data

Previous chapters dealt with cross-sectional data, that is, we observe
n
units at a particular time point, collecting various covariates and outcomes. In addition, we assume that these units are independent, and sometimes, we even assume they are IID draws. Many applications have correlated data. Two canonical examples are

(E1)

repeated measurements of the same set of units over time, which are often called
longitudinal data
in biostatistics
(
Fitzmaurice et al., 2012
)
or
panel data
in econometrics
(
Wooldridge, 2010
)
; and

(E2)

clustered observations belonging to classrooms, villages, etc, which are common in cluster-randomized experiments in education
(
Schochet, 2013
)
and public health
(
Turner et al., 2017a
;
Turner et al., 2017b
)
.

Many excellent textbooks cover this topic intensively. This chapter focuses on a simple yet powerful strategy, which is a natural extension of the GLM discussed in the last chapter. It was initially proposed in
Liang and Zeger, 1986
, the most cited paper published in
Biometrika
in the past one hundred years
(
Titterington, 2013
)
.
For simplicity, we will use the term “longitudinal data” for general correlated data.

118
Examples of correlated data

118.1
Longitudinal data

We have used the data from
Royer et al., 2015
in Chapter
22
. Each worker’s number of gym visits was repeatedly measured over more than 100 weeks. It is a standard longitudinal dataset. In Chapter
22
, we conducted analysis for each week separately, and in this chapter, we will accommodate the longitudinal structure of the data.

(i,t)

119
Marginal model and the generalized estimating equation

We will extend the restricted mean model to deal with longitudinal
data, where we observe outcome
y_{it}
and covariate
x_{it}
for
each unit
i=1,\ldots,n
at time
t=1,\ldots,n_{i}.
The
n_{i}
’s can vary across units. When
n_{i}=1

for all units, we drop the time index and model the conditional mean
as

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),

and use the following estimating equation
to estimate the parameter
\beta
:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{y_{i}-\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}=0.

(155)

In (
155
),
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{i},\beta)
is a working variance function usually motivated by a GLM, but it can be misspecified. With an
n_{i}\times 1
vector outcome and an
n_{i}\times p
covariate matrix

\displaystyle Y_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}y_{i1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{in_{i}}\end{pmatrix},\quad X_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{pmatrix},\quad(i=1,\ldots,n)

(156)

we can extend the restricted mean model to

\displaystyle E(Y_{i}\mid X_{i})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}E(y_{i1}\mid X_{i})\\
\vdots\\
E(y_{in_{i}}\mid X_{i})\end{array}\right)

(169)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}E(y_{i1}\mid x_{i1})\\
\vdots\\
E(y_{in_{i}}\mid x_{in_{i}})\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\\
\vdots\\
\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\mu(X_{i},\beta),

where (
119
) and (
169
) are definitions, and (
119
) and (
119
)
are the two key assumptions. Assumption (
119
) requires that the conditional mean of
y_{it}
depends only on
x_{it}

but not on any other
x_{is}
with
s\neq t
. Assumption (
119
) requires
that the relationship between
x_{it}
and
y_{it}
is stable across units and time points with the function
\mu(\cdot)
and the parameter

\beta
not varying with respect to
i
or
t
. The model assumptions in (
119
) and (
119
)
are really strong, and I defer the critiques to the end of this chapter.
Nevertheless, the marginal model attracts practitioners for

(A1)

its similarity to GLM and the restricted mean model, and

(A2)

its simplicity of requiring only specification of the marginal conditional
means, not the whole joint distribution.

The advantage (A
1
) facilitates
the interpretation of the coefficient, and the advantage (A
2
) is crucial
because of the lack of familiar multivariate distributions in statistics
except for the multivariate Normal. The generalized estimating equation
(GEE) for
\beta
is the vector form of (
155
):

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\frac{\partial\mu(X_{i},\beta)}{\partial\beta}}_{p\times n_{i}}\underbrace{\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta)}_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}\underbrace{\left\{Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\beta)\right\}}_{n_{i}\times 1}=\underbrace{0}_{p\times 1},

(170)

where (
170
) has a similar form as (
155
)
with three terms organized to match the dimension so that matrix multiplications
are well-defined:

(GEE1)

the last term

Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\beta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{i1}-\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\\
\vdots\\
y_{in_{i}}-\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\end{array}\right)

represents the residual vector,

(GEE2)

the second term is the inverse of
\tilde{V}(X_{i},\beta)
, a working covariance
matrix of the conditional distribution of
Y_{i}
given
X_{i}

which may be misspecified:

\tilde{V}(X_{i},\beta)\neq V(X_{i})\equiv\textup{cov}(Y_{i}\mid X_{i}).

It is relatively easy to specify the working variance
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{it},\beta)
for each marginal component, for example, based on the marginal GLM. So the key is to specify the
n_{i}\times n_{i}
dimensional correlation matrix
R_{i}
to obtain

\tilde{V}(X_{i},\beta)=\text{diag}\{\tilde{\sigma}(x_{it},\beta)\}_{i=1}^{n_{i}}R_{i}\text{diag}\{\tilde{\sigma}(x_{it},\beta)\}_{i=1}^{n_{i}}.

We assume that the
R_{i}
’s are given now, and will discuss how to choose them in a later section.

(GEE3)

the first term is the partial derivative of an
n_{i}\times 1

vector
\mu(X_{i},\beta)=(\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),\ldots,\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta))^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

with respect to a
p\times 1
vector
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{p})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mu(X_{i},\beta)}{\partial\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta},\ldots,\frac{\partial\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta_{1}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta_{1}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta_{p}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta_{p}}\end{array}\right),

which is a
p\times n_{i}
matrix, denoted by
D_{i}(\beta).

120
Statistical inference with GEE

120.1
Computation using the Gauss–Newton method

We can use Newton’s method to solve the GEE (
170
).
However, calculating the derivative of the left-hand side of (
170
)
involves calculating the second order derivative of
\mu(X_{i},\beta)
with respect to
\beta
.
A simpler alternative without calculating the second-order derivative
is the Gauss–Newton method based on the following approximation:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\mu(X_{i},\beta)}{\partial\beta}\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i})\left\{Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\beta)\right\}

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\beta^{\text{old}})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})\left[\left\{Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})\right\}-D_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\beta^{\text{old}})(\beta-\beta^{\text{old}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\beta^{\text{old}})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})\left\{Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\beta^{\text{old}})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})D_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\beta^{\text{old}})(\beta-\beta^{\text{old}}).

So given
\beta^{\text{old}}
, we update it as

\displaystyle\beta^{\text{new}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{\text{old}}+\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\beta^{\text{old}})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})D_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\beta^{\text{old}})\right\}^{-1}

(172)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\beta^{\text{old}})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})\left\{Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\beta^{\text{old}})\right\}.

120.2
Asymptotic inference

The asymptotic distribution of
\hat{\beta}
follows from Theorem
31.2
. Similar to the proof of Theorem
24.1
, we can verify that
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,B^{-1}MB^{-1})

in distribution where

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=E\left\{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\beta)\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta)D_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\beta)\right\},

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=E\left\{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\beta)\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta)V(X_{i})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\beta)D_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\beta)\right\}.

After obtaining
\hat{\beta}
and the residual vector
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\hat{\beta})

for unit
i

(i=1,\ldots,n)
, we can conduct asymptotic inference
based on the Normal approximation

\hat{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{a}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}(\beta,n^{-1}\hat{B}^{-1}\hat{M}\hat{B}^{-1}),

where

\displaystyle\hat{B}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\hat{\beta})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\hat{\beta})D_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\hat{\beta}),

\displaystyle\hat{M}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}(\hat{\beta})\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\hat{\beta})\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\tilde{V}^{-1}(X_{i},\hat{\beta})D_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}(\hat{\beta}).

This covariance estimator proposed by
Liang and Zeger, 1986
, is robust to the misspecification of the marginal variances and the correlation structure as long as the conditional mean of
Y_{i}
given
X_{i}
is correctly specified.

120.3
Implementation: choice of the working covariance matrix

We have not discussed the choice of the working correlation matrix

R_{i}
. Different choices do not affect the consistency
but affect the efficiency of
\hat{\beta}
. A simple starting point
is the independent working correlation matrix
R_{i}=I_{n_{i}}
.
Under this correlation matrix, the GEE reduces to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\partial\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta},\ldots,\frac{\partial\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}(x_{i1},\beta)\\
&\ddots\\
&&\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}(x_{in_{i}},\beta)\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{i1}-\mu(x_{i1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\\
\vdots\\
y_{in_{i}}-\mu(x_{in_{i}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\end{array}\right)=0,

or, more compactly,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{y_{it}-\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{it},\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}=0.

(175)

This is simply the estimating equation of a restricted mean model
treating all data points
(i,t)
as independent observations. This
implies that the point estimate assuming the independent working correlation matrix is still consistent, although we must change the standard error
as in Section
120.2
.

With this simple starting point, we have a consistent yet inefficient
estimate of
\beta
, and then we can compute the residuals. The correlation
among the residuals contains information about the true covariance
matrix. With small and equal
n_{i}
’s, we can estimate the conditional
covariance without imposing any structure based on the residuals.
Using the estimated covariance matrix, we can update the GEE estimate
to improve efficiency. This leads to a two-step procedure.

An important intermediate case is motivated by the exchangeability
of the data points within the same unit
i
, so the working covariance
matrix is
\tilde{V}(X_{i},\beta)=\text{diag}\{\tilde{\sigma}(x_{it})\}_{i=1}^{n_{i}}R_{i}(\rho)\text{diag}\{\tilde{\sigma}(x_{it})\}_{i=1}^{n_{i}}
, where

R_{i}(\rho)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&\rho&\cdots&\rho\\
\rho&1&\cdots&\rho\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\rho&\rho&\cdots&1\end{array}\right).

We can estimate
\rho
based on the residuals from the first step.

The above three choices of the working covariance matrix are called
“
independent
”, “
unstructured
”, and “
exchangeable
” in the “
corstr
”
parameter of the function
gee
in the
gee
package in
R
. This function
also contains other choices proposed by
Liang and Zeger, 1986
.

A carefully chosen working covariance matrix can lead to efficiency gain compared to the simple independent covariance matrix. An efficient estimator requires a correctly specified working covariance matrix. This is often an infeasible goal, and what is more, the conditional covariance
\textup{cov}(Y_{i}\mid X_{i})
is a nuisance parameter if the conditional mean is the main parameter of interest. In practice, the independent working covariance suffices in many applications despite its potential efficiency loss. This is similar to the use of OLS in the presence of heteroskedasticity in linear models. Section
121
focuses on the independent working covariance, which is common in econometrics. Section
123
gives further justifications for this simple strategy.

121
A special case: cluster-robust standard error

Importantly,
Liang and Zeger, 1986
’s standard error treats each cluster
i
as an independent contributor to the uncertainty. In econometrics, this is called the cluster-robust standard error. Alternatively, we can use the bootstrap by resampling the clusters to approximate the asymptotic covariance matrix.
I will discuss linear and logistic regressions in this section, and leave the technical details of Poisson regression as a homework problem.

Stack the
Y_{i}
’s and
X_{i}
’s in (
156
) together to obtain

Y=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
Y_{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
\vdots\\
X_{n}\end{pmatrix},

which are the
N
dimensional outcome vector and
N\times p
covariate matrix, where
N=\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i}.

121.1
OLS

An important special case is the
marginal linear model with an independent working covariance matrix
and homoskedasticity, resulting in the following estimating equation:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{it}(y_{it}-x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)=0.

So the point estimator is just the pooled OLS using all data
points:

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{it}x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{it}y_{it}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y_{i}

\displaystyle=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y.

The three forms of
\hat{\beta}
above are identical: the first one is based on
N
observations, the second one is based on
n
independent units, and the last one is based on the matrix form with the pooled data.
Although the point estimate is identical to the case with independent
data points, we must adjust for the standard error according to Section

120.2
. From

D_{i}(\beta)=(x_{i1},\ldots,x_{in_{i}})=X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}},

we can verify that

\hat{\textup{cov}}(\hat{\beta})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}\right)^{-1},

where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-X_{i}\hat{\beta}=(\hat{\varepsilon}_{i1},\ldots,\hat{\varepsilon}_{in_{i}})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

is the residual vector of unit
i
. This is called the (Liang–Zeger) cluster-robust
covariance matrix in econometrics. The square roots of the diagonal terms are called the cluster-robust standard errors.
The cluster-robust
covariance matrix is often much larger than the
(Eicker–Huber–White) heteroskedasticity-robust covariance matrix assuming
independence of observations
(i,t)
:

\hat{\textup{cov}}_{\textsc{ehw}}(\hat{\beta})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{it}x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}^{2}x_{it}x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{it}x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}.

Note that

X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{it}x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}},

so the bread matrices in
\hat{\textup{cov}}(\hat{\beta})
and
\hat{\textup{cov}}_{\textsc{ehw}}(\hat{\beta})
are identical. The only difference is due to the meat matrices:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}x_{it}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}x_{it}\right)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\neq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}^{2}x_{it}x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

in general.

121.2
Logistic regression

For binary outcomes, we can use the marginal logistic model with an independent working covariance matrix,
resulting in the following estimating equation:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{it}\left\{y_{it}-\pi(x_{it},\beta)\right\}=0

where
\pi(x_{it},\beta)=e^{x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}/(1+e^{x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta}).

So the point estimator is the pooled logistic regression using all data
points, but we must adjust for the standard error according to Section

120.2
. From

\displaystyle D_{i}(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\pi(x_{i1},\beta)\{1-\pi(x_{i1},\beta)\}x_{i1},\ldots,\pi(x_{in_{i}},\beta)\{1-\pi(x_{in_{i}},\beta)\}x_{in_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\tilde{V}(X_{i},\beta),

with
\tilde{V}(X_{i},\beta)=\text{diag}\{\pi(x_{it},\beta)\{1-\pi(x_{it},\beta)\}\}_{t=1}^{n_{i}}
,
we can verify that

\hat{B}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{V}_{i}X_{i},\quad\hat{M}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i},

where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=(\hat{\varepsilon}_{i1},\ldots,\hat{\varepsilon}_{in_{i}})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
with residual

\hat{\varepsilon}_{it}=y_{it}-e^{x_{it}\hat{\beta}}/(1+e^{x_{it}\hat{\beta}})
, and
\hat{V}_{i}=\text{diag}\{\pi(x_{it},\hat{\beta})\{1-\pi(x_{it},\hat{\beta})\}\}_{t=1}^{n_{i}}.

So the cluster-robust covariance estimator for logistic regression is

\hat{\textup{cov}}(\hat{\beta})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{V}_{i}X_{i}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{V}_{i}X_{i}\right)^{-1}.

I leave the cluster-robust covariance estimator for Poisson regression to Problem
25.5
.

package for all the analyses below.

GEE analysis of the gym data

123
Critiques on the key assumptions

Consider the simple case with
n_{i}=2
for all
i
below.

123.1
Assumption (
119
)

Assumption (
119
) requires

E(y_{it}\mid X_{i})=E(y_{it}\mid x_{it}),

which holds automatically if
x_{it}=x_{i}
is time-invariant. With time-varying covariates, it effectively rules out the dynamics between
x
and
y
. Assumption (
119
) holds in the following data-generating process:

\textstyle{x_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{i2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i1}}
\textstyle{y_{i2}}

It does not hold if the lagged
x
affects
y
or the lagged
y
affects
x
:

\textstyle{x_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{i2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i1}}
\textstyle{y_{i2}}

or

\textstyle{x_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{i2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i2}}

or

\textstyle{x_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{i2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i2}}

With more complex data generating processes, Assumption (
119
) does not hold in general:

\textstyle{x_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{i2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i2}}

Liang and Zeger, 1986
assumed fixed covariates, ruling out the dynamics of
x
.

Pepe and Anderson, 1994
pointed out the importance of Assumption (
119
) in GEE with random time-varying covariates.
Pepe and Anderson, 1994
also showed that with an independent working covariance matrix, we can drop Assumption (
119
) as long as the marginal conditional mean is correctly specified. That is, if
E(y_{it}\mid x_{it})=\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)
, then

\displaystyle E\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{y_{it}-\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{it},\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}E\left[\frac{E\{y_{it}-\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\mid x_{it}\}}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(x_{it},\beta)}\frac{\partial\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\partial\beta}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

This gives another justification for the use of the independent working covariance matrix even though it can result in efficiency loss when Assumption (
119
) holds.

123.2
Assumption (
119
)

Assumption (
119
) requires a “stable” relationship between
x
and
y
across clusters and time points:

E(y_{it}\mid x_{it})=\mu(x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)

where
\mu
and
\beta
do not depend on
i
or
t
.
For clustered data, we can justify this assumption by the exchangeability of the units within clusters. However, it is much harder to interpret or justify it for longitudinal data with complex outcome dynamics.

We consider linear structural equations with a scalar time-invariant covariate. Without direct dependence of
y_{i2}
on
y_{i1}
, the data generating process

\displaystyle y_{i1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}+\beta x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i1},

\displaystyle y_{i2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}+\beta x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i2},

corresponding to the graph

\textstyle{x_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i1}}
\textstyle{y_{i2}}

has conditional expectations
E(y_{it}\mid x_{i})=\alpha_{t}+\beta x_{i}
if

E(\varepsilon_{it}\mid x_{i})=0.

(176)

However, with direct dependence of
y_{i2}
on
y_{i1}
, the data generating process

\displaystyle y_{i1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}+\beta x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i1},

\displaystyle y_{i2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}+\gamma y_{i1}+\delta x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i2},

corresponding to the graph

\textstyle{x_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{i2}}

has conditional expectations
E(y_{i1}\mid x_{i})=\alpha_{1}+\beta x_{i}
but

E(y_{i2}\mid x_{i})=\alpha_{2}+\gamma(\alpha_{1}+\beta x_{i})+\delta x_{i}=(\alpha_{2}+\gamma\alpha_{1})+(\delta+\beta\gamma)x_{i}

if (
176
) holds. The stability assumption requires

\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}+\gamma\alpha_{1},\quad\beta=\beta\gamma+\delta,

which are strange restrictions on the model parameters.

With time-varying covariates, this issue becomes even more subtle because Assumption (
119
) is unlikely to hold in the first place.

123.3
Explanation and prediction

Liang and Zeger, 1986
’s marginal model is more useful if the goal is to explain the relationship between
x
and
y
, in particular, a component of
x_{it}
represents the time-invariant treatment and
y_{it}
represents the time-varying outcomes. If the goal is prediction, then the marginal model can be problematic. For instance, if we observe the covariate value for a future observation
x_{is}
, the marginal model gives predicted outcome
\mu(x_{is}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta})
with the associated standard error computed based on the delta method. We can see two obvious problems with this prediction. First, it does not depend on
s
. Consequently, predicting
s=10
is the same as predicting
s=100
. However, the intuition is overwhelming that predicting the long-run outcome is much more difficult than predicting the short-run outcome, so we hope the standard error should be much larger for predicting the outcome at
s=100
. Second, the prediction does not depend on the lag outcomes because the marginal model ignores the dynamics of the outcome. With longitudinal observations, building a model with the lag outcomes may increase the prediction ability.

124
Homework problems

25.1
Sandwich asymptotic covariance matrix for GEE

Verify the formulas of
B
and
M
in Section
120.2
.

25.2
Cluster-robust standard error in OLS with a cluster-specific binary regressor

Consider a special case with
x_{it}=(1,x_{i})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
and
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, and view “1” as treatment and “0” as control. Show that the coefficient of
x_{i}
in the pooled OLS fit of
y_{it}
on
x_{it}
equals
\hat{\tau}=\bar{y}_{1}-\bar{y}_{0}
where

\bar{y}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}x_{i}y_{it}/N_{1},\quad\bar{y}_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}(1-x_{i})y_{it}/N_{0},

with
N_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i}x_{i}
and
N_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i}(1-x_{i})
denoting the total number of observations under treatment and control, respectively. Further show that the cluster-robust standard error of
\hat{\tau}
equals the square root of

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}R_{i}^{2}}{N_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i})R_{i}^{2}}{N_{0}^{2}},

where

R_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}(y_{it}-\bar{y}_{1}),&&\text{if }x_{i}=1,\\
\sum_{t=1}^{n_{i}}(y_{it}-\bar{y}_{0}),&&\text{if }x_{i}=0.\end{array}\right.

25.3
Cluster-robust standard error in GLM with a cluster-specific binary regressor

Inherit the setting from Problem
25.2
.

With a binary outcome
y_{it}
, show that the coefficient of
x_{i}
in the pooled logit regression of
y_{it}
on
x_{it}
equals
\hat{\tau}=\text{logit}\bar{y}_{1}-\text{logit}\bar{y}_{0}
. Further show that the cluster-robust standard error of
\hat{\tau}
equals the square root of

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}R_{i}^{2}}{N_{1}^{2}\bar{y}_{1}(1-\bar{y}_{1})}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i})R_{i}^{2}}{N_{0}^{2}\bar{y}_{0}(1-\bar{y}_{0})}.

With a count outcome
y_{it}
, show that the coefficient of
x_{i}
in the pooled Poisson regression of
y_{it}
on
x_{it}
equals
\hat{\tau}=\log\bar{y}_{1}-\log\bar{y}_{0}
. Further show that the cluster-robust standard error of
\hat{\tau}
equals the square root of

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}R_{i}^{2}}{N_{1}^{2}\bar{y}_{1}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i})R_{i}^{2}}{N_{0}^{2}\bar{y}_{0}}.

Cluster-robust standard error in ANOVA

This problem extends Problems
5.5
,

and

.

.
If the units are clustered by a factor
c_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, we can obtain the cluster-robust covariances
\hat{V}_{\textsc{lz}}
and
\hat{V}_{\textsc{lz}}^{\prime}
from the two WLS fits. Show that
\hat{V}_{\textsc{lz}}=\hat{V}_{\textsc{lz}}^{\prime}.

25.5
Cluster-robust standard error for Poisson regression

Similar to Sections
121.1
and
121.2
, derive the cluster-robust covariance matrix for Poisson regression:

\hat{\textup{cov}}(\hat{\beta})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{V}_{i}X_{i}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X_{i}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{V}_{i}X_{i}\right)^{-1},

where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-\mu(X_{i},\hat{\beta})
and
\hat{V}_{i}=\text{diag}\{e^{x_{it}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}}\}_{t=1}^{n_{i}}.

Part VIII
Beyond Modeling the Conditional Mean

Chapter 26
Quantile Regression

125
From the mean to the quantile

For a random variable
y
, we can define its mean as

E(y)=\arg\min_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}}E\left\{(y-\mu)^{2}\right\}.

With IID data
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, we can compute the sample mean

\bar{y}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}=\arg\min_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\mu)^{2},

which satisfies the CLT:

\sqrt{n}(\bar{y}-E(y))\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,\textup{var}(y))

in distribution if the variance
\textup{var}(y)
is finite.

Figure 50:
CDF and quantile

However, the mean can miss important information about
y
. How about other features of the outcome
y
? Quantiles can
characterize the distribution of
y
. For a random variable
y
,
we can define its distribution function as
F(c)=\textup{pr}(y\leq c)
and
its
\tau
th quantile as

F^{-1}(\tau)=\inf\left\{q:F(q)\geq\tau\right\}.

This defines a quantile function
F^{-1}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
If the distribution function is strictly monotone, then the quantile
function reduces to the inverse of the distribution function, and the
\tau
-th quantile solves
\tau=\textup{pr}(y\leq q)
as an equation of
q
.
See Figure
50
. For simplicity, this chapter focuses on the case with a monotone distribution
function. The definition above formulates the mean as the minimizer of an objective function. Similarly, we can define quantiles in an equivalent
way below.

Proposition 26.1

With a monotone distribution function
and positive density at the
\tau
th quantile, we have

F^{-1}(\tau)=\arg\min_{q\in\mathbb{R}}E\left\{\rho_{\tau}(y-q)\right\},

where

\rho_{\tau}(u)=u\left\{\tau-1(u<0)\right\}=\begin{cases}u\tau,&\text{if }u\geq 0,\\
-u(1-\tau),&\text{if }u<0,\end{cases}

is the check function (the name comes from its shape; see Figure
51
). In particular,
the median of
y
is

\textup{median}(y)=F^{-1}(0.5)=\arg\min_{q\in\mathbb{R}}E\left\{|y-q|\right\}.

Figure 51:
Check function

Proof of Proposition
26.1
:

To simplify the proof, we further assume that
y
has density function

f(\cdot)
. We will use Leibniz’s integral rule:

\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}x}\left\{\int_{a(x)}^{b(x)}f(x,t)\textnormal{d}t\right\}=f(x,b(x))b^{\prime}(x)-f(x,a(x))a^{\prime}(x)+\int_{a(x)}^{b(x)}\frac{\partial f(x,t)}{\partial x}\textnormal{d}t.

We can write

E\left\{\rho_{\tau}(y-q)\right\}=\int_{-\infty}^{q}(\tau-1)(c-q)f(c)\textnormal{d}c+\int_{q}^{\infty}\tau(c-q)f(c)\textnormal{d}c.

To minimize it over
q
, we can solve the first-order condition

\frac{\partial E\left\{\rho_{\tau}(y-q)\right\}}{\partial q}=(1-\tau)\int_{-\infty}^{q}f(c)\textnormal{d}c-\tau\int_{q}^{\infty}f(c)\textnormal{d}c=0.

So

(1-\tau)\textup{pr}(y\leq q)-\tau\left\{1-\textup{pr}(y\leq q)\right\}=0

which implies that

\tau=\textup{pr}(y\leq q),

so the
\tau
th quantile satisfies the first-order condition.
The second-order condition ensures it is the minimizer:

\frac{\partial^{2}E\left\{\rho_{\tau}(y-q)\right\}}{\partial q^{2}}\Big|_{q=F^{-1}(\tau)}=f\left\{F^{-1}(\tau)\right\}>0

by Leibniz’s integral rule again.

\square

The empirical distribution function is
\hat{F}(c)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}1(y_{i}\leq c)
,
which is a step function, increasing but not strictly monotone. With
Proposition
26.1
, we can easily define
the sample quantile as

\hat{F}^{-1}(\tau)=\arg\min_{q\in\mathbb{R}}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{\tau}(y_{i}-q),

which may not be unique even though the population quantile is. We
can view
\hat{F}^{-1}(\tau)
as a set containing all minimizers,
and with large samples the values in the set do not differ much. Similar
to the sample mean, the sample quantile also satisfies a CLT.

Theorem 26.1

Assume
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}y
with distribution function
F(\cdot)

that is strictly increasing and density function
f(\cdot)
that
is positive at the
\tau
th quantile. The sample quantile
is consistent for the true quantile and is asymptotically Normal:

\sqrt{n}\left\{\hat{F}^{-1}(\tau)-F^{-1}(\tau)\right\}\rightarrow\textsc{N}\left(0,\frac{\tau(1-\tau)}{\left[f\left\{F^{-1}(\tau)\right\}\right]^{2}}\right)

in distribution. In particular, the sample median satisfies

\sqrt{n}\left\{\hat{F}^{-1}(0.5)-\textup{median}(y)\right\}\rightarrow\textsc{N}\left(0,\frac{1}{4\left[f\left\{\textup{median}(y)\right\}\right]^{2}}\right)

in distribution.

Proof of Theorem
26.1
:

Based on the first order condition in Proposition
26.1
, the population quantile solves

E\{m_{\tau}(y-q)\}=0,

and the sample quantile solves

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{\tau}(y_{i}-q)=0,

where the check function has a partial derivative with respect to

u
except for the point
0
:

\displaystyle m_{\tau}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tau-1)1(u<0)+\tau 1(u>0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau-1(u<0).

By Theorem
31.1
, we only need to find the bread and meat matrices, which are scalars now:

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E\{m_{\tau}(y-q)\}}{\partial q}\Big|_{q=F^{-1}(\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E\{\tau-1(y\leq q)\}}{\partial q}\Big|_{q=F^{-1}(\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial F(q)}{\partial q}\Big|_{q=F^{-1}(\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-f\{F^{-1}(\tau)\},

and

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left[\{m_{\tau}(y-q)\}^{2}\right]\Big|_{q=F^{-1}(\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left[\left\{\tau-1(y\leq q)\right\}^{2}\right]\Big|_{q=F^{-1}(\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left[\tau^{2}+1(y\leq q)-2\cdot 1(y\leq q)\tau\right]\Big|_{q=F^{-1}(\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau^{2}+\tau-2\tau^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(1-\tau).

Therefore,
\sqrt{n}\{\hat{F}^{-1}(\tau)-F^{-1}(\tau)\}
converges to Normal with mean zero and variance
M/B^{2}=\tau(1-\tau)/[f\{F^{-1}(\tau)\}]^{2}
.

\square

To conduct statistical inference for the quantile
F^{-1}(\tau)
, we need to estimate the density of
y
at the
\tau
th quantile to obtain the estimated standard error of
\hat{F}^{-1}(\tau)
. Alternatively, we can use the bootstrap to obtain the estimated standard error. We will discuss the inference of quantiles in
R
in Section
128
.

126
From the conditional mean to conditional quantile

With an explanatory variable
x
for outcome
y
, we can define
the conditional mean as

E(y\mid x)=\arg\min_{m(\cdot)}E\left[\left\{y-m(x)\right\}^{2}\right].

We can use a linear function
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
to approximate the conditional
mean with the population OLS coefficient

\beta=\arg\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}E\left\{(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)^{2}\right\}=\left\{E(xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\right\}^{-1}E(xy),

and the sample OLS coefficient

\hat{\beta}=\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\right).

We have discussed the statistical properties of
\hat{\beta}
in previous chapters.
Motivated by Proposition
26.1
, we can
define the conditional quantile function as

F^{-1}(\tau\mid x)=\arg\min_{q(\cdot)}E\left[\rho_{\tau}\left\{y-q(x)\right\}\right].

We can use a linear function
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta(\tau)
to approximate the
conditional quantile function with

\beta(\tau)=\arg\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}E\left\{\rho_{\tau}(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)\right\}

called the
\tau
th population regression quantile, and

\hat{\beta}(\tau)=\arg\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{\tau}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)

(177)

called the
\tau
th sample regression quantile.
As a special case, when
\tau=0.5
, we have the regression median:

\hat{\beta}(0.5)=\arg\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b|,

which is also called the least absolute deviations (LAD).

Koenker and Bassett Jr, 1978
started the literature under a correctly specified conditional quantile model:

F^{-1}(\tau\mid x)=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta(\tau).

The interpretation of the
j
-th coordinate of the coefficient,
\beta_{j}(\tau)
, is the partial influence of
x_{ij}
on the
\tau
th conditional quantile of
y_{i}
given
x_{i}
.

Angrist et al., 2006
discussed quantile regression under misspecification, viewing it as the best linear approximation to the true conditional quantile function. This chapter will focus on the statistical properties of
the sample regression quantiles following
Angrist et al., 2006
’s discussion of statistical inference allowing for the misspecification of the quantile regression model.

Before that, we first comment
on the population regression quantiles based on some generative models. Below assume that the
v_{i}
’s are IID independent of the covariates
x_{i}
’s, with mean zero and distribution
g(c)=\textup{pr}(v_{i}\leq c)
.

Example 26.1

Under the linear model
y_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\sigma v_{i}
, we can verify that

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta

and

F^{-1}(\tau\mid x_{i})=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\sigma g^{-1}(\tau).

Therefore, with the first regressor being
1
, we have

\beta_{1}(\tau)=\beta_{1}+\sigma g^{-1}(\tau),\quad\beta_{j}(\tau)=\beta_{j},\quad(j=2,\ldots,p).

In this case, both the true conditional mean and quantile functions are linear, and
the population regression quantiles are constant across
\tau
except
for the intercept.

Example 26.2

Under a heteroskedastic linear model
y_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma)v_{i}

with
x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma>0
for all
x_{i}
’s,
we can verify that

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta

and

F^{-1}(\tau\mid x_{i})=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\gamma g^{-1}(\tau).

Therefore,

\beta(\tau)=\beta+\gamma g^{-1}(\tau).

In this case, both the true conditional quantile functions are linear, and
all coordinates of the population regression quantiles vary with
\tau
.

Example 26.3

Under the transformed linear model
\log y_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\sigma v_{i}
, we can verify that

E(y_{i}\mid x_{i})=\exp(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)M_{v}(\sigma),

where
M_{v}(t)=E(e^{tv})
is the moment generating function of
v
,
and

F^{-1}(\tau\mid x_{i})=\exp\left\{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\sigma g^{-1}(\tau)\right\}.

In this case, both the true conditional mean and quantile functions are log-linear in covariates.

127
Sample regression quantiles

127.1
Computation

The regression quantiles (
177
) do
not have explicit formulas in general, and we need to solve the optimization
problem numerically. Motivated by the piece-wise linear feature of
the check function, we decompose
y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
into the
difference between its positive part and negative part:

y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta=u_{i}-v_{i},

where

u_{i}=\max(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta,0),\quad v_{i}=-\min(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta,0).

So the objective function simplifies to the summation of

\rho_{\tau}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)=\tau u_{i}+(1-\tau)v_{i},

which is simply a linear function of the
u_{i}
’s and
v_{i}
’s.
Of course, these
u_{i}
’s and
v_{i}
’s are not arbitrary because
they must satisfy the constraints by the data. Using the notation

Y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right),\quad X=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{array}\right),\quad u=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{n}\end{array}\right),\quad v=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{n}\end{array}\right),

finding the
\tau
th regression quantile is equivalent to a linear
programming problem with linear objective function and linear constraints:

\displaystyle\min_{b,u,v}\tau 1_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}u+(1-\tau)1_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}v,

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }Y=Xb+u-v,

\displaystyle\text{and }u_{i}\geq 0,v_{i}\geq 0\quad(i=1,\ldots,n).

The function
rq
in the
R
package
quantreg
computes the regression quantiles with various choices of methods.

127.2
Asymptotic inference

Similar to the sample quantiles, the regression quantiles are also
consistent for the population regression quantiles and asymptotically
Normal. So we can conduct asymptotic inference based on the results
in the following theorem
(
Angrist et al., 2006
)
.

Theorem 26.2

Assume
(y_{i},x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}(y,x)
. Under some regularity
conditions, we have

\sqrt{n}\left\{\hat{\beta}(\tau)-\beta(\tau)\right\}\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,B^{-1}MB^{-1})

in distribution, where

B=E\left[f_{y\mid x}\left\{x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta(\tau)\right\}xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right],\quad M=E\left[\left\{\tau-1\left(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta(\tau)\leq 0\right)\right\}^{2}xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right],

with
f_{y\mid x}(\cdot)
denoting the conditional density of
y

given
x
.

Proof of Theorem
26.2
:

The population regression quantile solves

E\left\{m_{\tau}(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)x\right\}=0,

and the sample regression quantile solves

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{\tau}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)x_{i}=0.

By Theorem
31.1
,
we only need to calculate the explicit forms of
B

and
M
. Let
F_{y\mid x}(\cdot)
and
f_{y\mid x}(\cdot)
be the
conditional distribution and density functions. We have

\displaystyle E\left\{m_{\tau}(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)x\right\}

\displaystyle=E\left[\left\{\tau-1(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b\leq 0)\right\}x\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\left\{\tau-F_{y\mid x}(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)\right\}x\right],

so

\frac{\partial E\left\{m_{\tau}(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)x\right\}}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}=-E\left\{f_{y\mid x}(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}b)xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right\}.

This implies the formula of
B
. The formula of
M
follows from

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=E\left\{m_{\tau}^{2}(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta(\tau))xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle=E\left[\left\{\tau-1(y-x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta(\tau)\leq 0)\right\}^{2}xx^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right].

\square

Based on Theorem
26.2
, we can estimate the asymptotic covariance matrix of
\hat{\beta}(\tau)
by
n^{-1}\hat{B}^{-1}\hat{M}\hat{B}^{-1}
, where

\hat{M}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\tau-1\left(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}(\tau)\leq 0\right)\right\}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

and

\hat{B}=\left(2nh\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}1\left\{|y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}(\tau)|\leq h\right\}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

for a carefully chosen
h
.
Powell, 1991
’s theory suggests to use
h
satisfying condition
h=O(n^{-1/3})
, but the theory is not so helpful since it only suggests the order of
h
. The
quantreg
package in
R
chooses a specific
h
that satisfies this condition.
In finite samples, the bootstrap often gives a better estimation of the asymptotic covariance matrix.

128
Numerical examples

128.1
Sample quantiles

Figure 52:

We can use the
quantile
function to obtain the sample quantiles. However, it does not report standard errors. Instead, we can use the
rq
function to compute sample quantiles by regressing the outcome on constant 1. These two functions may return different sample quantiles when they are not unique. The difference is often small with large sample sizes.

I use the following simulation to compare various methods for standard error estimation. The first data-generating process has a standard Normal outcome.

In the above,

,

correspond to the standard errors by
Koenker and Bassett Jr, 1978

I also run the same simulation but replace the Normal outcome with Exponential:

)
. Figure

compares the estimated standard errors with the true asymptotic standard error in Theorem

. Bootstrap works the best, and the one involving kernel estimation of the density seems biased.

Figure
53
compares OLS and LAD under the above four data-generating processes. With Normal errors, OLS is more efficient; with Laplace errors, LAD is more efficient. This confirms the theory of MLE. With Exponential errors, LAD is also more efficient than OLS. Under the fourth data-generating process, LAD is more efficient than OLS. In general, however, OLS and LAD target the conditional mean and conditional median, respectively. Since the parameters differ in general, the comparison of the standard errors is not very meaningful. Both OLS and LAD give useful information about the data.
