131
Homework problems

26.1
Quantile regression with a binary regressor

For
i=1,\ldots,n
, the first
1/3
observations have
x_{i}=1
and the last
2/3
observations have
x_{i}=0
;

y_{i}\mid x_{i}=1
follows an Exponential
(1)
, and
y_{i}\mid x_{i}=0
follows an Exponential
(2)
. Find

(\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta})=\arg\min_{(a,b)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{1/2}(y_{i}-a-bx_{i}).

and the joint asymptotic distribution.

26.2
Conditional quantile function in bivariate Normal

Show that if
(x,y)
follows a bivariate Normal, the conditional quantile function of
y
given
x
is linear in
x
with the same slope across all
\tau
.

26.3
Quantile range and variance

A symmetric random variable
y
satisfies
y\sim-y
.
Define the
1-\alpha
quantile range of a symmetric random variable
y
as the interval of its
\alpha/2
and
1-\alpha/2
quantiles.
Given two symmetric random variables
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, show that if the
1-\alpha
quantile range of
y_{1}
is wider than that of
y_{2}
for all
\alpha
, then
\textup{var}(y_{1})\geq\textup{var}(y_{2})
. Does the converse of the statement hold? If so, give a proof; if not, give a counterexample.

26.4
Interquartile range and estimation

The interquartile range of a random variable
y
equals the difference between its 75% and 25% quantiles.
Based on IID data
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, write a function to estimate the interquartile range and the corresponding standard error using the bootstrap. Use simulated data to evaluate the finite sample properties of the point estimate (e.g., bias and variance) and the 95% confidence interval (e.g. coverage rate and length).

Find the asymptotic distribution of the estimator for the interquartile range.

26.5
Joint asymptotic distribution of the sample median and the mean

Assume that
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\sim y
are IID. Find the joint asymptotic distribution of the sample mean
\bar{y}
and median
\hat{m}
.

Hint: The mean
\mu
and median
m
satisfy the estimating equation with

w(y,\mu,m)=\begin{pmatrix}y-\mu\\
0.5-1(y-m\leq 0)\end{pmatrix}.

26.6
Weighted quantile regression and application

Many real data contain weights due to sampling. For example, in
Angrist et al., 2006
’s data,
perwt
is the sampling weight. Define the weighted quantile regression problem theoretically and re-analyze
Angrist et al., 2006
’s data with weights. Note that similar to
lm
and
glm
, the quantile regression function
rq
also has a parameter
weights
.

Chapter 27
Modeling Time-to-Event Outcomes

132
Examples

Time-to-event data are common in biomedical and social sciences. Statistical analysis of time-to-event data is called

survival analysis
in biostatistics and
duration analysis
in econometrics.
The former name comes from biomedical applications where the outcome
denotes the survival time or the time to the recurrence of the disease of interest. The
latter name comes from the economic applications where the outcome
denotes the weeks unemployed or days until the next arrest after being released from incarceration. See
Kalbfleisch and Prentice, 2011
for biomedical applications and
Heckman and Singer, 1984
for economic applications.
Freedman, 2008
gave a concise and critical introduction to survival analysis.

132.1
Survival analysis

The Combined Pharmacotherapies and Behavioral Interventions study evaluated the efficacy of medication, behavioral therapy, and their combination for the treatment of alcohol dependence
(
Anton et al., 2006
)
. Between January 2001 and January 2004,
n=1224
recently alcohol-abstinent volunteers were randomized to receive medical management with 16 weeks of naltrexone (100mg daily) or placebo, with or without a combined behavioral intervention. It was a
2\times 2
factorial experiment. The outcome of interest is the time to the first day of heavy drinking and other endpoints. I adopt the data from
Lin et al., 2016
.

An obvious feature of time-to-event data is that the outcome is non-negative. This can be easily dealt with by the log transformation. However, the outcomes may be censored, resulting in inadequate tail information. With right censoring, modeling the mean involves extrapolation in the right tail.

133
Time-to-event data

Let
T\geq 0
denote the outcome of interest. We can characterize
a non-negative continuous
T
using its density
f(t)
, distribution
function
F(t)
, survival function
S(t)=1-F(t)=\textup{pr}(T>t)
, and hazard
function

\lambda(t)=\lim_{\Delta t\downarrow 0}\textup{pr}(t\leq T<t+\Delta t\mid T\geq t)/\Delta t.

Within a small time interval
[t,t+\Delta t]
, we have approximation

\textup{pr}(t\leq T<t+\Delta t\mid T\geq t)\cong\lambda(t)\Delta t,

so the hazard function denotes the death rate within a small interval conditioning on surviving up to time
t
.
Both the survival and hazard functions are commonly used to describe a positive random
variable.
First, the survival function has a simple relationship with the expectation.

Proposition 27.1

For a non-negative random variable
T
,

E(T)=\int_{0}^{\infty}S(t)\textnormal{d}t.

Proposition
27.1
holds for both continuous and discrete non-negative random variables.
It states that the expectation of a nonnegative random variable equals the area under the survival function. It does not require the existence of the density function of
T
.

Proof of Proposition
27.1
:

Fubini’s theorem allows us to swap the expectation and integral below:

\displaystyle E(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left\{\int_{0}^{T}\textnormal{d}t\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left\{\int_{0}^{\infty}1(T>t)\textnormal{d}t\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}E\{1(T>t)\}\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{pr}(T>t)\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}S(t)\textnormal{d}t.

\square

Second, the survival and hazard functions can determine each other in the following way.

Proposition 27.2

For a non-negative continuous random variable
T
,

\displaystyle\lambda(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{f(t)}{S(t)}=-\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\log S(t),

\displaystyle S(t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s)\textnormal{d}s\right\}.

Proof of Proposition
27.2
:

By definition,

\displaystyle\lambda(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta t\downarrow 0}\frac{\textup{pr}(t\leq T<t+\Delta t)}{\Delta t}\frac{1}{\textup{pr}(T\geq t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta t\downarrow 0}\frac{F(t+\Delta t)-F(t)}{\Delta t}\frac{1}{S(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{f(t)}{S(t)}.

We can further write the above equation as

\displaystyle\lambda(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{f(t)}{S(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\textnormal{d}S(t)/\textnormal{d}t}{S(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\log S(t),

so we can use the Newton–Leibniz formula to obtain

\textnormal{d}\log S(t)=-\lambda(t)\textnormal{d}t

which implies

\log S(t)-\log S(0)=-\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s)\textnormal{d}s.

Because
\log S(0)=0
, we have
\log S(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s)\textnormal{d}s
, giving the final result.

\square

Example 27.1
(Exponential)

The Exponential
(\lambda)
random variable
T
has density
f(t)=\lambda e^{-\lambda t}
, survival function
S(t)=e^{-\lambda t}
, and constant hazard function

\lambda(t)=\lambda.

An important feature of the Exponential random variable is its memoryless property as shown in Proposition
29.6
.

Example 27.2
(Gamma)

The Gamma
(\alpha,\beta)
random variable
T
has density

f(t)=\beta^{\alpha}t^{\alpha-1}e^{-\beta t}/\Gamma(\alpha).

When
\alpha=1
, it reduces to Exponential
(\beta)
with a constant hazard function. In general, the survival function and hazard function do not have simple forms, but we can use
dgamma
and
pgamma
to compute them numerically. The left panel of Figure
57
plots the hazard functions of Gamma
(\alpha,\beta)
. When
\alpha<1
, the hazard function is decreasing; when
\alpha>1
, the hazard function is increasing.

Figure 57:
Left: Gamma
(\alpha,\beta=2)
hazard functions; Right: Log-Normal
(\mu,\sigma^{2})
hazard functions

Example 27.3
(Log-Normal)

The Log-Normal random variable
T\sim
Log-Normal
(\mu,\sigma^{2})
equals exponential of
\textsc{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
. The right panel of Figure
57
plots the hazard functions with four different parameter combinations.

Example 27.4
(Weibull)

The Weibull distribution has many different parametrizations. Here I follow the
R
function
dweibull
, which has a
shape
parameter
a>0
and
scale
parameter
b>0
. The Weibull
(a,b)
random variable
T
can be generated by

\displaystyle T=bZ^{1/a}

(178)

which is equivalent to

\log T=\log b+a^{-1}\log Z,

where
Z\sim
Exponential
(1)
. We can verify that
T
has the density function

f(t)=\frac{a}{b}\left(\frac{t}{b}\right)^{a-1}\exp\left\{-\left(\frac{t}{b}\right)^{a}\right\},

survival function

S(t)=\exp\left\{-\left(\frac{t}{b}\right)^{a}\right\},

and hazard function

\lambda(t)=\frac{a}{b}\left(\frac{t}{b}\right)^{a-1}.

So when
a=1
, Weibull reduces to Exponential with constant hazard function. When
a>1
, the hazard function increases; when
a<1
, the hazard function decreases.

We can characterize a positive discrete random variable
T\in\left\{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots\right\}

by its probability mass function
f(t_{k})=\textup{pr}(T=t_{k})
, distribution
function
F(t)=\sum_{k:t_{k}\leq t}f(t_{k})
, survival function
S(t)=\sum_{k:t_{k}>t}f(t_{k})
,
and discrete hazard function

\lambda_{k}=\textup{pr}(T=t_{k}\mid T\geq t_{k})=\frac{f(t_{k})}{S(t_{k}-)},

where
S(t_{k}-)
denotes the left limit of the function
S(t)

at
t_{k}
. Figure
58
shows an example of a survival function for a discrete random variable, which shows that
S(t)
is a step function and right-continuous with left limits.

Figure 58:
Discrete survival function with masses
(0.1,0.05,0.15,0.2,0.3,0.2)
at
(1,2,3,4,5,6)

The discrete hazard and survival functions have the following
connection which will be useful for the next section.

Proposition 27.3

For a positive discrete random variable

T
, its survival function is a step function determined by

S(t)=\textup{pr}(T>t)=\prod_{k:t_{k}\leq t}(1-\lambda_{k}).

Note that
S(t)
is a step function decreasing at each
t_{k}
because
\lambda_{k}
is probability and thus bounded between zero and one.

Proof of Proposition
27.3
:

By definition,

1-\lambda_{k}=1-\textup{pr}(T=t_{k}\mid T\geq t_{k})=\textup{pr}(T>t_{k}\mid T\geq t_{k})

is the probability of surviving longer than
t_{k}
conditional on
surviving at least as long as
t_{k}
. We can verify Proposition

27.3
within each interval of the
t_{k}
’s.
For example, if
t<t_{1}
, then
S(t)=\textup{pr}(T>t)=1
; if
t_{1}\leq t<t_{2}
,
then

\displaystyle S(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(T>t_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(T>t_{1},T\geq t_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(T>t_{1}\mid T\geq t_{1})\textup{pr}(T\geq t_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\lambda_{1};

if
t_{2}\leq t<t_{3}
, then

\displaystyle S(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(T>t_{2})=\textup{pr}(T>t_{2},T\geq t_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(T>t_{2}\mid T\geq t_{2})\textup{pr}(T\geq t_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\lambda_{2})(1-\lambda_{1}).

We can also verify other values of
S(t)
by induction.

\square

134
Kaplan–Meier survival curve

Without censoring, estimating the CDF or the survival function is rather straightforward. With IID data
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{n})
, we can estimate the CDF by
\hat{F}(t)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}1(T_{i}\leq t)
and the survival function by
\hat{S}(t)=1-\hat{F}(t)
.

Figure 59:
Data structure for the Kaplan–Meier curve

Figure
59
shows the common data structure with censoring in survival analysis:

(S1)

t_{1},\ldots,t_{K}
are the death times, and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{K}
are the corresponding number of deaths;

(S2)

r_{1},\ldots,r_{K}
are the number of patients at risk, that is,
r_{1}
patients are not dead or censored right before time
t_{1}
, and so on;

(S3)

c_{1},\ldots,c_{K}
are the number of censored patients within interval
[t_{1},t_{2}),\ldots,[d_{K},\infty)
.

Kaplan and Meier, 1958
proposed the following simple estimator for the survival function.

Definition 27.1
(Kaplan–Meier curve)

First estimate the discrete hazard function at the failure times
\left\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{K}\right\}

as
\hat{\lambda}_{k}=d_{k}/r_{k}\ (k=1,\ldots,K)
and then estimate the survival
function as

\hat{S}(t)=\prod_{k:t_{k}\leq t}(1-\hat{\lambda}_{k}).

The
\hat{S}(t)
in Definition
27.1
is also called the product-limit estimator
of the survival function due to its mathematical form.

At each failure time
t_{k}
, we view
d_{k}
as the result of
r_{k}

Bernoulli trials with probability
\lambda_{k}
. So
\hat{\lambda}_{k}=d_{k}/r_{k}

has variance
\lambda_{k}(1-\lambda_{k})/r_{k}
which can be estimated
by

\hat{\textup{var}}(\hat{\lambda}_{k})=\hat{\lambda}_{k}(1-\hat{\lambda}_{k})/r_{k}.

We can estimate the variance of the survival function using the delta
method. We can approximate the variance of

\displaystyle\log\hat{S}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k:t_{k}\leq t}\log(1-\hat{\lambda}_{k})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\sum_{k:t_{k}\leq t}\log(1-\lambda_{k})-\sum_{k:t_{k}\leq t}(1-\lambda_{k})^{-1}(\hat{\lambda}_{k}-\lambda_{k})

by

\displaystyle\hat{\textup{var}}\left\{\log\hat{S}(t)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k:t_{k}\leq t}(1-\lambda_{k})^{-2}\hat{\textup{var}}(\hat{\lambda}_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k:t_{k}\leq t}(1-\hat{\lambda}_{k})^{-2}\hat{\lambda}_{k}(1-\hat{\lambda}_{k})/r_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k:t_{k}\leq t}\frac{d_{k}}{r_{k}(r_{k}-d_{k})},

which is called Greenwood’s formula
(
Greenwood, 1926
)
. A hidden assumption above is the independence of the
\hat{\lambda}_{k}
’s. This assumption cannot be justified due to the dependence of the events. However, a deeper theory of counting processes shows that Greenwood’s formula is valid even without the independence
(
Fleming and Harrington, 2011
)
.

Based on Greenwood’s formula, we can construct a confidence interval for
\log S(t)
:

\log\hat{S}(t)\pm z_{\alpha}\sqrt{\hat{\textup{var}}\left\{\log\hat{S}(t)\right\}},

which implies a confidence interval for
S(t)
. However, this interval
can be outside of range
[0,1]
because the log transformation
\log S(t)
is in the range of
(-\infty,0)
but the Normal approximation is in the range
(-\infty,\infty).

A better transformation is log-log:

v(t)=\log\left\{-\log S(t)\right\},\quad\hat{v}(t)=\log\left\{-\log\hat{S}(t)\right\}.

Using Taylor expansion, we can approximate the variance of

\hat{v}(t)\cong\log\left\{-\log S(t)\right\}-\frac{1}{\log S(t)}\left\{\log\hat{S}(t)-\log S(t)\right\}

by

\frac{\hat{\textup{var}}\left\{\log\hat{S}(t)\right\}}{\left\{\log\hat{S}(t)\right\}^{2}}.

Based on this formula and Greenwood’s formula above, we can construct
a confidence interval for
v(t)
:

\log\left\{-\log\hat{S}(t)\right\}\pm z_{\alpha}\sqrt{\hat{\textup{var}}\left\{\log\hat{S}(t)\right\}}/\log\hat{S}(t),

which implies another confidence interval for
S(t)
.
In the
R
package
survival
, the function
survfit
can fit the Kaplan–Meier curve, where the specifications
conf
.
type

=

"log"
and
conf
.
type

=

"log-log"
return confidence intervals based on the log and log-log transformations, respectively.

Figure
60
plots four curves based on the combination of
NALTREXONE
and
THERAPY
using the data of
Lin et al., 2016
. I do not show the confidence intervals due to the large overlap.

⬇

>

km4groups

=

survfit
(
Surv
(
futime
,

relapse
)

~

NALTREXONE

+

THERAPY
,

+

data

=

COMBINE
)

>

plot
(
km4groups
,

bty

=

"n"
,

col

=

1:4,

+

xlab

=

"t"
,

=

"survival
␣
functions"
)

>

legend
(
"topright"
,

+

c
(
"NALTREXONE=0,
␣
THERAPY=0"
,

+

"NALTREXONE=0,
␣
THERAPY=1"
,

+

"NALTREXONE=1,
␣
THERAPY=0"
,

+

"NALTREXONE=1,
␣
THERAPY=1"
),

+

=

1:4,

lty

=

1,

bty

=

"n"
)

Figure 60:
Lin et al., 2016
’s data

The above discussion on the Kaplan–Meier curve is rather heuristic. More fundamentally, what is the underlying censoring mechanism that ensures the possibility that the distribution of the survival time can be recovered by the observed data? It turns out that we have implicitly assumed that the survival time and the censoring time are independent. Homework problem
27.1
gives a theoretical statement.

135
Cox model for time-to-event outcome

Another important problem is to model the dependence of the survival time
T
on covariates
x
. The major challenge is that the survival time is often censored. Let
C_{i}
be the censoring time of unit
i
, and we can only observe the minimum value of the survival time and the censoring time. So the observed data are

(x_{i},y_{i},\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{n},

where

y_{i}=\min(T_{i},C_{i}),\quad\delta_{i}=1(T_{i}\leq C_{i})

are the event time and the censoring indicator, respectively. A key assumption is that the censoring mechanism is noninformative:

Assumption 27.1
(noninformative censoring)

T_{i}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}C_{i}\mid x_{i}.

We can start with parametric models.

Example 27.5

Assume
T_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim
Log-Normal
(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta,\sigma^{2})
. Equivalently,

\log T_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon_{i},

where the
\varepsilon_{i}
’s are IID
\textsc{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
independent of the
x_{i}
’s. This is a Normal linear model on
\log T_{i}
.

Example 27.6

Assume that
T_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim
Weibull
(a,b=e^{x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta})
. Based on the definition of the Weibull distribution in Example
178
, we have

\log T_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta+\varepsilon_{i}

where the
\varepsilon_{i}
’s are IID
a^{-1}\log\text{Exponential}(1)
, independent of the
x_{i}
’s.

The
R
package
survival
contains the function
survreg
to fit parametric survival models including the choices of
dist

=

"lognormal"
,
dist

=

"weibull"
, etc. However, these parametric models are not commonly used in practice. The parametric forms can be too strong, and due to right censoring, the inference can be driven by extrapolation to the right tail.

135.1
Cox model and its interpretation

Cox, 1972
proposed to model the conditional hazard function

\displaystyle\lambda(t\mid x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta t\downarrow 0}\textup{pr}(t\leq T<t+\Delta t\mid T\geq t,x)/\Delta t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{f(t\mid x)}{S(t\mid x)}.

His celebrated proportional hazards model has the following form.

Assumption 27.2
(Cox proportional hazards model)

Assume the conditional hazard ratio function has the form

\displaystyle\lambda(t\mid x)=\lambda_{0}(t)\exp(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),

(179)

where
\beta
is an unknown parameter and
\lambda_{0}(\cdot)
is an unknown function.

Assumption
27.2
is equivalent to

\log\lambda(t\mid x)=\log\lambda_{0}(t)+x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta.

Unlike other regression models,
x
does not contain the intercept in (
179
). If the first component of
x
is
1
, then we can write

\displaystyle\lambda(t\mid x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}(t)\exp(x_{1}\beta_{1}+\cdots+x_{p}\beta_{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}(t)e^{\beta_{1}}\exp(x_{2}\beta_{2}+\cdots+x_{p}\beta_{p})

and redefine
\lambda_{0}(t)e^{\beta_{1}}
as another unknown function. With an intercept, we cannot identify
\lambda_{0}(t)
and
\beta_{1}
separately. So we drop the intercept to ensure identifiability.

From the log-linear form of the conditional hazard function, we have

\log\frac{\lambda(t\mid x^{\prime})}{\lambda(t\mid x)}=(x^{\prime}-x)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta,

so each coordinate of
\beta
measures the log conditional hazard ratio holding other covariates constant.
Because of this, (
179
) is called the proportional hazards model.
A positive
\beta_{j}
suggests a “positive” effect on the hazard function and thus a “negative” effect on the survival time itself.
Consider a special case with a binary
x_{i}
, then the proportional hazards assumption implies that
\lambda(t\mid 1)=\gamma\lambda(t\mid 0)
with
\gamma=\exp(\beta)
, and therefore the survival functions satisfy

\displaystyle S(t\mid 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left\{-\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(u\mid 1)\textnormal{d}u\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left\{-\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(u\mid 0)\textnormal{d}u\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{S(t\mid 0)\right\}^{\gamma},

which is a power transformation.
Qualitatively, we have the following two cases:

(PH1)

\beta<0
: so the hazard ratio
\gamma=\exp(\beta)<1
and
S(t\mid 1)\geq S(t\mid 0)
for all
t
, which implies a longer survival time under treatment;

(PH2)

\beta>0
: so the hazard ratio
\gamma=\exp(\beta)>1
and
S(t\mid 1)\leq S(t\mid 0)
for all
t
, which implies a shorter survival time under treatment.

Figure
61
shows some survival functions satisfying the proportional hazards assumption, none of which cross each other within the interval
t\in(0,\infty).
When the two survival functions cross, the proportional hazards assumption does not hold.

Figure 61:
Proportional hazards assumption with different baseline survival functions, where the power equals
\gamma=\exp(\beta)
.

Theoretically, we can allow the covariates to be time-dependent, that is,
x_{i}(t)
can depend on
t
and thus is a stochastic process. However, the interpretation of the coefficient becomes challenging
(
Fisher and Lin, 1999
)
. This chapter focuses on the simple case with time-invariant covariates.

135.2
Partial likelihood

The likelihood function is rather complicated, which depends on an unknown function
\lambda_{0}(t)
.
Assuming no ties,
Cox, 1972
proposed to use the partial likelihood function
to estimate
\beta
:

L(\beta)=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\exp(x_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)},

where the product is over
K
time points with failures,
x_{k}
is the covariate value of the failure at time
t_{k}
, and
R(t_{k})
contains the indices of the units at risk at time
t_{k}
, i.e., the units not censored or failed right before the time
t_{k}
.

Freedman, 2008
gives a heuristic explanation of the partial likelihood based on the following results which extends Proposition
29.7
on the Exponential distribution.

Theorem 27.1

If
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}
are independent with hazard function
\lambda_{i}(t)

(i=1,\ldots,n)
, then their minimum value
\underline{T}=\min_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}
has hazard function
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(t)
.
Moreover, if
\lambda_{i}(t)=c_{i}\lambda(t)
, then

\textup{pr}(T_{i}=\underline{T})=\frac{c_{i}}{\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}c_{i^{\prime}}}.

Proof of Theorem
27.1
:

The survival function of
\underline{T}
is

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(\underline{T}>t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(T_{1}>t,\ldots,T_{n}>t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{i}(t),

so Proposition
27.2
implies that its hazard function is

\displaystyle-\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\log\textup{pr}(\underline{T}>t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{-\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\log S_{i}(t)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(t).

So the first conclusion follows.

As a byproduct of the above proof, the density of
\underline{T}
is
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(t)\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{i}(t)
based in Proposition
27.2
. It must have integral one; with
\lambda_{i}(t)=c_{i}\lambda(t)
, this implies

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda(t)\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{i}(t)\textnormal{d}t=1.

(180)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(T_{i}=\underline{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}\{T_{i}\leq T_{i^{\prime}}\text{ for all }i^{\prime}\neq i\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i^{\prime}\neq i}S_{i^{\prime}}(t)f_{i}(t)\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}S_{i^{\prime}}(t)\lambda_{i}(t)\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda(t)\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}S_{i^{\prime}}(t)\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{i}\Big/\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i^{\prime}},

where the last equality holds due to (
180
).

\square

Theorem
27.1
explains each of the
K
components in the partial likelihood. At time
t_{k}
, the units in
R(t_{k})
are all at risk, and unit
k
fails, assuming no ties. The probability that unit
k
has the smallest failure time among units in
R(t_{k})
is

\frac{\exp(x_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}{\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}

from Theorem
27.1
. The product in the partial likelihood is based on the independence of the events at the
K
failure times, which is more difficult to justify. A rigorous justification relies on the deeper theory of counting processes
(
Fleming and Harrington, 2011
)
or semiparametric statistics
(
Tsiatis, 2007
)
.

The log-likelihood function is

\log L(\beta)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left\{x_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta-\log\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\right\},

and the score function is

\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left\{x_{k}-\frac{\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)x_{l}}{\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)}\right\}.

Define

\pi_{\beta}(l\mid R_{k})=\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)/\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),\quad(l\in R(t_{k}))

which sum to one, so they induce a probability measure leading to
expectation
E_{\beta}(\cdot\mid R_{k})
and covariance
\textup{cov}_{\beta}(\cdot\mid R_{k})
.
With this notation, the score function simplifies to

\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left\{x_{k}-E_{\beta}(x\mid R_{k})\right\},

where

E_{\beta}(x\mid R_{k})=\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\pi_{l}(\beta\mid R_{k})x_{l};

the Hessian matrix simplifies to

\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\textup{cov}_{\beta}(x\mid R_{k})\preceq 0,

where

\displaystyle\textup{cov}_{\beta}(x\mid R_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)x_{l}x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\\
-\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)x_{l}\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{pmatrix}\Big/\left\{\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\exp(x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\pi_{\beta}(l\mid R_{k})x_{l}x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}-\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\pi_{\beta}(l\mid R_{k})x_{l}\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}\pi_{\beta}(l\mid R_{k})x_{l}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

The
coxph
function in the
R
package
survival
uses Newton’s method to compute the maximizer
\hat{\beta}
of the partial likelihood function, and uses the inverse of the observed Fisher information to approximate its asymptotic variance.

Lin and Wei, 1989
proposed a sandwich covariance estimator to allow for the misspecification of the Cox model. The
coxph
function with
robust

=

TRUE
reports the corresponding robust standard errors.

135.4
Log-rank test as a score test from Cox model

A standard problem in clinical trials is to compare the survival times under treatment and control. Assume no ties in the failure times, and let
x
denote the binary indicator for treatment.
Under the proportional hazards assumption, the
control group has hazard
\lambda_{0}(t)
, and the treatment group has
hazard
\lambda_{1}(t)=\lambda_{0}(t)e^{\beta}.
We are interested in testing the null hypothesis

\beta=0\Longleftrightarrow\lambda_{1}(t)=\lambda_{0}(t)\Longleftrightarrow S_{1}(t)=S_{0}(t).

Under the null hypothesis, the
score function reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log L(\beta)}{\partial\beta}\Big|_{\beta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left\{x_{k}-E_{\beta=0}(x\mid R_{k})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left(x_{k}-\frac{r_{k1}}{r_{k}}\right),

because

E_{\beta=0}(x\mid R_{k})=\frac{\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}x_{l}}{\sum_{l\in R(t_{k})}1}=\frac{r_{k1}}{r_{k}}

equaling the ratio of the number of treated units at risk
r_{k1}
over the
number of units at risk
r_{k}
, at time
t_{k}
. The Fisher information at the null is

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(0)}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\textup{cov}_{\beta=0}(x\mid R_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{r_{k1}}{r_{k}}\left(1-\frac{r_{k1}}{r_{k}}\right).

The score test for classical parametric models relies on

\frac{\partial\log L(0)}{\partial\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{a}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}\left(0,-\frac{\partial^{2}\log L(0)}{\partial\beta\partial\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right),

which follows from Bartlett’s identity and the CLT. Applying this fact to Cox’s model, we have

\text{LR}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left(x_{k}-\frac{r_{k1}}{r_{k}}\right)}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{r_{k1}}{r_{k}}\left(1-\frac{r_{k1}}{r_{k}}\right)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{a}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}(0,1).

So we reject the null at level
\alpha
if
|\text{LR}|
is larger than the upper
1-\alpha/2
quantile of standard Normal. This
is almost identical to the log-rank test without ties. Allowing for ties,
Mantel, 1966
proposed a more general form of the log-rank test
34
34

34

Peto and Peto, 1972
popularized the name log-rank test.
.

The
survdiff
function in the
survival
package implements various tests including the log-rank test as a special case. Below, I use the
gehan
dataset in the
MASS
package to illustrate the log rank test. The data were from a matched-pair experiment of 42 leukaemia patients
(
Gehan, 1965
)
. Treated units received the drug 6-mercaptopurine, and the rest are controls. For illustration purposes, I ignore the pair indicators.

The log-rank test is a standard tool in survival analysis. However, what it delivers is just a special case of the Cox proportional hazards model. The
p
-value from the log-rank test is close to the
p
-value from the score test of the Cox proportional hazards model with only a binary treatment indicator. The latter can also adjust for other pretreatment covariates.

136
Extensions

136.1
Stratified Cox model

Many randomized trials are stratified. The Combined Pharmacotherapies and Behavioral Interventions study reviewed at the beginning of this chapter is an example with
site
indicating the strata. The previous analysis includes the dummy variables of
site
in the Cox model. An alternative more flexible model is to allow for different baseline hazard functions across strata. Technically, assume

\lambda_{s}(t\mid x)=\lambda_{s}(t)\exp(\beta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x)

for strata
s=1,\ldots,S
, where
\beta
is an unknown parameter and
\{\lambda_{1}(\cdot),\ldots,\lambda_{S}(\cdot)\}
are unknown functions. Therefore, within each stratum
s
, the proportional hazards assumption holds; across strata, the proportional hazard assumption may not hold. Within stratum
s
, we can obtain the partial likelihood
L_{s}(\beta)
; by independence of the data across strata, we can obtain the joint partial likelihood

\prod_{s=1}^{S}L_{s}(\beta).

Based on the standard procedure, we can obtain the MLE and conduct inference based on the large-sample theory. The
coxph
function can naturally allow for stratification with the

+

strata
()
in the regression formula.

137
Critiques on survival analysis

The Kaplan–Meier curve and the Cox proportional hazards model are standard tools for analyzing medical data with censored survival times. They are among the most commonly used methods in medical journals.
Kaplan and Meier, 1958
and
Cox, 1972
are two of the most cited papers in statistics.

Freedman, 2008
criticized these two standard tools. Both rely on the critical assumption of noninformative censoring that censoring and survival time are independent or conditionally independent given covariates. When censoring is due to administrative constraints, this may be a plausible assumption. The data from
Lin et al., 2016
is a convincing example of noninformative censoring. However, many other studies have more complex censoring mechanisms, for example, one may drop out of the study, and another may be killed by an irrelevant cause. The Cox model relies on an additional assumption of proportional hazards. This particular functional form facilitates the interpretation of the coefficients as log conditional hazard ratios if the model is correctly specified. However, its interpretation becomes obscure when the model is mis-specified. Two survival curves based on
Lin et al., 2016
’s data cross each other, which makes the proportional hazards assumption dubious.

Hernán, 2010
offered a more fundamental critique on hazard-based survival analysis. For example, in a randomized treatment-control experiment, the hazard ratio at time
t
is the ratio of the instantaneous probability of death conditioning on the event that the patients have survived up to time
t
:

\frac{\lim_{\Delta t\downarrow 0}\textup{pr}(t\leq T<t+\Delta t\mid x=1,T\geq t)/\Delta t}{\lim_{\Delta t\downarrow 0}\textup{pr}(t\leq T<t+\Delta t\mid x=0,T\geq t)/\Delta t}

This ratio is difficult to interpret because patients who have survived up to time
t
can be quite different in treatment and control groups, especially when the treatment is effective. Even though patients are randomly assigned at the baseline, the survivors up to time
t
are not.
Hernán, 2010
suggested focusing on the comparison of the survival functions.

138
Homework problems

27.1
Identifiability of the survival time under independent censoring

Assume the survival time
T
and censoring time
C
are continuous and independent random variables. But we can only observe
y=\min(T,C)
and
\delta=1(T\leq C)
. Show that the hazard function of
T
can be identified by the following formula:

\lambda_{T}(t)=\frac{\textup{pr}(y=t,\delta=1)}{\textup{pr}(y\geq t)}.

27.2
Log-Normal regression model

Does the log-Normal regression model in Example
27.5
satisfy the proportional hazards assumption?
Based on

(y_{i},x_{i},\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, what is the likelihood function under Assumption
27.1
?
Compare it with the partial likelihood function.

27.3
Weibull random variable

Using (
178
) to show the formulas of density, survival, and hazard functions. Calculate its mean and variance.

Hint: Use the Gamma function to express the moments.

27.4
Weibull regression model

Find the distribution of

\varepsilon_{i}
in the Weibull regression model in Example
27.6
.
Show
E(T\mid x)
is log-linear in
x
, and
E(\log T\mid x)
is linear in
x
.
Does it satisfy the proportional hazards assumption? Based on

(y_{i},x_{i},\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, what is the likelihood function under Assumption
27.1
?
Compare it with the partial likelihood function.

27.5
Invariance of the proportional hazards model

Assume that
T\mid x
follows a proportional hazards model. Show
that any non-negative and strictly increasing transformation
g(T)\mid x
also follows a proportional
hazards model.

Part IX
Appendices

Chapter 28
Linear Algebra

All vectors are column vectors in this book. This is coherent with
R
.

Appendix 28.A
Basics of vectors and matrices

Euclidean space

The
n
-dimensional Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a set of
all
n
-dimensional vectors equipped with an inner product:

\langle x,y\rangle=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}

where
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
and
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

are two
n
-dimensional vectors. The length of a vector
x

is defined as

\|x\|=\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle}=\sqrt{x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x}.

The Cauchy–Schwarz
inequality states that

|\langle x,y\rangle|\leq\|x\|\cdot\|y\|,

or, more transparently,

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\right)^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{2}\right).

The equality holds if and only if
y_{i}=a+bx_{i}
for some
a
and
b
, for all
i=1,\ldots,n.
We can use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to prove the triangle inequality

\|x+y\|\leq\|x\|+\|y\|.

We say that
x
and
y
are
orthogonal
, denoted by
x\perp y
, if
\langle x,y\rangle=0
. We call a set of vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

orthonormal
if they all have unit length and are mutually orthogonal.

Geometrically, we can define the cosine of the angle between two vectors
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as

\cos\angle(x,y)=\frac{\langle x,y\rangle}{\|x\|\|y\|}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{2}}}.

For unit vectors, it reduces to the inner product.
When both
x
and
y
are orthogonal to
1_{n}
, that is,
\bar{x}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=0
and
\bar{y}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}=0
, the formula of the cosine of the angle is identical to the sample Pearson correlation coefficient

\hat{\rho}_{xy}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})(y_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}}.

Sometimes, we simply say that the cosine of the angle of two vectors measures their correlation even when they are not orthogonal to
1_{n}
.

Column space of a matrix

Given an
n\times m
matrix
A
, we can view it in terms of all elements

A=(a_{ij})=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}&\cdots&a_{1m}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
a_{n1}&\cdots&a_{nm}\end{pmatrix},

or row vectors

A=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
a_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{pmatrix},

where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

(i=1,\ldots,n)
, or column vectors

A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}),

where
A_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

j=1,\ldots,m
.
In statistics, the rows are corresponding to the units, so the
i
th row vector is the vector observations for unit
i
. Moreover, viewing
A
in terms of its column vectors can give more insights. Define the column space of
A
as

\mathcal{C}(A)=\left\{\alpha_{1}A_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{m}A_{m}:\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}\in\mathbb{R}\right\},

which is the set of all linear combinations of the column vectors
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
. The column space is important because we can write
A\alpha
, with
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
, as

A\alpha=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{m}\end{pmatrix}=\alpha_{1}A_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{m}A_{m}\in\mathcal{C}(A).

We define the row space of
A
as the column space of
A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
.

28.1
Matrix product

Given an
n\times m
matrix
A=(a_{ij})
and an
m\times r
matrix
B=(b_{ij})
, we can define their product as
C=AB
where the
n\times r
matrix
C=(c_{ij})
has the
(i,j)
th element

c_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{ik}b_{kj}.

In terms of the row vectors or column vectors of
A
and
B
, we have

c_{ij}=a_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}B_{j},

that is,
c_{ij}
equals the inner product of the
i
th row vector of
A
and the
j
th column vector of
B
. Moreover, the matrix product satisfies

AB=A(B_{1},\ldots,B_{r})=(AB_{1},\ldots,AB_{r})

(181)

so the column vectors of
AB
belongs to the column space of
A
; it also satisfies

AB=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
a_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{pmatrix}B=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}B\\
\vdots\\
a_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}B\end{pmatrix}

(182)

so the row vectors of
AB
belong to the column space of
B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
, or equivalently, the row space of
B
.

28.2
Linearly independent vectors and rank

We call a set of vectors
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

linearly independent
if

x_{1}A_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}A_{m}=0

must imply
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{m}=0
.
We call
A_{j_{1}},\ldots,A_{j_{k}}
maximally linearly independent if adding another vector makes them linearly dependent. Define
k
as the rank of of
\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}\}
and also define
k
as the rank of the matrix
A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})
.

A set of vectors may have different subsets of vectors that are maximally linearly independent. But the rank
k
is unique. We can also define the rank of a matrix in terms of its row vectors. A remarkable theorem in linear algebra is that it does not matter whether we define the rank of a matrix in terms of its column vectors or row vectors.

From the matrix product formulas (
181
) and (
182
), we have the following result.

Proposition 28.1

\textup{rank}(AB)\leq\min\{\textup{rank}(A),\textup{rank}(B)\}.

The rank decomposition of a matrix decomposes
A
into the product of two matrices of full ranks.

Proposition 28.2

If an
n\times m
matrix has rank
k
, then
A=BC
for some
n\times k
matrix
B
and
k\times m
matrix
C
.

Proof of Proposition
28.2
:

Let
A_{j_{1}},\ldots,A_{j_{k}}
be the maximally linearly independent column vectors of
A
. Stack them into an
n\times k
matrix
B=(A_{j_{1}},\ldots,A_{j_{k}})
. They can linearly represent all column vectors of
A
:

A=(c_{11}A_{j_{1}}+\cdots+c_{k1}A_{j_{k}},\ldots,c_{1m}A_{j_{1}}+\cdots+c_{km}A_{j_{k}})=(BC_{1},\ldots,BC_{m})=BC,

where
C=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{m})
is an
k\times m
matrix with column vectors

C_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}c_{11}\\
\vdots\\
c_{k1}\end{pmatrix},\quad C_{m}=\begin{pmatrix}c_{1m}\\
\vdots\\
c_{km}\end{pmatrix}.

\square

Proposition
28.1
ensures that rank
(B)\geq k
and rank
(C)\geq k
so they must both have rank
k
. The decomposition in Proposition
28.2
is not unique since the choice of the maximally linearly independent column vectors of
A
is not unique.

Some special matrices

An
n\times n
matrix
A
is symmetric if
A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=A
.
An
n\times n
diagonal matrix
A
has zero off-diagonal elements,
denoted by
A=\text{diag}\{a_{11},\ldots,a_{nn}\}.

Diagonal matrices are symmetric.

An
n\times n

matrix is orthogonal if
A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A=AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=I_{n}
. The column vectors of an orthogonal matrix are orthonormal; so are its row vectors. If
A
is orthogonal, then

\|Ax\|=\|x\|

for any vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. That is, multiplying a vector by an orthogonal matrix does not change the length of the vector. Geometrically, an orthogonal matrix corresponds to rotation.

An
n\times n
matrix
A
is upper triangular if
a_{ij}=0
for
i>j
and lower triangular if
a_{ij}=0
for
i<j
. An
n\times n
matrix
A
can be factorized as

A=LU

where
L
is a lower triangular matrix and
U
is an upper triangular matrix. This is called the LU decomposition of a matrix.

Determinant

The original definition of the determinant of a square matrix
A=(a_{ij})
has a very complex form, which will not be used in this book.

The determinant of a
2\times 2
matrix has a simple form:

\textup{det}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=ad-bc.

(183)

The determinant of the Vandermonde matrix has the following formula:

\textup{det}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{1}^{2}&\cdots&x_{1}^{n-1}\\
1&x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}&\cdots&x_{2}^{n-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
1&x_{n}&x_{n}^{2}&\cdots&x_{n}^{n-1}\end{pmatrix}=\prod_{1\leq i,j\leq n}(x_{j}-x_{i}).

(184)

The properties of the determinant are more useful. I will review two.

Proposition 28.3

For two square matrices
A
and
B
, we have

\textup{det}(AB)=\textup{det}(A)\textup{det}(B)=\textup{det}(BA).

Proposition 28.4

For two square matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we have

\textup{det}\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
C&B\end{pmatrix}=\textup{det}\begin{pmatrix}A&D\\
0&B\end{pmatrix}=\textup{det}(A)\textup{det}(B).

Inverse of a matrix

Let
I_{n}
be the
n\times n
identity matrix. An
n\times n

matrix
A
is invertible/nonsingular if there exists an
n\times n
matrix

B
such that
AB=BA=I_{n}.
We call
B
the inverse of
A
, denoted
by
A^{-1}.
If
A
is an orthogonal matrix, then
A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=A^{-1}.

A square matrix is invertible if and only if det
(A)\neq 0.

The inverse of a
2\times 2
matrix is

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\frac{1}{ad-bc}\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{pmatrix}.

(185)

The inverse of a
3\times 3
lower triangular matrix is

\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}a&0&0\\
b&c&0\\
d&e&f\end{array}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{acf}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}cf&0&0\\
-bf&af&0\\
be-cd&-ae&ac\end{array}\right).

(186)

A useful identity is

(AB)^{-1}=B^{-1}A^{-1}

if both
A
and
B
are invertible.

Eigenvalues and eigenvectors

For an
n\times n
matrix
A
, if there exists a pair of
n
-dimensional
vector
x
and a scalar
\lambda
such that

Ax=\lambda x,

then we call
\lambda
an eigenvalue and
x
the associated eigenvector of
A
. From
the definition, eigenvalue and eigenvector always come in pairs. The
following eigen-decomposition theorem is important for real symmetric
matrices.

Theorem 28.1

If
A
is an
n\times n
symmetric matrix, then there exists
an orthogonal matrix
P
such that

P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AP=\textup{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\},

where the
\lambda
’s are the
n
eigenvalues of
A
, and the column vectors of
P=(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n})
are the corresponding eigenvectors.

If we write the eigendecomposition as

AP=P\text{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}

or, equivalently,

A(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n})=(\lambda_{1}\gamma_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n}\gamma_{n}),

then
(\lambda_{i},\gamma_{i})
must be a pair of eigenvalue and eigenvector. Moreover, the eigendecomposition in Theorem
28.1
is unique up to the permutation of the columns of
P
and the corresponding
\lambda_{i}
’s.

Corollary 28.1

If
P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AP=\textup{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}
, then

A=P\textup{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}P^{\tiny\textsc{t}},\quad A^{k}=A\cdot A\cdots A=P\textup{diag}\{\lambda_{1}^{k},\ldots,\lambda_{n}^{k}\}P^{\tiny\textsc{t}};

if the eigenvalues of
A
are nonzero, then

A^{-1}=P\textup{diag}\{1/\lambda_{1},\ldots,1/\lambda_{n}\}P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

The eigen-decomposition is also useful for defining the square root
of an
n\times n
symmetric matrix. In particular, if the eigenvalues
of
A
are nonnegative, then we can define

A^{1/2}=P\text{diag}\{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{n}}\}P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

By definition,
A^{1/2}
is a symmetric matrix satisfying
A^{1/2}A^{1/2}=A.

There are other definitions of the square root of a symmetric matrix,
but we adopt this form in this book.

From Theorem
28.1
, we can write
A
as

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=P\text{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n})\text{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\gamma_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
\gamma_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\gamma_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

For an
n\times n
symmetric matrix
A
, its rank equals the number of non-zero eigenvalues
and its determinant equals the product of all eigenvalues. The matrix

A
is of full rank if all its eigenvalues are non-zero, which implies
that its rank equals
n
and its determinant is non-zero.

Quadratic form

For an
n\times n
symmetric matrix
A=(a_{ij})
and an
n
-dimensional
vector
x
, we can define the quadratic form as

x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax=\langle x,Ax\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}.

We always consider a symmetric matrix in the quadratic form without
loss of generality. Otherwise, we can symmetrize
A
as
\tilde{A}=(A+A^{\tiny\textsc{t}})/2

without changing the value of the quadratic form because

x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\tilde{A}x.

We call
A
positive semi-definite, denoted by
A\succeq 0,
if
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax\geq 0

for all
x
; we call
A
positive definite, denoted by
A\succ 0,

if
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax>0
for all nonzero
x.

We can also define the partial order between matrices. We call
A\succeq B

if and only if
A-B\succeq 0
, and we call
A\succ B
if and only
if
A-B\succ 0
. This is important in statistics because we often
compare the efficiency of estimators based on their variances or covariance matrices. Given two unbiased estimators
\hat{\theta}_{1}
and
\hat{\theta}_{2}
for a scalar parameter
\theta
, we say that
\hat{\theta}_{1}
is more efficient than
\hat{\theta}_{2}
if var
(\hat{\theta}_{2})\geq
var
(\hat{\theta}_{1})
. In the vector case, we say that
\hat{\theta}_{1}
is more efficient than
\hat{\theta}_{2}
if cov
(\hat{\theta}_{2})\succeq
cov
(\hat{\theta}_{1})
, which is equivalent to var
(\ell^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\theta}_{2})\geq
var
(\ell^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\theta}_{1})
for any linear combination of the estimators.

The eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix determine whether it is positive semi-definite or positive definite.

Theorem 28.2

For a symmetric matrix
A
, it is positive semi-definite
if and only if all its eigenvalues are nonnegative, and it is positive
definite if and only if all its eigenvalues are positive.

An important result is the relationship between the eigenvalues and the extreme values of the quadratic form. Assume that the eigenvalues are rearranged in decreasing order such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
.
For a unit vector
x
, we have that

x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\gamma_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}

where

\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x\\
\vdots\\
\gamma_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x\end{pmatrix}=P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x

has length
\|\alpha\|^{2}=\|x\|^{2}=1
.
Then the maximum value of
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax
is
\lambda_{1}
which is achieved at
\alpha_{1}=1
and
\alpha_{2}=\cdots=\alpha_{n}=0
(for example, if
x=\gamma_{1}
, then
\alpha_{1}=1
and
\alpha_{2}=\cdots=\alpha_{n}=0
). For a unit vector
x
that is orthogonal to
\gamma_{1}
, we have that

x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax=\sum_{i=2}^{n}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}

where
\alpha=P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x
has unit length with
\alpha_{1}=0
. The maximum value of
x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax
is
\lambda_{2}
which is achieved at
\alpha_{2}=1
and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{3}=\cdots=\alpha_{n}=0
, for example,
x=\gamma_{2}
. By induction, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 28.3

Suppose that an
n\times n
symmetric matrix has eigen-decomposition
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\gamma_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
where
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
.

1.

The optimization problem

\max_{x}x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax\text{ such that }\|x\|=1

has maximum
\lambda_{1}
which can be achieved by
\gamma_{1}
.

2.

The optimization problem

\max_{x}x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax\text{ such that }\|x\|=1,x\perp\gamma_{1}

has maximum
\lambda_{2}
which can be achieved by
\gamma_{2}
.

3.

The optimization problem

\max_{x}x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax\text{ such that }\|x\|=1,x\perp\gamma_{1},\ldots,x\perp\gamma_{k}

has maximum
\lambda_{k+1}
which can be achieved by
\gamma_{k+1}

(k=1,\ldots,n-1)
.

Theorem
28.3
implies the following theorem on the Rayleigh quotient

r(x)=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax/x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Theorem 28.4

(Rayleigh quotient and eigenvalues)
The maximum and minimum eigenvalues of an
n\times n
symmetric matrix
A
equals

\lambda_{\max}(A)=\max_{x\neq 0}r(x),\qquad\lambda_{\min}(A)=\min_{x\neq 0}r(x)

with the maximizer and minimizer being the eigenvectors corresponding to the maximum and minimum eigenvalues, respectively.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
28.4
is that the diagonal elements of
A
are bounded by the smallest and largest eigenvalues of
A
. This follows by taking
x=(0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
where only the
i
th element equals
1
.

Trace

The trace of an
n\times n
matrix
A=(a_{ij})
is the sum of all
its diagonal elements, denoted by

\text{trace}(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{ii}.

The trace operator has two important properties that can sometimes
help to simplify calculations.

Proposition 28.5

\textup{trace}(AB)=\textup{trace}(BA)
as long as
AB
and
BA

are both square matrices.

We can verify
Proposition
28.5
by definition. It states that
AB
and
BA
have the same trace although
AB
differs from
BA
in general. In fact, it is particularly useful if the dimension of
BA

is much lower than the dimension of
AB
. For example, if both
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

and
B=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
are vectors, then
\text{trace}(AB)=\text{trace}(BA)=\langle B^{\tiny\textsc{t}},A\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{i}.

Proposition 28.6

The trace of an
n\times n
symmetric matrix
A
equals the sum
of its eigenvalues:
\textup{trace}(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}
.

Proof of Proposition
28.6
:

It follows from the eigen-decomposition and Proposition
28.5
. Let
\Lambda=\text{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}
,
and we have

\text{trace}(A)=\text{trace}(P\Lambda P^{\tiny\textsc{t}})=\text{trace}(\Lambda P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}P)=\text{trace}(\Lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}.

\square

Projection matrix

An
n\times n
matrix
H
is a projection matrix if it is symmetric and
H^{2}=H.
The eigenvalues of
H
must be either
1
or
0
. To see this, we assume that
Hx=\lambda x
for some nonzero vector
x
, and use two ways to calculate
H^{2}x
:

H^{2}x=Hx=\lambda x,\quad H^{2}x=H(Hx)=H(\lambda x)=\lambda Hx=\lambda^{2}x.

So
(\lambda-\lambda^{2})x=0
which implies that
\lambda-\lambda^{2}=0
, i.e.,
\lambda=0
or
1
.
So the trace of

H
equals its rank:

\text{trace}(H)=\text{rank}(H).

Why is this a reasonable definition of a “projection matrix”? Or, why must a projection matrix
satisfy
H^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=H
and
H^{2}=H
? First, it is reasonable
to require that
Hx_{1}=x_{1}
for any
x_{1}\in\mathcal{C}(H)
,
the column space of
H.
Since
x_{1}=H\alpha
for some
\alpha
,
we indeed have
Hx_{1}=H(H\alpha)=H^{2}\alpha=H\alpha=x_{1}
because
of the property
H^{2}=H
. Second, it is reasonable to require that
x_{1}\perp x_{2}

for any vector
x_{1}=H\alpha\in\mathcal{C}(H)
and
x_{2}
such that
Hx_{2}=0
.
So we need
\alpha^{\tiny\textsc{t}}H^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{2}=0
which is true if
H=H^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

Therefore, the two conditions are natural for the definition of a
projection matrix.

More interestingly, a project matrix has a more explicit form as stated below.

Theorem 28.5

If an
n\times p
matrix
X
has
p
linearly independent columns, then
H=X(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
is a projection matrix. Conversely, if an
n\times n
matrix
H
is a projection matrix with rank
p
, then
H=X(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
for some
n\times p
matrix
X
with linearly independent columns.

It is relatively easy to verify the first part of Theorem
28.5
; see Chapter
3
. The second part of Theorem
28.5
follows from the eigen-decomposition of
H
, with the first
p
eigen-vectors being the column vectors of
X
.

Cholesky decomposition

An
n\times n
positive semi-definite matrix
A
can be decomposed
as
A=LL^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
where
L
is an
n\times n
lower triangular matrix
with non-negative diagonal elements.
If
A
is positive definite, the decomposition is unique. In general, it is not.
Take an arbitrary orthogonal
matrix
Q
, we have
A=LQQ^{\tiny\textsc{t}}L^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=CC^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
where
C=LQ
. So
we can decompose a positive semi-definite matrix
A
as
A=CC^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
, but
this decomposition is not unique.

Singular value decomposition (SVD)

Any
n\times m
matrix
A
can be decomposed as

A=UDV^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

where
U
is
n\times n
orthogonal matrix,
V
is
m\times m
orthogonal matrix, and
D
is
n\times m
matrix with all zeros for the non-diagonal elements.
For a tall matrix with
n\geq m
, the diagonal matrix
D
has many zeros, so we can also write

A=UDV^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

where
U
is
n\times m
matrix with orthonormal columns (
U^{\tiny\textsc{t}}U=I_{m}
),
V
is
m\times m
orthogonal matrix, and
D
is
m\times m
diagonal matrix. Similarly, for a wide matrix with
n\leq m
, we can write

A=UDV^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

where
U
is
n\times n
orthogonal matrix,
V
is
m\times n
matrix with orthonormal columns (
V^{\tiny\textsc{t}}V=I_{n}
), and
D
is
n\times n
diagonal matrix.

If
D
has only
r\leq\min(m,n)
nonzero elements, then we can further simplify the decomposition as

A=UDV^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

where
U
is
n\times r
matrix with orthonormal columns (
U^{\tiny\textsc{t}}U=I_{r}
),
V
is
m\times r
matrix with orthonormal columns (
V^{\tiny\textsc{t}}V=I_{r}
), and
D
is
r\times r
diagonal matrix. With more explicit forms of

U=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{r}),\quad D=\text{diag}(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}),\quad V=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{r}),

we can write
A
as

A=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{r})\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&d_{r}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}V_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\\
\vdots\\
V_{r}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}U_{k}V_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

The SVD implies that

AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=UDD^{\tiny\textsc{t}}U^{\tiny\textsc{t}},\quad A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A=VD^{\tiny\textsc{t}}DV^{\tiny\textsc{t}},

which are the eigen decompositions of
AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
and
A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A
. This ensures that
AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
and
A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A
have the same non-zero eigenvalues.

An application of the SVD is to define the pseudoinverse of any matrix. Define
D^{+}
as the pseudoinverse of
D
with the non-zero elements inverted but the zero elements intact at zero. Define

A^{+}=VD^{+}U^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}^{-1}V_{k}U_{k}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

as the pseudoinverse of
A
. The definition holds even if
A
is not a square matrix. We can verify that

AA^{+}A=A,\quad A^{+}AA^{+}=A^{+}.

If
A
is a square nondegenerate matrix, then
A^{+}=A^{-1}
equals the standard definition of the inverse.
In the special case with a symmetric
A
, its SVD is identical to its eigen decomposition. If
A
is not invertible, its pseudoinverse equals

A^{+}=P\text{diag}(\lambda_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}^{-1},0,\ldots,0)P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

if rank
(A)=k<n
and
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
are the nonzero eigen-values.

Another application of the SVD is the
polar decomposition
for any square matrix
A
. Since
A=UDV^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=UDU^{\tiny\textsc{t}}UV^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
with orthogonal
U
and
V
, we have

A=(AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}})^{1/2}\Gamma,

where
(AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}})^{1/2}\ =UDU^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
and
\Gamma=UV^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
is an orthogonal matrix.

Appendix 28.B
Vector calculus

If
f(x)
is a function from
\mathbb{R}^{p}
to
\mathbb{R}
, then we use the notation

\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_{p}}\end{pmatrix}

for the component-wise partial derivative, which must have the same dimension as
x
. It is often called the
gradient
of
f.

For example, for a linear function
f(x)=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}a=a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x
with
a,x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x}{\partial x}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x}{\partial x_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x}{\partial x_{p}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}x_{j}}{\partial x_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}x_{j}}{\partial x_{p}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\\
\vdots\\
a_{p}\end{pmatrix}=a;

(187)

for a quadratic function
f(x)=x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax
with a symmetric
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have

\frac{\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax}{\partial x}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax}{\partial x_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax}{\partial x_{p}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}}{\partial x_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}}{\partial x_{p}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}2a_{11}x_{1}+\cdots+2a_{1p}x_{p}\\
\vdots\\
2a_{p1}x_{1}+\cdots+2a_{pp}x_{p}\end{pmatrix}=2Ax.

These are two important rules of vector calculus used in this book, summarized below.

Proposition 28.7

We have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2Ax.

We can also extend the definition to vector functions. If
f(x)=(f_{1}(x),\ldots,f_{q}(x))^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
is a function from
\mathbb{R}^{p}
to
\mathbb{R}^{q}
, then we use the notation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x}\equiv\left(\frac{\partial f_{1}(x)}{\partial x},\cdots,\frac{\partial f_{q}(x)}{\partial x}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial f_{1}(x)}{\partial x_{1}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial f_{q}(x)}{\partial x_{1}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial f_{1}(x)}{\partial x_{p}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial f_{q}(x)}{\partial x_{p}}\end{pmatrix},

(188)

which is a
p\times q
matrix with rows corresponding to the elements of
x
and the columns corresponding to the elements of
f(x)
.
We can easily extend the first result of Proposition
28.7
.

Proposition 28.8

For
B\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have

\frac{\partial B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x}{\partial x}=B.

Proof of Proposition
28.8
:

Partition
B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{q})
in terms of its column vectors.
The
j
th element of
B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x
is
B_{j}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x
so the
j
-th column of
\partial B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x/\partial x
is
B_{j}
based on Proposition
28.7
. This verifies that
\partial B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x/\partial x
equals
B
.

\square

Some authors define
\partial f(x)/\partial x
as the transpose of (
188
). I adopt this form for its natural connection with
(
187
) when
q=1
.
Sometimes, it is indeed more convenient to work with the transpose of
\partial f(x)/\partial x
. Then I will use the notation

\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}=\left(\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x}\right)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=\left(\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_{1}},\cdots,\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_{p}}\right)

which puts the transpose notation on
x
.

The above formulas become more powerful in conjunction with the chain rule. For example, for any differentiable function
h(z)
mapping from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
with derivative
h^{\prime}(z)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial h(a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x)}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x)a,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial h(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax)}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2h^{\prime}(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax)Ax.

For any differentiable function
h(z)
mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{q}
to
\mathbb{R}
with gradient

\partial h(z)/\partial z
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial h(B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x)}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial h(B_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x,\ldots,B_{q}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x)}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{q}\frac{\partial h(B_{1}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x,\ldots,B_{q}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x)}{\partial z_{j}}B_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B\frac{\partial h(B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x)}{\partial z}.

Moreover, we can also define the Hessian matrix of a function
f(x)
mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{p}
to
\mathbb{R}
:

\frac{\partial^{2}f(x)}{\partial x\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}=\left(\frac{\partial^{2}f(x)}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq p}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\left(\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x}\right).

Appendix 28.C
Homework problems

28.1
Triangle inequality of the inner product

With three unit vectors
u,v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, prove that

\sqrt{1-\langle u,w\rangle}\leq\sqrt{1-\langle u,v\rangle}+\sqrt{1-\langle v,w\rangle}.

Remark: The result is a direct consequence of the standard triangle inequality but it has an interesting implication. If
\langle u,v\rangle\geq 1-\epsilon
and
\langle v,w\rangle\geq 1-\epsilon
, then
\langle u,w\rangle\geq 1-4\epsilon
. This implied inequality is mostly interesting when
\epsilon
is small. It states that when
u
and
v
are highly corrected and
v
and
w
are highly correlated, then
u
and
w
must also be highly correlated. Note that we can find counterexamples for the following relationship:

\langle u,v\rangle>0,\quad\langle v,w\rangle>0\quad\text{ but }\quad\langle u,w\rangle=0.

28.2
Van der Corput inequality

Assume that
v,u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
have unit length. Prove that

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\langle v,u_{i}\rangle\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle u_{i},u_{j}\rangle.

Remark: This result is not too difficult to prove but it says something fundamentally interesting. If
v
is correlated with many vectors
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
, then at least some vectors in
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
must be also correlated.

28.3
Inverse of a block matrix

Prove that

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A^{-1}+A^{-1}B(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}CA^{-1}&-A^{-1}B(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}\\
-(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}CA^{-1}&(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}&-(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}BD^{-1}\\
-D^{-1}C(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}&D^{-1}+D^{-1}C(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}BD^{-1}\end{array}\right),

provided that all the inverses of the matrices exist. The two forms of the inverse imply the Woodbury formula:

(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}=A^{-1}+A^{-1}B(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}CA^{-1},

which further implies the Sherman–Morrison formula:

(A+uv^{\tiny\textsc{t}})^{-1}=A^{-1}-(1+v^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A^{-1}u)^{-1}A^{-1}uv^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A^{-1},

where
A
is an invertible square matrix, and
u
and
v
are two column vectors.

28.4
Matrix determinant lemma

Prove that given the identity matrix
I_{n}
and two
n
-vectors
u
and
v
, we have

\text{det}(I_{n}+uv^{\tiny\textsc{t}})=1+v^{\tiny\textsc{t}}u.

Further show that if
I_{n}
is replaced by an
n\times n
invertible matrix
A
, we have

\text{det}(A+uv^{\tiny\textsc{t}})=(1+v^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A^{-1}u)\cdot\text{det}(A).

28.5
Decomposition of a positive semi-definite matrix

Show that if
A
is positive semi-definite, then there exists a matrix
C
such that
A=CC^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
.

28.6
Trace of the product of two matrices

Prove that
A
and
B
are two
n\times n
positive semi-definite matrices, then trace
(AB)\geq 0.

Hint: Use the eigen-decomposition of
A=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\gamma_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
.

Remark: In fact, a stronger result holds. If two
n\times n
symmetric matrices
A
and
B
have eigenvalues

\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n},\quad\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}

respectively, then

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\mu_{n+1-i}\leq\textup{trace}(AB)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}.

The result is
due to
Von Neumann, 1937
and
Ruhe, 1970
. See also
Chen and Li, 2019
.

28.7
Vector calculus

What is the formula for
\partial x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Ax/\partial x
if
A
is not symmetric in Proposition

28.7
?

Chapter 29
Random Variables

Let “IID” denote “independent and identically distributed”, “
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}
” denote a sequence of random variables that are IID with some common distribution, and “
” denote independence between random variables.
Define Euler’s Gamma function as

\Gamma(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{z-1}e^{-x}\textnormal{d}x,\qquad(z>0),

which is a natural extension of the factorial since
\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!
. Further define
the digamma function as
\psi(z)=\textup{d}\log\Gamma(z)/\textup{d}z
and the trigamma function as
\psi^{\prime}(z)
.
In
R
, we can use

⬇

lgamma
(
z
)

digamma
(
z
)

trigamma
(
z
)

to compute
\Gamma(z)
,
\log\Gamma(z)
,
\psi(z)
, and
\psi^{\prime}(z)
.

Appendix 29.A
Some important univariate random variables

29.A.1
Normal,
\chi^{2}
,
t
and
F

The standard Normal random variable
Z\sim\textsc{N}(0,1)
has density

f(z)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\exp\left(-z^{2}/2\right).

A Normal random variable
X
has mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
, denoted by
\textsc{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
,
if
X=\mu+\sigma Z
. We can show that
X
has density

f(x)=(2\pi)^{1/2}\exp\left\{-(x-\mu)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})\right\}.

A chi-squared random variable with degrees of freedom
n,
denoted
by
Q_{n}\sim\chi_{n}^{2},
can be represented as

Q_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{2},

where
Z_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}(0,1)
. We can show that its density is

f_{n}(q)=q^{n/2}\exp(-q/2)\Big/\left\{2^{n/2}\Gamma(n/2)\right\},\qquad(q>0).

(189)

We can verify that the above density (
189
) is well-defined even if we change
the integer
n
to be an arbitrary positive real number
\nu
, and
call the corresponding random variable
Q_{\nu}
a chi-squared random
variable with degrees of freedom
\nu
, denoted by
Q_{\nu}\sim\chi^{2}_{\nu}
.

A
t
random variable with degrees of freedom
\nu
can be represented
as

t_{\nu}=\frac{Z}{\sqrt{Q_{\nu}/\nu}}

where
Z\sim\textsc{N}(0,1),Q_{\nu}\sim\chi_{\nu}^{2}
,
and
Z\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Q_{\nu}.

An
F
random variable with degrees of freedom
(r,s)
can be represented
as

F=\frac{Q_{r}/r}{Q_{s}/s}

where
Q_{r}\sim\chi_{r}^{2},Q_{s}\sim\chi_{s}^{2}
, and
Q_{r}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Q_{s}
.

29.A.2
Beta–Gamma duality

The
\text{Gamma}(\alpha,\beta)
random variable with parameters

\alpha,\beta>0
has density

f(x)=\frac{\beta^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}x^{\alpha-1}e^{-\beta x},\quad(x>0).

(190)

The
\text{Beta}(\alpha,\beta)
random variable with parameters
\alpha,\beta>0

has density

f(x)=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}x^{\alpha-1}(1-x)^{\beta-1},\quad(0<x<1).

These two random variables are closely related as shown in the following
theorem.

Theorem 29.1

If
X\sim\textup{Gamma}(\alpha,\theta),Y\sim\textup{Gamma}(\beta,\theta)

and
X\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y
, then

1.

X+Y\sim\textup{Gamma}(\alpha+\beta,\theta),

2.

X/(X+Y)\sim\textup{Beta}(\alpha,\beta),

3.

X+Y\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}X/(X+Y).

Another simple but useful fact is that
\chi^{2}
is a special Gamma
random variable. Comparing the densities in (
189
) and
(
190
), we obtain the following result.

Proposition 29.1

\chi_{n}^{2}\sim\textup{Gamma}(n/2,1/2).

We can also calculate the moments of the Gamma and Beta distributions.

Proposition 29.2

If
X\sim\textup{Gamma}(\alpha,\beta)
,
then

\displaystyle E(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{\beta},

\displaystyle\textup{var}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{\beta^{2}}.

Proposition 29.3

If
X\sim\textup{Gamma}(\alpha,\beta)
,
then

\displaystyle E(\log X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi(\alpha)-\log\beta,

\displaystyle\textup{var}(\log X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\alpha).

Proposition 29.4

If
X\sim\textup{Beta}(\alpha,\beta),
then

\displaystyle E(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta},

\displaystyle\textup{var}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\beta}{(\alpha+\beta)(\alpha+\beta+1)}.

Proposition 29.5

If
X\sim\textup{Beta}(\alpha,\beta),
then

\displaystyle E(\log X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi(\alpha)-\psi(\alpha+\beta),

\displaystyle\textup{var}(\log X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\alpha)-\psi^{\prime}(\alpha+\beta).

I leave the proofs of the above propositions as Problem
29.2
.

29.A.3
Exponential, Laplace, and Gumbel distributions

An Exponential
(\lambda)
random variable
X\geq 0
has density
f(x)=\lambda e^{-\lambda x}
, mean
1/\lambda
, median
\log 2/\lambda
and variance
1/\lambda^{2}
. The standard Exponential random variable
X_{0}
has
\lambda=1
, and
X_{0}/\lambda
generates Exponential
(\lambda)
.

An important feature of Exponential
(\lambda)
is the memoryless property.

Proposition 29.6

If
X\sim
Exponential
(\lambda)
, then

\textup{pr}(X\geq x+c\mid X\geq c)=\textup{pr}(X\geq x).

If
X
represents the survival time, then the probability of surviving another
x
time is always the same no matter how long the existing survival time is.

Proof of Proposition
29.6
:

Because
\textup{pr}(X>x)=e^{-\lambda x}
, we have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(X\geq x+c\mid X\geq c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{pr}(X\geq x+c)}{\textup{pr}(X\geq c)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\lambda(x+c)}}{e^{-\lambda c}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(X\geq x).

\square

The minimum of independent exponential random variables also follows an exponential distribution.

Proposition 29.7

Assume that
X_{i}\sim\text{Exponential}(\lambda_{i})
are independent
(i=1,\ldots,n)
. Then

\underline{X}=\min(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\sim\textup{Exponential}(\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n})

and

\textup{pr}(X_{i}=\underline{X})=\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}}.

Proof of Proposition
29.7
:

First,

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(\underline{X}>x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(X_{i}>x,i=1,\ldots,n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\textup{pr}(X_{i}>x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\lambda_{i}x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}x}

so
\underline{X}
is Exponential
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i})
.

Second, we have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(X_{i}=\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\textup{pr}(X_{i}<X_{j}\text{ for all }j\neq i)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j\neq i}\textup{pr}(X_{j}>x)\lambda_{i}e^{-\lambda_{i}x}\textnormal{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j\neq i}e^{-\lambda_{j}x}\lambda_{i}e^{-\lambda_{i}x}\textnormal{d}x

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\lambda_{j}x}\textnormal{d}x

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}x}\textnormal{d}x

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}/\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}.

\square

The difference between two IID exponential random variables follows the Laplace distribution

Proposition 29.8

If
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are two IID Exponential
(\lambda)
, then
y=y_{1}-y_{2}
has density

\frac{\lambda}{2}\exp(-\lambda|c|),\quad-\infty<c<\infty

which is the density of a Laplace distribution with mean
0
and variance
2/\lambda^{2}
.

Proof of Proposition
29.8
:

Both
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
have density
f(c)=\lambda e^{-\lambda c}

and CDF
F(c)=1-e^{-\lambda c}
. The CDF of
y=y_{1}-y_{2}
at
c\geq 0

is

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{1}-y_{2}\leq c)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{pr}(y_{2}\geq z-c)\lambda e^{-\lambda z}\text{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda(z-c)}\lambda e^{-\lambda z}\text{d}z

\displaystyle=\lambda e^{\lambda c}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\lambda z}\text{d}z

\displaystyle=\lambda e^{\lambda c}/(2\lambda)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda c}/2.

By symmetry,
y_{1}-y_{2}\sim y_{2}-y_{1}
, so the CDF at
c\leq 0

is

\displaystyle\textup{pr}(y_{1}-y_{2}\leq c)

\displaystyle=1-\textup{pr}(y_{1}-y_{2}\leq-c)

\displaystyle=1-e^{-\lambda c}/2.

Therefore, the density of
y
at
c\geq 0
is

\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{\lambda c},

and the density of
y
at
c\leq 0
is

\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{-\lambda c},

which can be unified as

\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{\lambda|c|}.

\square

If
X_{0}
is the standard exponential random variable, then we define the Gumbel
(\mu,\beta)
random variable as

Y=\mu-\beta\log X_{0}.

The standard Gumbel distribution has
\mu=0
and
\beta=1
, with
CDF

F(y)=\exp(-e^{-y}),\quad y\in\mathbb{R}

and density

f(y)=\exp(-e^{-y})e^{-y},\quad y\in\mathbb{R}.

By definition and Proposition
29.7
, we can verify that the maximum of IID Gumbels is also Gumbel.

Proposition 29.9

If
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are IID Gumbel
(\mu,\beta)
, then

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}Y_{i}\sim\textup{Gumbel}(\mu+\beta\log n,\beta).

If
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are independent Gumbel
(\mu_{i},1)
, then

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}Y_{i}\sim\textup{Gumbel}\left(\log\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\mu_{i}},1\right).

I leave the proof as Problem
29.3
.

Appendix 29.B
Multivariate distributions

A random vector
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
is a vector consisting of
n
random
variables. If all components are continuous, we can define its joint
density
f_{X_{1}\cdots X_{n}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}).

For a random vector
\binom{X}{Y}
with
X
and
Y
possibly being
vectors, if it has joint density
f_{XY}(x,y)
, then we can obtain
the marginal distribution of
X

f_{X}(x)=\int f_{XY}(x,y)\textnormal{d}y

and define the conditional density

f_{Y|X}(y\mid x)=\frac{f_{XY}(x,y)}{f_{X}(x)}\qquad\text{if }f_{X}(x)\neq 0.

Based on the conditional density, we can define the conditional expectation
of any function of
Y
as

E\left\{g(Y)\mid X=x\right\}=\int g(y)f_{Y|X}(y\mid x)\textnormal{d}y

and the conditional variance as

\textup{var}\left\{g(Y)\mid X=x\right\}=E\left[\left\{g(Y)\right\}^{2}\mid X=x\right]-\left[E\left\{g(Y)\mid X=x\right\}\right]^{2}.

In the above definitions, the conditional mean and variance are both deterministic
functions of
x
. We can replace
x
by the random variable
X
to define

E\left\{g(Y)\mid X\right\}

and
\textup{var}\left\{g(Y)\mid X\right\}
, which are functions of the
random variable
X
and are thus random variables.

Below are two important laws of conditional expectation and variance.

Theorem 29.2
(Law of total expectation)

We have

E(Y)=E\left\{E(Y\mid X)\right\}.

Theorem 29.3
(Law of total variance or analysis of variance)

We have

\textup{var}(Y)=E\left\{\textup{var}(Y\mid X)\right\}+\textup{var}\left\{E(Y\mid X)\right\}.

Independence

Random variables
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
are mutually independent
if

f_{X_{1}\cdots X_{n}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=f_{X_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots f_{X_{n}}(x_{n}).

Note that in this definition, each of
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
can
be vectors. We have the following rules under independence.

Proposition 29.10

If
X\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y
, then
h(X)\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}g(Y)
for any functions

h(\cdot)
and
g(\cdot)
.

Proposition 29.11

If
X\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y
, then

\displaystyle f_{XY}(x,y)

\displaystyle=f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(y),

\displaystyle f_{Y\mid X}(y\mid x)

\displaystyle=f_{Y}(y),

\displaystyle E\left\{g(Y)\mid X\right\}

\displaystyle=E\left\{g(Y)\right\},

\displaystyle E\left\{g(Y)h(X)\right\}

\displaystyle=E\left\{g(Y)\right\}E\left\{h(X)\right\}.

Expectations of random vectors or random matrices

For a random matrix
W=(W_{ij})
, we define
E(W)=(E(W_{ij}))
.
For constant matrices
A
and
C
, we can verify that

\displaystyle E(AW+C)

\displaystyle=AE(W)+C,

\displaystyle E(AWC)

\displaystyle=AE(W)C.

Covariance between two random vectors

If
W\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{s},
then their covariance

\textup{cov}(W,Y)=E\left[\left\{W-E(W)\right\}\left\{Y-E(Y)\right\}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right]

is an
r\times s
matrix.
As a special case,

\textup{cov}(Y)=\textup{cov}(Y,Y)=E\left[\left\{Y-E(Y)\right\}\left\{Y-E(Y)\right\}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right]=E(YY^{\tiny\textsc{t}})-E(Y)E(Y)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

For a scalar random variable,
\textup{cov}(Y)=\textup{var}(Y).

Proposition 29.12

For
A\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

C\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, we have
\textup{cov}(AY+C)=A\textup{cov}(Y)A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
.

Using Proposition
29.12
,
we can verify that for any
n
-dimensional random vector,
\textup{cov}(Y)\succeq 0

because for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\textup{cov}(Y)x=\textup{cov}(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y)=\textup{var}(x^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y)\geq 0.

Proposition 29.13

For two random vectors
W
and
Y
, we have

\textup{cov}(AW+C,BY+D)=A\textup{cov}(W,Y)B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

and

\textup{cov}(AW+BY)=A\textup{cov}(W)A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}+B\textup{cov}(Y)B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}+A\textup{cov}(W,Y)B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}+B\textup{cov}(Y,W)A^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

Similar to Theorem
29.3
, we have the following decomposition of the covariance.

Theorem 29.4
(Law of total covariance)

We have

\textup{cov}\left(Y,W\right)=E\left\{\textup{cov}\left(Y,W\mid X\right)\right\}+\textup{cov}\left\{E(Y\mid X),E(W\mid X)\right\}.

Appendix 29.C
Multivariate Normal and its properties

I use a generative definition of the multivariate Normal random vector.
First,
Z
is a standard Normal random vector if
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

has components
Z_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}(0,1)
. Given a mean vector
\mu
and a
positive semi-definite covariance matrix
\Sigma
, define a Normal
random vector
Y\sim\text{N($\mu,\Sigma$)}
