with mean

\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
if
Y
can be represented as

\displaystyle Y=\mu+AZ,

(191)

where
A
satisfies
\Sigma=AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
. We can verify that
\textup{cov}(Y)=\Sigma
,
so indeed
\Sigma
is its covariance matrix. If
\Sigma\succ 0
,
then we can verify that
Y
has density

f_{Y}(y)=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\left\{\det(\Sigma)\right\}^{-1/2}\exp\left\{-(y-\mu)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}(y-\mu)/2\right\}.

(192)

We can easily verify the following result by calculating the density.

Proposition 29.14

If
Z\sim\textup{N}(0,I_{n})
and
\Gamma
is an orthogonal matrix, then

\Gamma Z\sim\textsc{N}(0,I_{n}).

I do not define multivariate Normal based on the density (
192
) because it is only well defined with a positive definite
\Sigma
. I do not define multivariate Normal based on the characteristic function because it is more advanced than the level of this book.
Definition (
191
) does not require
\Sigma
to be positive definite and is more elementary.
However, it has a subtle issue of uniqueness. Although
the decomposition
\Sigma=AA^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
is not unique, the resulting distribution

Y=\mu+AZ
is. We can verify this using the Polar decomposition.
Because
A=\Sigma^{1/2}\Gamma
where
\Gamma
is an orthogonal matrix,
we have
Y=\mu+\Sigma^{1/2}\Gamma Z=\mu+\Sigma^{1/2}\tilde{Z}
where

\tilde{Z}=\Gamma Z
is a standard Normal random vector by Proposition
29.14
.
Importantly, although the definition (
191
) can be general, we usually use the following representation

Y=\mu+\Sigma^{1/2}Z.

Theorem 29.5

Assume that

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{array}\right)\sim\textup{N}\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Sigma_{11}&\Sigma_{12}\\
\Sigma_{21}&\Sigma_{22}\end{array}\right)\right).

Then
Y_{1}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y_{2}
if and only if
\Sigma_{12}=0.

I leave the proof of Theorem
29.5
as Problem
29.4
.

Proposition 29.15

If
Y\sim\textup{N($\mu,$$\Sigma$)},
then
BY+C\sim\textup{N$(B\mu+C,B\Sigma B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}$)}
,
that is, any linear transformation of a Normal random vector is also
a Normal random vector.

Proof of Proposition
29.15
:

By definition,
Y=\mu+\Sigma^{1/2}Z
where
Z
is the standard Normal
random vector, we have

\displaystyle BY+c

\displaystyle=B(\mu+\Sigma^{1/2}Z)+C

\displaystyle=B\mu+C+B\Sigma^{1/2}Z

\displaystyle\sim\text{N}(B\mu+C,B\Sigma^{1/2}\Sigma^{1/2\tiny\textsc{t}}B^{\tiny\textsc{t}})

\displaystyle\sim\text{N}(B\mu+C,B\Sigma B^{\tiny\textsc{t}}).

\square

An obvious corollary of Proposition
29.15
is that if
X_{1}\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})
and
X_{2}\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2})
are independent, then
X_{1}+X_{2}\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})
. So the summation of two independent Normals is also Normal. Remarkably, the reverse of the result is also true.

Theorem 29.6
(Levy–Cramer)

If
X_{1}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}X_{2}
and
X_{1}+X_{2}
is Normal, then both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
must be Normal.

The statement of Theorem
29.6
is extremely simple. But its proof is non-trivial and beyond the scope of this book. See
Benhamou et al., 2018
for a proof.

Theorem 29.7

Assume

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{array}\right)\sim\textup{N}\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Sigma_{11}&\Sigma_{12}\\
\Sigma_{21}&\Sigma_{22}\end{array}\right)\right).

1.

The marginal distributions are Normal:

\displaystyle Y_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\textup{N}\left(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{11}\right),

\displaystyle Y_{2}

\displaystyle\sim\textup{N}\left(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{22}\right).

2.

If
\Sigma_{22}\succ 0,
then the conditional distribution is Normal:

Y_{1}\mid Y_{2}=y_{2}\sim\textup{N}\left(\mu_{1}+\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}(y_{2}-\mu_{2}),\Sigma_{11}-\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}\Sigma_{21}\right);

Y_{2}
is independent of the residual

Y_{1}-\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}(Y_{2}-\mu_{2})\sim\textup{N}\left(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{11}-\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}\Sigma_{21}\right).

I review some other results of the multivariate Normal below.

Proposition 29.16

Assume
Y\sim\textup{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}I_{n})
. If
AB^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=0
, then

AY\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}BY
.

Proposition 29.17

Assume

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{array}\right)\sim\textup{N}\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{1}^{2}&\rho\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\\
\rho\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}&\sigma_{2}^{2}\end{array}\right)\right),

where
\rho
is the correlation coefficient defined as

\rho=\frac{\textup{cov}(Y_{1},Y_{2})}{\sqrt{\textup{var}(Y_{1})\textup{var}(Y_{2})}}.

Then the conditional distribution is

Y_{1}\mid Y_{2}=y_{2}\sim\textup{N}\left(\mu_{1}+\rho\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{2}}(y_{2}-\mu_{2}),\sigma_{1}^{2}(1-\rho^{2})\right).

Appendix 29.D
Quadratic forms of random vectors

Given a random vector
Y
and a symmetric matrix
A
, we can define
the quadratic form
Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY
, which is a random variable playing
an important role in statistics. The first theorem is about its mean.

Theorem 29.8

If
Y
has mean
\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
, then

E(Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY)=\textup{trace}(A\Sigma)+\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A\mu.

Proof of Theorem
29.8
:

The proof relies on the following two basic facts.

•

E(YY^{\tiny\textsc{t}})=\textup{cov}(Y)+E(Y)E(Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}})=\Sigma+\mu\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}}.

•

For an
n\times n
symmetric random matrix
W=(w_{ij})
, we have
E\left\{\text{trace}(W)\right\}=\text{trace}\left\{E(W)\right\}
because
E\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{ii}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(w_{ii}).

The conclusion follows from

\displaystyle E(Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY)

\displaystyle=E\left\{\text{trace}(Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY)\right\}

\displaystyle=E\left\{\text{trace}(AYY^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\right\}

\displaystyle=\text{trace}\left\{E(AYY^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\right\}

\displaystyle=\text{trace}\left\{AE(YY^{T})\right\}

\displaystyle=\text{trace}\left\{A(\Sigma+\mu\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}})\right\}

\displaystyle=\text{trace}\left(A\Sigma+A\mu\mu^{T}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{trace}(A\Sigma)+\text{trace}(\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A\mu)

\displaystyle=\text{trace}(A\Sigma)+\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A\mu.

\square

The variance of the quadratic form is much more complicated for a general
random vector. For the multivariate Normal random vector, we have the
following formula.

Theorem 29.9

If
Y\sim\textup{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
,
then

\textup{var}(Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY)=2\textup{trace}(A\Sigma A\Sigma)+4\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A\Sigma A\mu.

I relegate the proof as Problem
29.10
.

From its definition,
\chi_{n}^{2}
is the summation of the squares of
n
IID standard Normal random variables. It is closely related to
quadratic forms of multivariate Normals.

Theorem 29.10

1.

If
Y\sim\textup{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
is an
n
-dimensional random vector
with
\Sigma\succ 0
, then

(Y-\mu)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{-1}(Y-\mu)\sim\chi_{n}^{2}.

If rank
(\Sigma)=k\leq n
, then

(Y-\mu)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{+}(Y-\mu)\sim\chi_{k}^{2}.

2.

If
Y\sim\textup{N}(0,I_{n})
and
H
is a projection matrix of rank

K
, then

Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}HY\sim\chi_{K}^{2}.

3.

If
Y\sim\textup{N}(0,H)
where
H
is a projection matrix of rank

K
, then

Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y\sim\chi_{K}^{2}.

Proof of Theorem
29.10
:

1.

I only prove the general result with rank
(\Sigma)=k\leq n
.
By definition,
Y=\mu+\Sigma^{1/2}Z
where
Z
is a standard Normal
random vector, then

\displaystyle(Y-\mu)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{+}(Y-\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\Sigma^{1/2}\Sigma^{+}\Sigma^{1/2}Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}^{2}\sim\chi_{k}^{2}.

2.

Using the eigendecomposition of the projection matrix

H=P\text{diag}\left\{1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0\right\}P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

with
K

1
’s in the diagonal matrix, we have

\displaystyle Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}HY

\displaystyle=Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}P\text{diag}\left\{1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0\right\}P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y

\displaystyle=Z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\text{diag}\left\{1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0\right\}Z,

where
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y\sim\text{N}(0,P^{\tiny\textsc{t}}P)=\text{N}(0,I_{n})

is a standard Normal random vector. So

Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}HY=\sum_{i=1}^{K}Z_{i}^{2}\sim\chi_{K}^{2}.

3.

Writing
Y=H^{1/2}Z
where
Z
is a standard Normal random vector,
we have

Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y=Z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}H^{1/2}H^{1/2}Z=Z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}HZ\sim\chi_{K}^{2}

using the second result.

\square

Appendix 29.E
Homework problems

29.1
Beta-Gamma duality

Prove Theorem
29.1
.

Hint: Calculate the joint density of
(X+Y,X/(X+Y))
.

29.2
Gamma and Beta moments

Prove Propositions
29.2
–
29.5
.

29.3
Maximums of Gumbels

Prove Proposition
29.9
.

29.4
Independence and uncorrelatedness in the multivariate Normal

Prove Theorem
29.5
.

29.5
Transformation of bivariate Normal

Prove that if
(Y_{1},Y_{2})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
follows
a bivariate Normal distribution

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{array}\right)\sim\textup{N}\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\rho\\
\rho&1\end{array}\right)\right),

then

Y_{1}+Y_{2}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y_{1}-Y_{2}.

Remark: This result holds for arbitrary
\rho
.

29.6
Normal conditional distributions

Suppose that
(X_{1},X_{2})
has the joint distribution

f_{X_{1}X_{2}}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\propto C_{0}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\left(Ax_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}-2Bx_{1}x_{2}-2C_{1}x_{1}-2C_{2}x_{2}\right)\right\},

where
C_{0}
is the normalizing constant depending on
(A,B,C_{1},C_{2})
. To ensure that this is a well-defined density, we need
A\geq 0
, and if
A=0
then
|B|<1
. Prove that the conditional distributions are

\displaystyle X_{1}\mid X_{2}=x_{2}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\textsc{N}\left(\frac{Bx_{2}+C_{1}}{Ax_{2}^{2}+1},\frac{1}{Ax_{2}^{2}+1}\right),

\displaystyle X_{2}\mid X_{1}=x_{1}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\textsc{N}\left(\frac{Bx_{1}+C_{2}}{Ax_{1}^{2}+1},\frac{1}{Ax_{1}^{2}+1}\right).

Remark:
For a bivariate Normal distribution, the two conditional distributions are both Normal. The converse of the statement is not true. That is, even if the two conditional distributions are both Normal, the joint distribution may not be bivariate Normal.

Gelman and Meng, 1991
reported this interesting result.

29.7
Inverse of covariance matrix and conditional independence in multivariate Normal

Assume
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\sim\textsc{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
. Denote the inverse of its covariance matrix by
\Sigma^{-1}=(\sigma^{jk})_{1\leq j,k\leq p}
. Show that for any pair of
j\neq k
, we have

\sigma^{jk}=0\Longleftrightarrow X_{j}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}X_{k}\mid X_{\backslash(j,k)}

where
X_{\backslash(j,k)}
contains all the variables except
X_{j}
and
X_{k}
.

29.8
Independence of linear and quadratic functions of the multivariate Normal

Assume
Y\sim\text{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}I_{n})
. For an
n
dimensional
vector
a
and two
n\times n
symmetric matrices
A
and
B
, show that

1.

if
Aa=0
, then
a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY
;

2.

if
AB=BA=0
, then
Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}BY
.

Hint: To simplify the proof, you can the pseudoinverse of
A
which satisfies
AA^{+}A=A
.
In fact, a strong result holds.
Ogasawara and Takahashi, 1951
proved the following theorem; see also
Styan, 1970
.

Theorem 29.11

Assume
Y\sim\text{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
. Define quadratic forms
Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY
and
Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}BY
for two symmetric matrices
A
and
B
. The
Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}AY
and
Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}BY
are independent if and only if

\Sigma A\Sigma B\Sigma=0,\quad\Sigma A\Sigma B\mu=\Sigma B\Sigma A\mu=0,\quad\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A\Sigma B\mu=0.

29.9
Independence of the sample mean and variance of IID Normals

If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}),
then
\bar{X}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}S^{2}
,
where
\bar{X}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
and
S^{2}=(n-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\bar{X})^{2}
.

Remark: A remarkable result due to
Geary, 1936
ensures the reverse of the above result. That is, if
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are IID and
\bar{X}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}S^{2}
, then
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
must be Normals. See
Lukacs, 1942
and
Benhamou et al., 2018
for proofs.

29.10
Variance of the quadratic form of the multivariate Normal

Prove Theorem
29.9
. Use it to further prove that if
Y\sim\textup{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
, then

\textup{cov}(Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A_{1}Y,Y^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A_{2}Y)=2\textup{trace}(A_{1}\Sigma A_{2}\Sigma)+4\mu^{\tiny\textsc{t}}A_{1}\Sigma A_{2}\mu.

Hint:
Write
Y=\mu+\Sigma^{1/2}Z
and reduce the problem to calculating the moments of standard Normals.

Chapter 30
Limiting Theorems and Basic Asymptotics

This chapter reviews the basics of limiting theorems and asymptotic analyses that are useful for this book. See
Newey and McFadden, 1994
and
Van der Vaart, 2000
for in-depth discussions.

Appendix 30.A
Convergence in probability

Definition 30.2

Random vectors
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
converge to
Z
in probability,
denoted by
Z_{n}\rightarrow Z
in probability, if

\textup{pr}\left\{\|Z_{n}-Z\|>c\right\}\rightarrow 0,\quad n\rightarrow\infty

for all
c>0
.

This definition incorporates the classic definition of convergence
of non-random vectors:

Proposition 30.1

If non-random vectors
Z_{n}\rightarrow Z
, the convergence also
holds in probability.

Convergence in probability for random vectors is equivalent to element-wise
convergence because of the following result:

Proposition 30.2

If
Z_{n}\rightarrow Z
and
W_{n}\rightarrow W
in probability,
then
(Z_{n},W_{n})\rightarrow(Z,W)
in probability.

The above proposition does not require any conditions on the joint distribution of
(Z_{n},W_{n})
.

For an IID sequence of random vectors, we have the following weak law of
large numbers:

Proposition 30.3
(Khintchine’s weak law of large numbers)

If
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
are
IID
with mean
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{K},

then
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\rightarrow\mu
in probability.

A more elementary tool is Markov’s inequality:

\textup{pr}\left\{\|Z_{n}-Z\|>c\right\}\leq E\left\{\|Z_{n}-Z\|\right\}/c

(193)

or

\textup{pr}\left\{\|Z_{n}-Z\|>c\right\}\leq E\left\{\|Z_{n}-Z\|^{2}\right\}/c^{2}.

(194)

Inequality (
193
) is useful if
E\left\{\|Z_{n}-Z\|\right\}

converges to zero, and inequality (
194
) is useful
if
E\left\{\|Z_{n}-Z\|^{2}\right\}
converges to zero. The latter
gives a standard tool for establishing convergence in probability
by showing that the covariance matrix converges to zero.

Proposition 30.4

If random vectors
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
have mean zero and covariance

\textup{cov}(Z_{n})=a_{n}C_{n}
where
a_{n}\rightarrow 0
and
C_{n}\rightarrow C<\infty
,
then
Z_{n}\rightarrow 0
in probability.

Proof of Proposition
30.4
:

Using (
194
), we have

\displaystyle\textup{pr}\left\{\|Z_{n}\|>c\right\}

\displaystyle\leq c^{-2}E\left\{\|Z_{n}\|^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=c^{-2}E\left(Z_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Z_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=c^{-2}\text{trace}\left\{E\left(Z_{n}Z_{n}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=c^{-2}\text{trace}\left\{\textup{cov}(Z_{n})\right\}

\displaystyle=c^{-2}a_{n}\text{trace}(C_{n})\rightarrow 0,

which implies that
Z_{n}\rightarrow 0
in probability.

\square

For example, we usually use Proposition
30.4
to show the weak law of large numbers for the sample mean of independent random variables
\bar{Z}_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
. If we can show that

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\bar{Z}_{n})=n^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textup{cov}(Z_{i})\rightarrow 0,

(195)

then we can conclude that
\bar{Z}_{n}-n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(Z_{i})\rightarrow 0
in probability. The condition in (
195
) holds if
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textup{cov}(Z_{i})
converges to a constant matrix.

Note that convergence in probability does not imply convergence of moments in general. The following theorem gives a sufficient condition.

Proposition 30.5
(dominant convergence theorem)

If
Z_{n}\rightarrow Z
in probability and
\|Z_{n}\|\leq\|Y\|
with
E\|Y\|<\infty
, then
E(Z_{n})\rightarrow E(Z)
.

Appendix 30.B
Convergence in distribution

Definition 30.3

Random vectors
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
converge to
Z
in distribution,
if for all every continuous point
z
of the function
t\rightarrow\textup{pr}(Z\leq t)
, we have

\textup{pr}(Z_{n}\leq z)\rightarrow\textup{pr}(Z\leq z),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

When the limit is a constant, we have an equivalence of convergences
in probability and distribution:

Proposition 30.6

If
c
is a non-random vector, then
Z_{n}\rightarrow c
in probability
is equivalent to
Z_{n}\rightarrow c
in distribution.

For IID sequences of random vectors, we have the Lindeberg–Lévy central limit theorem
(CLT):

Proposition 30.7
(Lindeberg–Lévy CLT)

If random vectors
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
are
IID
with mean
\mu

and covariance
\Sigma
, then
n^{1/2}(\bar{Z}_{n}-\mu)=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Z_{i}-\mu)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,\Sigma)

in distribution.

The more general Lindeberg–Feller CLT holds for independent sequences
of random vectors:

Proposition 30.8

For each
n
, let
Z_{n1},\ldots,Z_{n,k_{n}}
be independent random
vectors with finite variances such that

(LF1)

\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}E\left[\|Z_{ni}\|^{2}1\left\{\|Z_{ni}\|>c\right\}\right]\rightarrow 0

for every
c>0
;

(LF2)

\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\textup{cov}(Z_{ni})\rightarrow\Sigma.

Then
\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\left\{Z_{ni}-E(Z_{ni})\right\}\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,\Sigma)

in distribution.

Condition (LF
2
) often holds by proper standardization, and the key is to verify Condition (LF
1
).
Condition (LF
1
) is general but it looks cumbersome. In many cases, we impose a stronger moment condition that is easier to verify:

(LF1’)

\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}E\|Z_{ni}\|^{2+\delta}\rightarrow 0
for some
\delta>0
.

We can show that (LF
1
’) implies that (LF
1
):

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}E\left[\|Z_{ni}\|^{2}1\left\{\|Z_{ni}\|>c\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}E\left[\|Z_{ni}\|^{2+\delta}\|Z_{ni}\|^{-\delta}1\left\{\|Z_{ni}\|^{\delta}>c^{\delta}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}E\|Z_{ni}\|^{2+\delta}c^{-\delta}\rightarrow 0.

Condition (LF
1
’) is called the Lyapunov condition.

A beautiful application of the Lindeberg–Feller CLT is the proof of
Huber, 1973
’s theorem on OLS mentioned in Chapter
11
. I first review the theorem and then give a proof.

Theorem 30.1

Assume
Y=X\beta+\varepsilon
where the covariates are fixed and error terms
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
are IID non-Normal with mean zero and finite variance
\sigma^{2}
. Recall the OLS estimator
\hat{\beta}=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Y
. Any linear combination of
\hat{\beta}
is asymptotically Normal if and only if

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}h_{ii}\rightarrow 0,

where
h_{ii}
is the
i
th diagonal element of the hat matrix
H=X(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
.

In the main text,
h_{ii}
is called the
leverage score
of unit
i
. The maximum leverage score

\kappa=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}h_{ii}

plays an important role in analyzing the properties of OLS.

Theorem
30.1
assumes that the errors are not Normal because the asymptotic Normality under Normal errors is a trivial result (See Chapter
5
).
It is slightly different from the asymptotic analysis in Chapter
6
. Theorem
30.1
only concerns any linear combination of the OLS estimator alone, but the results in Chapter
6
allow for the joint inference of several linear combinations of the OLS estimator. An implicit assumption of Chapter
6
is that the dimension
p
of the covariate matrix is fixed, but Theorem
30.1
allows for a diverging
p
. The leverage score condition implicitly restricts the dimension and moments of the covariates. Another interesting feature of Theorem
30.1
is that the statement is coordinate-free, that is, it holds up to a non-singular transformation of the covariates (See also Problems
3.4
and
3.5
). The proof of sufficiency follows
Huber, 1973
closely, and the proof of necessity was suggested by Professor Peter Bickel.

Proof of Theorem
30.1
:

I first simplify the notation without essential loss of generality.
By the invariance of the OLS estimator and the hat matrix in Problems
3.4
and
3.5
, we can also assume
X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X=I_{p}
. So

\hat{\beta}-\beta=(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\varepsilon=X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\varepsilon

and the hat matrix

H=X(X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X)^{-1}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}=XX^{\tiny\textsc{t}}

has diagonal elements
h_{ii}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{i}=\|x_{i}\|^{2}
and non-diagonal elements
h_{ij}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{j}
. We can also assume
\sigma^{2}=1
.

Consider a fixed vector
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and assume
\|a\|^{2}=1
.
We have

a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}-a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta=a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\varepsilon\equiv s^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\varepsilon,

where

s=Xa\Longleftrightarrow s_{i}=x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}a\quad(i=1,\ldots,n)

satisfies

\|s\|^{2}=a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}Xa=\|a\|^{2}=1

and

s_{i}^{2}=(x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}a)^{2}\leq\|x_{i}\|^{2}\|a\|^{2}=\|x_{i}\|^{2}=h_{ii}

by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

I first prove the sufficiency.
The key term
a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}-a^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta
is a linear combination of the IID errors, and it has mean
0
and variance

\textup{var}(s^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\varepsilon)=\|s\|^{2}=1.

We only need to verify Condition (LF
1
) to establish the CLT. It holds because for any fixed
c>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\left[s_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}1\left\{|s_{i}\varepsilon_{i}|>c\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}^{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}E\left[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}1\left\{|s_{i}\varepsilon_{i}|>c\right\}\right]

(196)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq n}E\left[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}1\left\{|s_{i}\varepsilon_{i}|>c\right\}\right]

(197)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E\left[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}1\left\{\kappa^{1/2}|\varepsilon_{i}|>c\right\}\right]

(198)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle 0,

(199)

where (
196
) follows from the property of
\max
, (
197
) follows from the fact
\|s\|^{2}=1
, (
198
) follows from the fact that
|s_{i}|\leq|h_{ii}|\leq\kappa^{1/2}
, and (
199
) follows from
\kappa\rightarrow 0
and the dominant convergence theorem in Proposition
30.5
.

I then prove the necessity.
Pick one
i^{*}
from
\arg\max_{1\leq i\leq n}h_{ii}
.
Consider a special linear combination of the OLS estimator:
\hat{y}_{i^{*}}=x_{i^{*}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}
, which is the fitted value of the
i^{*}
th observation and has the form

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{i^{*}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{i^{*}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}X^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\varepsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{i^{*}}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}x_{j}\varepsilon_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{i^{*}i^{*}}\varepsilon_{i^{*}}+\sum_{j\neq i^{*}}h_{i^{*}j}\varepsilon_{j}.

If
\hat{y}_{i^{*}}
is asymptotically Normal, then both
h_{i^{*}i^{*}}\varepsilon_{i^{*}}
and
\sum_{j\neq i^{*}}h_{i^{*}j}\varepsilon_{j}
must have Normal limiting distributions by Theorem
29.6
. Therefore,
h_{i^{*}i^{*}}
must converge to zero because
\varepsilon_{i^{*}}
has a non-Normal distribution. So
\max_{1\leq i\leq n}h_{ii}
must converge to zero.

\square

Appendix 30.C
Tools for proving convergence in probability and distribution

The first tool is the continuous mapping theorem:

Proposition 30.9

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{L}
be continuous except
on a set
O
with
\textup{pr}(Z\in O)=0
. Then
Z_{n}\rightarrow Z
implies

f(Z_{n})\rightarrow f(Z)
in probability (and in distribution).

The second tool is Slutsky’s Theorem:

Proposition 30.10

Let
Z_{n}
and
W_{n}
be random vectors. If
Z_{n}\rightarrow Z

in distribution, and
W_{n}\rightarrow c
in probability (or in distribution)
for a constant
c
, then

1.

Z_{n}+W_{n}\rightarrow Z+c
in distribution;

2.

W_{n}Z_{n}\rightarrow cZ
in distribution;

3.

W_{n}^{-1}Z_{n}\rightarrow c^{-1}Z
in distribution if
c\neq 0
.

The third tool is the delta method. I will present a special case below for asymptotically Normal random vectors. Heuristically, it states that if
T_{n}
is asymptotically Normal, then any function of
T_{n}
is also asymptotically Normal. This is true because any function is a locally linear function by the first-order Taylor expansion.

Proposition 30.11

Let
f(z)
be a function from
\mathbb{R}^{p}
to
\mathbb{R}^{q}
, and
\partial f(z)/\partial z\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
be the partial derivative matrix.
If
\sqrt{n}(Z_{n}-\theta)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
in distribution, then

\sqrt{n}\{f(Z_{n})-f(\theta)\}\rightarrow\textsc{N}\left(\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\mu,\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\Sigma\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z}\right)

in distribution.

Proof of Proposition
30.11
:

I will give an informal proof. Using Taylor expansion, we have

\sqrt{n}\{f(Z_{n})-f(\theta)\}\cong\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\sqrt{n}(Z_{n}-\theta),

which is a linear transformation of
\sqrt{n}(Z_{n}-\theta)
. Because
\sqrt{n}(Z_{n}-\theta)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
in distribution, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\{f(Z_{n})-f(\theta)\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\textsc{N}(\mu,\Sigma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textsc{N}\left(\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\mu,\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\Sigma\frac{\partial f(\theta)}{\partial z}\right)

in distribution.

\square

Proposition
30.11
above is more useful when
\partial f(\theta)/\partial z\neq 0
. Otherwise, we need to invoke higher-order Taylor expansion to obtain a more accurate asymptotic approximation.

Chapter 31
M-Estimation and MLE

A wide range of statistics estimation problems can be formulated as an estimating equation:

\bar{m}(W,b)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(w_{i},b)=0

where
m(\cdot,\cdot)
is a vector function with the same dimension as
b
, and
W=\left\{w_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
are the observed data.
Let
\hat{\beta}
denote the solution which is an estimator of
\beta
. Under mild regularity conditions,
\hat{\beta}
is consistent and asymptotically Normal
35
35

35

There are counterexamples in which
\hat{\beta}
is inconsistent; see
Freedman and Diaconis, 1982
. The examples in this book are all regular.
.
This is the classical theory of M-estimation. I will review it below. See
Stefanski and Boos, 2002
for a reader-friendly introduction that contains many interesting and important examples. The proofs below are not rigorous.
See
Newey and McFadden, 1994
for the rigorous ones.

Appendix 31.A
M-estimation

I start with the simple case with IID data.

Theorem 31.1

Assume that
W=\left\{w_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
are IID with the same distribution as
w
.
The true parameter
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the unique solution of

E\left\{m(w,b)\right\}=0,

and the estimator
\hat{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the solution of

\bar{m}(W,b)=0.

Under some regularity conditions,

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\beta}-\beta\right)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,B^{-1}MB^{-\tiny\textsc{t}})

in distribution, where

B=-\frac{\partial E\left\{m(w,\beta)\right\}}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}},\quad M=E\{m(w,\beta)m(w,\beta)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\}.

Proof of Theorem
31.1
:

I give a “physics” proof. When I use approximations, I mean the
error terms are of higher orders under some regularity conditions. The
consistency follows from swapping the order of “solving equation”
and “taking the limit based on the law of large numbers”:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\text{solve }\bar{m}(W,b)=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\text{solve }\left\{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bar{m}(W,b)=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\text{solve }\left[E\left\{m(w,b)\right\}=0\right]

\displaystyle=\beta.

The asymptotic Normality follows from three steps. First, from the Taylor expansion

0=\bar{m}(W,\hat{\beta})\cong\bar{m}(W,\beta)+\frac{\partial\bar{m}(W,\beta)}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)

we obtain

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\beta}-\beta\right)\cong\left\{-\frac{\partial\bar{m}(W,\beta)}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{\text{$\sqrt{n}$}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(w_{i},\beta)\right\}.

Second, the law of large numbers ensures that

-\frac{\partial\bar{m}(W,\beta)}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\rightarrow-\frac{\partial E\left\{m(w,\beta)\right\}}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}=B

in probability, and the CLT ensures that

n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(w_{i},\beta)\rightarrow\ \textsc{N}(0,M)

in distribution.
Finally, Slutsky’s theorem implies the result.

\square

The above result also holds with independent but non-IID data.

Theorem 31.2

Assume that
\left\{w_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
are independent observations.
The true parameter
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the unique solution to

E\left\{\bar{m}(W,b)\right\}=0,

and the estimator
\hat{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the solution to

\bar{m}(W,b)=0,

Under some regularity conditions,

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\beta}-\beta\right)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,B^{-1}MB^{-\tiny\textsc{t}})

in distribution, where

B=-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial E\left\{m(w_{i},\beta)\right\}}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}},\quad M=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textup{cov}\{m(w_{i},\beta)\}.

For both cases above, we can further construct the following sandwich covariance estimator:

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial E\{m(w_{i},\hat{\beta})\}}{\partial b^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(w_{i},\hat{\beta})m(w_{i},\hat{\beta})^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial E\{m(w_{i},\hat{\beta})\}}{\partial b}\right)^{-1}

For non-IID data, the above covariance estimator can be conservative unless
E\{m(w_{i},\beta)\}=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Example 31.1

Assume that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}x
with mean
\mu
. The sample mean
\bar{x}
solves the estimating equation

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\mu)=0.

Apply Theorem
31.1
to obtain
B=-1
and
M=\sigma^{2}
, which imply

\sqrt{n}(\bar{x}-\mu)\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,\sigma^{2})

in distribution, the standard CLT for the sample mean. Moreover, the sandwich covariance estimator is

\hat{V}=n^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2},

which equals the sample variance of
x
, multiplied by
(n-1)/n^{2}\approx 1/n
. This is a standard result.

If we only assume that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are independent with the same mean
\mu
but possibly different variances
\sigma_{i}^{2}\ (i=1,\ldots,n)
, the sample mean
\bar{x}
is still a reasonable estimator for
\mu
which solves the same estimating equation above. Moreover, the sandwich covariance estimator
\hat{V}
is still a consistent estimator for the true variance of
\bar{x}
. This is less standard.

If we assume that
x_{i}\sim[\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}]
are independent, we can still use
\bar{x}
to estimate
\mu=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}
. The estimating equation remains the same as above. The sandwich covariance estimator
\hat{V}
becomes conservative since
E(x_{i}-\mu)\neq 0
in general.

Problem
31.1
gives more details.

Example 31.2

Assume that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}x
with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
. The sample mean and variance
(\bar{x},\hat{\sigma}^{2})
jointly solves the estimating equation with

m(x,\mu,\sigma^{2})=\begin{pmatrix}x-\mu\\
(x-\mu)^{2}-\sigma^{2}\end{pmatrix}

ignoring the difference between
n
and
n-1
in the definition of the sample variance. Apply Theorem
31.1
to obtain

B=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad M=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma^{2}&\mu_{3}\\
\mu_{3}&\mu_{4}-\sigma^{4}\end{pmatrix}

where
\mu_{k}=E\{(x-\mu)^{k}\}
, which imply

\sqrt{n}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{x}-\mu\\
\hat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}\end{pmatrix}\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,M)

in distribution.

Example 31.3

Assume that
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are IID draws from
(x,y)
with mean
(\mu_{x},\mu_{y})
. Use
\bar{x}/\bar{y}
to estimate
\gamma=\mu_{x}/\mu_{y}
. It satisfies the estimating equation with

m(x,y,\gamma)=x-\gamma y.

Apply Theorem
31.1
to obtain
B=-\mu_{y}
and
M=\textup{var}(x-\gamma y)
, which imply

\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{\bar{x}}{\bar{y}}-\frac{\mu_{x}}{\mu_{y}}\right)\rightarrow\textsc{N}\left(0,\frac{\textup{var}(x-\gamma y)}{\mu_{y}^{2}}\right)

if
\mu_{y}\neq 0
.

Appendix 31.B
Maximum likelihood estimator

As an important application of Theorem
31.1
, we can derive the asymptotic properties of the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE)
\hat{\theta}
under IID sampling from a parametric model

y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}f(y\mid\theta).

The MLE satisfies the following estimating equation:

\displaystyle E\left\{\frac{\partial\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta}\right\}=0,

(200)

which is Bartlett’s first identity.
Under regularity conditions,
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta)
converges in distribution to Normal with mean zero and covariance
B^{-1}MB^{-1}
, where

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}E\left\{\frac{\partial\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left\{-\frac{\partial^{2}\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta\partial\theta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right\}

is called the Fisher information matrix, denoted by
I(\theta)
,
and

M=E\left\{\frac{\partial\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta}\frac{\partial\log f(y\mid\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}\right\}

is sometimes also called the Fisher information matrix, denoted by
J(\theta)
.

If the model is correct,
Bartlett’s second identity ensures that

\displaystyle I(\theta)=J(\theta),

(201)

and therefore
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta)
converges in distribution to Normal with mean zero and covariance
I(\theta)^{-1}=J(\theta)^{-1}
. So a covariance matrix estimator for the MLE is
I_{n}(\hat{\theta})^{-1}
or
J_{n}(\hat{\theta})^{-1}
, where

I_{n}(\hat{\theta})=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}\log f(y_{i}\mid\hat{\theta})}{\partial\theta\partial\theta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}

and

J_{n}(\hat{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\log f(y_{i}\mid\hat{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\frac{\partial\log f(y_{i}\mid\hat{\theta})}{\partial\theta^{\tiny\textsc{t}}}.

Fisher, 1925b
pioneered the asymptotic theory of the MLE under correctly specified models.

If the model is incorrect,
I(\theta)
can be different from
J(\theta)
but the sandwich covariance
B^{-1}MB^{-1}
still holds. So a covariance matrix estimator for the MLE under misspecification is

I_{n}(\hat{\theta})^{-1}J_{n}(\hat{\theta})I_{n}(\hat{\theta})^{-1}.

Huber, 1967
studied the asymptotic theory of the MLE under correctly specified models. He focused on the case with IID observations and pioneered
the sandwich covariance formula.
Perhaps a more important question is what is the parameter if the model is misspecified. The population analog of the MLE is the minimizer of

-E\{\log f(y\mid\theta)\},

where the expectation is over true but unknown distribution
y\sim g(y)
. We can rewrite the population objective function as

-\int g(y)\log f(y\mid\theta)\textup{d}y=\int g(y)\log\frac{g(y)}{f(y\mid\theta)}\textup{d}y-\int g(y)\log g(y)\textup{d}y.

The first term is called the Kullback–Leibler divergence or relative entropy of
g(y)
and
f(y\mid\theta)
, whereas the second term is called the entropy of
g(y)
. The first term depends on
\theta
whereas the second term does not. Therefore, the targeted parameter of the MLE is the minimizer of the Kullback–Leibler divergence. By Gibbs’ inequality, the Kullback–Leibler divergence is non-negative in general and is
0
if
g(y)=f(y\mid\theta)
. Therefore, if the model is correct, then the true
\theta
indeed minimizes the Kullback–Leibler divergence with minimum value
0

Example 31.4

Assume that
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textsc{N}(\mu,1)
. The log-likelihood contributed by unit
i
is

\log f(y_{i}\mid\mu)=-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac{1}{2}(y_{i}-\mu)^{2},

so

\frac{\partial\log f(y_{i}\mid\mu)}{\partial\mu}=y_{i}-\mu,\quad\frac{\partial^{2}\log f(y_{i}\mid\mu)}{\partial\mu^{2}}=-1.

The MLE is
\hat{\mu}=\bar{y}
.
If the model is correctly specified, we can use

I_{n}(\hat{\mu})^{-1}=n^{-1}\text{ or }J_{n}(\hat{\mu})^{-1}=1/\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{\mu})^{2}

to estimate the variance of
\hat{\mu}
. If the model is misspecified, we can use

I_{n}(\hat{\mu})^{-1}J_{n}(\hat{\mu})I_{n}(\hat{\mu})^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{\mu})^{2}/n^{2}

to estimate the variance of
\hat{\mu}
.

The sandwich variance estimator seems the best overall. The Normal model can be totally wrong but it is still meaningful to estimate the mean parameter
\mu=E(y)
. The MLE is just the sample moment estimator which has variance var
(y)/n
. Since the sample variance
s^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{\mu})^{2}/(n-1)
is unbiased for var
(y)
, a natural unbiased estimator for var
(\hat{\mu})
is
s^{2}/n
, which is close to the sandwich variance estimator.

The above discussion extends to the case with independent but non-IID data. The covariance estimators still apply by replacing each
f
by
f_{i}
within the summation. Note that the sandwich covariance estimator is conservative in general.
White, 1982
pioneered the asymptotic analysis of the MLE with misspecified models in econometrics but made a mistake for the
M
term.
Chow, 1984
corrected his error, and
Abadie et al., 2014
developed a more general theory.

A leading application is the MLE under a misspecified Normal linear model. The EHW robust covariance arises naturally in this case.

Example 31.5

The Normal linear model has individual log-likelihood:

l_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^{2})-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)^{2},\quad(i=1,\ldots,n)

with the simplification
l_{i}=\log f(y_{i}\mid x_{i},\beta,\sigma^{2}).

So the first-order derivatives are

\frac{\partial l_{i}}{\partial\beta}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}x_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta),\quad\quad\frac{\partial l_{i}}{\partial\sigma^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2(\sigma^{2})^{2}}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)^{2};

the second derivative is

\frac{\partial^{2}l_{i}}{\partial\beta^{2}}=-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}},\quad\frac{\partial^{2}l_{i}}{\partial(\sigma^{2})^{2}}=\frac{1}{2(\sigma^{2})^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\sigma^{2})^{3}}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta)^{2}

and

\frac{\partial^{2}l_{i}}{\partial\beta\partial\sigma^{2}}=-\frac{1}{(\sigma^{2})^{2}}x_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\beta).

The MLE of
\beta
is the OLS estimator
\hat{\beta}
and the MLE of
\sigma^{2}
is
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}/n
, where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\hat{\beta}
is the residual.

We have

I_{n}(\hat{\beta},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})=\textup{diag}\left(\frac{1}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}},\frac{n}{2(\tilde{\sigma}^{2})^{2}}\right),

and

J_{n}(\hat{\beta},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{(\tilde{\sigma}^{2})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}&*\\
*&*\end{pmatrix},

where the
*
terms do not matter for the later calculations. If the Normal linear model is correctly specified, we can use the
(1,1)
th block of
I_{n}(\hat{\beta},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})^{-1}
as the covariance estimator for
\hat{\beta}
, which equals

\tilde{\sigma}^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}.

If the Normal linear model is misspecified, we can use the
(1,1)
th block of
I_{n}(\hat{\beta},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})^{-1}J_{n}(\hat{\beta},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})I_{n}(\hat{\beta},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})^{-1}
as the covariance estimator for
\hat{\beta}
, which equals

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\tiny\textsc{t}}\right)^{-1},

the EHW robust covariance estimator introduced in Chapter
6
.

Appendix 31.C
Homework problems

31.1
Estimating the mean

Example
31.1
concerns the asymptotic properties. This problem supplements it with more finite-sample results.

Slightly modify the sandwich covariance estimator to

\tilde{V}=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}.

Show that
E(\tilde{V})=\textup{var}(\bar{x})
when
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are independent with the same mean
\mu
, and
E(\tilde{V})\geq\textup{var}(\bar{x})
when
x_{i}\sim[\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}]
are independent.

31.2
Sample Pearson correlation coefficient

Assume that
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are IID draws from
(x,y)
with mean
(\mu_{x},\mu_{y})
and fourth moments. Derive the asymptotic distribution of the sample Pearson correlation coefficient. Express the asymptotic variance in terms of

\mu_{kl}=E\{(x-\mu_{x})^{k}(y-\mu_{y})^{l}\},

for example,

\textup{var}(x)=\mu_{20},\quad\textup{var}(y)=\mu_{02},\quad\textup{cov}(x,y)=\mu_{11},\quad\rho=\mu_{11}/\sqrt{\mu_{20}\mu_{02}}.

Hint: Use the fact that
\beta=(\mu_{x},\mu_{y},\mu_{20},\mu_{02},\rho)^{\tiny\textsc{t}}
satisfies the estimating equation with

m(x,y,\beta)=\begin{pmatrix}x-\mu_{x}\\
y-\mu_{y}\\
(x-\mu_{x})^{2}-\mu_{20}\\
(y-\mu_{y})^{2}-\mu_{02}\\
(x-\mu_{x})(y-\mu_{y})-\rho\sqrt{\mu_{20}\mu_{02}}\end{pmatrix}

and the sample moments and Pearson correlation coefficient are the corresponding estimators.
You may also find the formula (
186
) useful.

31.3
A misspecified Exponential model

Assume that
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{iid}}}{{\sim}}
Exponential distribution with mean
\mu
. Find the MLE of
\mu
and its asymptotic variance estimators under correctly specified and incorrectly specified models.
